CB 2 O: Consensus-Based Bi-Level Optimization
Nicolás García Trillos, Sixu Li, Konstantin Riedl, Yuhua Zhu

Abstract
Bi-level optimization problems, where one wishes to find the global minimizer of an upper-level objective function over the globally optimal solution set of a lower-level objective, arise in a variety of scenarios throughout science and engineering, machine learning, and artificial intelligence. In this paper, we propose and investigate, analytically and experimentally, consensus-based bi-level optimization (CB 2 O), a multi-particle metaheuristic derivative-free optimization method designed to solve bi-level optimization problems when both objectives may be nonconvex. Our method leverages within the computation of the consensus point a carefully designed particle selection principle implemented through a suitable choice of a quantile on the level of the lower-level objective, together with a Laplace principle-type approximation w.r.t. the upper-level objective function, to ensure that the bi-level optimization problem is solved in an intrinsic manner. We give an existence proof of solutions to a corresponding mean-field dynamics, for which we first establish the stability of our consensus point w.r.t. a combination of Wasserstein and L 2 L^{2} perturbations, and consecutively resort to PDE considerations extending the classical Picard iteration to construct a solution. For such solution, we provide a global convergence analysis in mean-field law showing that the solution of the associated nonlinear nonlocal Fokker-Planck equation converges exponentially fast to the unique solution of the bi-level optimization problem provided suitable choices of the hyperparameters. The practicability and efficiency of our CB 2 O algorithm is demonstrated through extensive numerical experiments in the settings of constrained global optimization, sparse representation learning, and robust (clustered) federated learning.



Keywords:
global optimization, derivative-free optimization, bi-level optimization, nonsmoothness, nonconvexity, metaheuristics, consensus-based optimization, mean-field limit, Fokker-Planck equations

AMS subject classifications:
65K10, 90C26, 90C56, 35Q90, 35Q84

1
Introduction

A variety of problems arising in science and engineering, machine learning and artificial intelligence as well as economics and operations research are naturally posed as nonconvex bi-level optimization problems of the form

\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}:=\operatorname*{\arg\min}_{\theta^{*}\in\Theta}G(\theta^{*})\quad\text{s.t.{}}\quad\theta^{*}\in\Theta:=\operatorname*{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}L(\theta),

(1.1)

where the aim is to minimize an upper-level objective function
G
over the solution set
\Theta
of a lower-level problem represented by the objective
L

[
43
,
13
,
59
,
69
]
.
Due to the widespread relevance of this type of problem, it has attracted great attention across several disciplines. In machine learning and artificial intelligence, for example,
this type of problem appears in hyperparameter optimization
[
55
]
,
continual learning
[
39
]
,
lexicographic optimization
[
59
]
,
machine learning fairness
[
99
]
,
adversarial robustness
[
56
]
,
neural architecture search
[
46
]
,
as well as
ill-posed or over-parameterized machine learning
[
69
]
, just to give a few examples.
In science and engineering,
this problem formulation appears in
compressed sensing
[
44
]
,
optimal control,
resource allocation
[
97
]
, among many other settings. For a more exhaustive survey of examples and applications of such bi-level optimization problems, we refer the reader to
[
43
,
76
]
as well as references therein.
Problems of the type (
1.1
) are commonly referred to as simple bi-level optimization problems
[
43
,
13
,
88
,
45
,
90
,
69
,
89
,
96
,
30
]
, and they comprise a relevant subclass of general bi-level optimization problems,
where in general the lower-level objective may be parametrized by some arguments of the upper-level objective function.

In several of the aforementioned applications, solving such problem may be viewed as enforcing a model selection principle in a setting where several models perform equally well w.r.t. the objective function
L
,
while only one amongst them grants desirable properties beyond the lower-level objective. Such properties may be related to robustness, fairness, sparsity, efficacy, and are encoded through the upper-level objective function
G
. Mathematically speaking, the set of feasible solutions to the upper-level problem
G
is given by the set of global minimizers of the lower-level objective function
L
.
Throughout this paper, both the upper- and lower-level objectives
G
and
L
, respectively, are assumed to be real-valued functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
In accordance with this description, the lower-level objective function
L
is typically a nonconvex function that admits multiple global minimizers
\theta^{*}
. We denote
L
’s associated set of minimizers by
\Theta:=\operatorname*{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}L(\theta)
and the value of
L
at those points by
\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L
. We further assume that, as imposed by the problem formulation in (
1.1
), only one of the elements in
\Theta
is optimal w.r.t. the upper-level objective function
G
.
That is, the function
G
is assumed to have a unique minimizer within the set
\Theta
, which we henceforth denote by
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
. For an illustration of the problem setting, we refer to Figure
2
below.

Figure 1
:
An illustration of the CB
2
O algorithm (
1.2
) and its working principles for solving bi-level optimization problems of the form (
1.1
).
We depict a typical setting of (
1.1
) in two dimensions with the lower-level objective function
L
being a Himmelblau function (plotted as contours in the
xy
-plane) and with the upper-level objective function
G
being a parabola (plotted as a surface)
2
2

2

We ask the reader to appreciate the placement of the objective functions
L
and
G
in Figure
2
in analogy with their intuitive interpretation, i.e.,
L
as the lower- and
G
as the upper-level objective.
. The set of global minimizers of
L
, i.e., the set
\Theta
, consists of the three green dots and the one green star that are plotted on the
xy
plane. The green star identifies
the
global minimizer
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
of
L
which is optimal w.r.t. the upper-level objective function
G
among the points in
\Theta
, and thereby is the solution to the bi-level optimization problem (
1.1
).
The setting in this example satisfies the assumptions made later in the paper for our theoretical analysis.

At every point in time, CB
2
O employs
N=50
particles (depicted as yellow points, including both circles and filled dots on the
xy
-plane) and computes the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho^{N}_{t})
, which all particles are attracted to as they explore the space through noise (not depicted here). In order to compute the aforementioned consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho^{N}_{t})
, each particle first evaluates the lower-level objective function
L
. For a chosen quantile parameter
\beta=0.25
, the
\beta N
w.r.t.
L
best positioned particles (depicted as the yellow filled dots) are selected. They belong to the set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho^{N}_{t}]
defined in (
1.7
), which serves as an approximation to the set
\Theta
of global minimizers of
L
. Based on those points, the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho^{N}_{t})
is computed, while the remaining particles (depicted as the yellow circles) are discarded from the computation of the consensus point. Figuratively speaking, those particles that belong to the quantile set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho^{N}_{t}]
are lifted to the upper-level objective function
G
, which they evaluate in a second step. For a chosen weight parameter
\alpha=10
, the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho^{N}_{t})
is then computed as in (
1.6
). It approximates the global minimizer
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
of the bi-level optimization problem (
1.1
).

Bi-level optimization problems of the form (
1.1
) come with two fundamental challenges.
First, there is typically no simple characterization or explicit formula for the set
\Theta
of global minimizers of the lower-level objective function
L
.
Because of this, it is impractical to directly employ projection-based or projection-free optimization methods, since projecting onto or solving a minimization problem over such an implicitly defined feasible set is not straightforward.
Second, both the lower- and upper-level objective functions
L
and
G
may potentially be nonconvex, as is typical in classical engineering or modern machine learning problems.
As a result, many existing methods developed for convex bi-level optimization problems might become less effective in a more challenging, non-convex setting (see also the discussions on related works in Section
1.2
). The potential lack of convexity in one of the objectives also renders approaches such as unconstrained optimization of a weighted sum of the two objectives difficult to implement since it is in general challenging to tune weights to prevent the scheme from tilting towards arbitrary global minimizers of either
G
or
L
. Related to this, we note that problem formulation (
1.1
) is invariant to outer composition of the lower objective with an increasing function. Under such transformations of the lower-objective, the geometry of the set
\Theta
is unchanged, suggesting that a desirable algorithm should be stable to such perturbations of the problem.

To address the aforementioned difficulties,
we design an interacting particle-based method,
C
onsensus-
B
ased
B
i-level
O
ptimization (CB
2
O),
which is tailored to solving the optimization problem (
1.1
), i.e., finding the target good global minimizer
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
of
L
w.r.t.
G
.
Our approach is inspired by consensus-based optimization (CBO) for standard optimization problems as introduced in
[
82
]
, but enhanced by a suitable selection principle.
The method’s working principles are described in what follows and illustrated in Figure
2
.
For this purpose, let us start by establishing some notation.
CB
2
O methods employ a finite number of agents
\theta^{1},\dots,\theta^{N}
,
which are formally stochastic processes, i.e.,
\theta^{i}=(\theta^{i}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
for
i=1,\dots,N
,
to explore the domain and to form a global consensus about the location of
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
as time passes.
For user-specified parameters
\alpha,\beta,\lambda,\sigma>0
,
the time-evolution of the
i
-th agent is defined by the stochastic differential equation (SDE)

\begin{split}d\theta_{t}^{i}&=-\lambda\left(\theta_{t}^{i}-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})\right)dt+\sigma D\left(\theta_{t}^{i}-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})\right)dB_{t}^{i},\\
\theta_{0}^{i}&\sim\rho_{0},\end{split}

(1.2)

where
((B_{t}^{i})_{t\geq 0})_{i=1,\dots,N}
are independent standard Brownian motions in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The philosophy behind the design of the dynamics is in the spirit of many metaheuristics
[
16
,
71
,
1
]
,
which orchestrate an interaction between local improvement procedures and global strategies by combining deterministic and random decisions to create a process capable of escaping from local optima and performing a robust search in the solution space (see
[
51
, Section 1]
for more background on this quite popular class of optimization methods).
The dynamics of the particles
\theta^{1},\dots,\theta^{N}
in (
1.2
) are governed by two competing terms at each point in time.
A drift term drags each agent towards an instantaneous consensus point,
denoted by
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}
,
which is computed as a weighted average of the particles’ positions and acts as a momentaneous proxy for
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
. We will elaborate in a moment on how this consensus point is computed, given that it encapsulates the foundational innovations of the method and is therefore central to it.
The second term is stochastic and with it we diffuse agents according to a scaled Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, featuring the exploration of the energy landscape of the objective functions
L
and
G
.
Two commonly studied diffusion types are isotropic
[
82
,
31
,
51
]
and anisotropic
[
34
,
50
]
diffusion, which correspond to

D\!\left(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}\right)=\begin{cases}\left\|{\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}}\right\|_{2}\mathrm{Id},&\text{for isotropic diffusion,}\\
\diag\left(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}\right)\!,&\text{for anisotropic diffusion},\end{cases}

(1.3)

where
\mathrm{Id}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is the identity matrix and
\diag:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
the operator mapping a vector onto a diagonal matrix with the vector as its diagonal.
Ideally, as result of the described drift-diffusion mechanism, the agents should eventually achieve a near optimal global consensus, in the sense that the associated empirical measure
\rho^{N}_{t}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\theta^{i}_{t}}
should converge in time to a Dirac delta at some point close to
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
.

In order to compute the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho^{N}_{t})
at time
t
,
we first and foremost evaluate the lower-level objective function
L
for each of the
N
particles, i.e.,
\{L(\theta^{i}_{t})\}_{i=1}^{N}
,
and arrange them in a non-decreasing order

L(\theta_{t}^{\#1})\leq L(\theta_{t}^{\#2})\leq\cdots\leq L(\theta_{t}^{\#N}),

(1.4)

where
\theta_{t}^{\#j}
denotes the position of the particle with the
j
-th smallest lower-level objective function value at time
t
within the set
\{L(\theta_{t}^{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}
.
For
\beta\in(0,1]
, let us denote by
\lceil\beta N\rceil
the smallest integer greater than or equal to
\beta N
.
We then define the
\beta
-quantile of the set
\{L(\theta_{t}^{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}
as

q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho^{N}_{t}]:=L\big(\theta_{t}^{\#\lceil\beta N\rceil}\big).

(1.5)

This means that
q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho^{N}_{t}]
is the value for which
\lceil\beta N\rceil
particles of
\{\theta^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
attain a lower-level function value less than or equal to
q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho^{N}_{t}]
.
Intuitively speaking, the
\beta
-quantile
q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]
can be regarded as a proxy for
\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L
,
the minimum value of objective function
L
, given the currently available information amongst the particles.
This approximation becomes more and more accurate (still constrained by the currently available information) as
\beta
becomes smaller and smaller.
The consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})
is now defined as a weighted average of precisely those particle positions, or put into formulas as

\begin{split}m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N}):=\sum_{\theta_{t}^{i}:L(\theta_{t}^{i})\leq q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]}\theta_{t}^{i}\frac{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta_{t}^{i})}{\sum_{\theta_{t}^{j}:L(\theta_{t}^{j})\leq q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]}\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta_{t}^{j})},\qquad\text{with}\quad\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta):=\exp\left(-\alpha G(\theta)\right),\end{split}

(1.6)

where the
\beta
-quantile
q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]
of the full particle set
\{\theta_{t}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
w.r.t.
L
is as defined in (
1.5
).
In order to facilitate the upcoming discussion and analysis,
let us define the sub-level set of the lower-level objective function
L
w.r.t. the
\beta
-quantile
q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]
defined in (
1.5
) as

Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]:=\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:L(\theta)\leq q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]\right\}.

(1.7)

In words,
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]
encompasses those regions of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
where the lower-level objective function
L
attains values less than or equal to the
\beta
-quantile
q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]
.
Recalling that
q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]
can be thought of as a proxy for
\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L
, the set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]
can therefore be regarded as an approximation of the neighborhood of the set
\Theta
of global minimizers of
L
.
As the number
N
of particles becomes large,
the particles are expected to effectively cover this neighborhood in full,
with the approximation becoming more and more refined as
\beta
becomes smaller and smaller; see Remark
2.9
. With this notation, the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})
in (
1.6
) can be rewritten as

m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})=\sum_{\theta_{t}^{i}:\theta_{t}^{i}\in Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]}\theta_{t}^{i}\frac{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta_{t}^{i})}{\sum_{\theta_{t}^{j}:\theta_{t}^{j}\in Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]}\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta_{t}^{j})}.

(1.8)

Note that the set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]
, and therefore also the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})
, is unchanged if
L
is composed with an arbitrary increasing function and it therefore shares the same type of invariance with the set
\Theta
.

Unlike in standard CBO
[
82
]
for classical optimization,
where the consensus point is defined as a weighted average of
all
particle positions,
our consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})
, as defined in (
1.8
), is obtaiend as an average over a subset of particles from
\{\theta_{t}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
,
namely those belonging to the sub-level set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]
.
While by construction the consensus point is supported on the set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]
and computed from positions with good lower-level objective values,
this does not yet mean that the consensus point itself will lie within the set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]
or have a good lower-level objective value
L
for some
\alpha
.
However, since the Laplace principle
[
42
,
79
]

ensures that for any absolutely continuous probability distribution
\varrho
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\alpha\rightarrow\infty}\left(-\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\int\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(v)\,d\varrho(v)\right)\right)=\inf\limits_{v\in\operatorname{supp}(\varrho)}G(v),

(1.9)

the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})
can be regarded as a proxy for the global minimizer of the upper-level objective function
G
restricted to the set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]
.
This approximation improves as
\alpha
becomes larger; see Remark
2.10
.
The combination and interplay between these two mechanisms, each of them related to the hyperparameters
\beta
and
\alpha
, respectively, ensure that the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})
is a proxy for the good global minimizer
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
, i.e., the global minimizer of
G
within the set
\Theta
of global minimizers of
L
.

Apart from the crucial difference in the definition of the consensus point introduced above and the one appearing in the standard CBO setting (difference motivated by the specific goal of solving problem (
1.1
)),
the CB
2
O system (
1.2
) closely resembles the dynamics of the CBO method for classical optimization introduced in
[
82
]
. For notational convenience, when defining the CB
2
O system in a more generic way later on, and to better align with the conventional notation in the CBO literature
[
82
,
31
,
51
]
,
we introduce the measure

I^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]:=\mathds{1}_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]}\rho_{t}^{N},

(1.10)

which is the (unnormalized) empirical measure of those particles of
\{\theta_{t}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
that belong to the quantile set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]
, i.e., the particles that are used to compute the consensus point.
With the definition of
I^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]
,
we can write the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})
in integral form as

m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N}):=\int\theta\frac{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta)}{\|\omega_{\alpha}^{G}\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}])}}dI^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}](\theta)

(1.11)

with normalization factor
\|{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}}\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}])}:=\int\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta)\,dI^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}](\theta)
.
Let us remark in particular that
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})=m^{G}_{\alpha}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}])
, where
m^{G}_{\alpha}
is just the definition of the consensus point in classical CBO for optimization, see, e.g.,
[
51
, Equation (1.5)]
.

To wrap up, by following the dynamics (
1.2
),
the particles are attracted towards a position, namely the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})
, which has a small value of the upper-level objective function
G
while simultaneously lying
within
the regions where the lower-level objective function
L
is small. This intuition aligns with our goal of finding
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
, which is
the
global minimizer of
L
that has the smallest objective function value w.r.t.
G
.
The mechanisms behind computing the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})
are illustrated in Figure
2
.

A theoretical analysis of the CB
2
O dynamics can either be done directly at the level of the microscopic interacting particle system (
1.2
),
or, following the recent and popular vein of works
[
31
,
34
,
51
,
50
,
60
,
84
,
83
]
, by analyzing the macroscopic behavior of agents density
\rho=(\rho_{t})_{t\geq 0}
through a mean-field limit associated with the particle-based dynamics (
1.2
), which we will specify more rigorously shortly. Typically, this second perspective allows to leverage more agile deterministic calculus tools and leads generally to more expressive theoretical results that characterize the average agent behavior through the evolution of the measure
\rho
.
This analytical perspective is justified by the mean-field limit or mean-field approximation,
which qualitatively or quantitatively captures the convergence of the microscopic system (
1.2
) to the mean-field limit (
1.12
) or associated Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
) as the number
N
of agents grows; see Remark
1.1
for more details.
For the mean-field limit of the interacting CB
2
O method (
1.2
), i.e., as
N\rightarrow\infty
,
it is natural to postulate the stochastic process
\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta=(\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
satisfying the self-consistent nonlinear nonlocal SDE

\begin{split}d\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta_{t}&=-\lambda\left(\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta_{t}-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})\right)dt+\sigma D\left(\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta_{t}-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})\right)dB_{t},\\
\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta_{0}&\sim\rho_{0},\end{split}

(1.12)

where
\rho_{t}:=\mathrm{Law}(\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta_{t})
.
Here, the definition of the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})
is analogous to the definition in (
1.11
) with
\rho_{t}
replacing the empirical measure
\rho_{t}^{N}
; see also Section
2.1
.
The macroscopic dynamics (
1.12
) can be regarded as the evolution of a typical particle in an interacting particle system of the form (
1.2
) when the number
N
of particles is large.
The path
\rho\in\mathcal{C}([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with
\rho_{t}=\rho(t)=\Law(\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta_{t})
satisfies the nonlinear nonlocal Fokker-Planck equation

\partial_{t}\rho_{t}=\lambda\textrm{div}\left(\left(\theta-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})\right)\rho_{t}\right)+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\partial_{kk}\left(D\left(\theta-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})\right)_{kk}^{2}\rho_{t}\right)

(1.13)

in a weak sense (see Definition
2.2
).

Remark 1.1

(Mean-field approximation for CB
2
O)
.

Analyzing the mean-field dynamics (
1.12
) or the associated Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
) in lieu of the interacting particle system (
1.2
)
is justified by establishing the convergence

\rho^{N}\rightharpoonup\rho\in\mathcal{C}([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\quad N\rightarrow\infty,

(1.14)

or, more quantitatively, by obtaining a mean-field approximation result of the form

\max_{i=1,\dots,N}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\theta_{t}^{i}-\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta_{t}^{i}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\leq CN^{-1},

(1.15)

where
\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta_{t}^{i}
denote
N
i.i.d. copies of the mean-field dynamics (
1.12
)
which are coupled to the processes
\theta_{t}^{i}
by choosing the same initial conditions as well as same Brownian motion paths,
see, e.g., the recent review papers
[
38
,
37
]
.
While we leave establishing the convergence (
1.14
) or an estimate of the form (
1.15
) for the CB
2
O dynamics as an interesting and challenging (due to the more intricate definition of the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}
) open problem for future research,
analogous results for CBO in the setting of classical optimization have been provided
[
65
,
51
,
57
,
64
]
.
An exhaustive survey of this type of results in the setting of CBO can be found in
[
51
, Remark 1.2]
or, in more detail, in
[
84
, Section 3.1.2]
.

Remark 1.2

(Choice of the hyperparameter
\alpha
in CBO-type methods)
.

Although it might seem tempting to set the hyperparameter
\alpha
to infinity in view of typical mean-field convergence statement for CBO-type methods, see, e.g.,
[
51
, Theorem 3.7]
or Theorem
2.7
,
this is not an advisable strategy in the finite particle regime.
As elaborated on in
[
51
, Section 3.3]
and
[
84
, Section 3.1]
,
the analytical framework leveraging a mean-field perspective decomposes the approximation error of the finite interacting particle dynamics (
1.2
) into two central error contributions.
First, the mean-field approximation error (see Remark
1.1
), which captures how well the microscopic interacting particle system (
1.2
) approximates the macroscopic mean-field limit (
1.12
) and (
1.13
) as the number
N
of employed particles grows.
Second, the approximation error of the global target minimizer
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
by the mean-field dynamics (
1.12
).
While the latter error improves as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
, the first error would degenerate, see
[
51
, Proposition 3.11]
, where the constant
C
in (
1.15
) is shown to exponentially depend on
\alpha
.
For this reason, in practical applications, a trade-off is required when it comes to the choice of the hyperparameter
\alpha
, see
[
51
, Theorem 3.8]
.

Before wrapping up this introduction by giving an overview of the contributions and organization of this paper, as well as provide pointers to relevant related literature, we first discuss in the subsequent remark an extension of the CB
2
O dynamics where each particle experiences an additional local gradient drift w.r.t. the lower-level objective function
L
.

Remark 1.3

(CB
2
O algorithm with additional gradient drift w.r.t.
L
)
.

In several applications of real-world interest,
it can be of numerical benefit to enhance the particle selection mechanisms present in the CB
2
O method by adding a local gradient drift into the dynamics. This extra term facilitates the detection of minimizers of the lower-level objective function
L
, since individual particles can get closer to, at the very least, locally optimal points of the objective landscape of
L
. In concrete terms, the time-evolution of the
i
-th agent of this version of CB
2
O is given by

\begin{split}d\theta_{t}^{i}&=-\lambda\left(\theta_{t}^{i}-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})\right)dt+\sigma D\left(\theta_{t}^{i}-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})\right)dB_{t}^{i}\\
&\quad\,-\lambda_{\nabla}\nabla L(\theta_{t}^{i})\,dt+\sigma_{\nabla}D\left(\nabla L(\theta_{t}^{i})\right)dB_{t}^{\nabla,i},\\
\theta_{0}^{i}&\sim\rho_{0}.\end{split}

(1.16)

Note that a gradient drift w.r.t. the upper-level objective function
G
is typically not desirable, as
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
is generally not a critical point of
G
on the whole domain.

1.1
Contributions

The contributions of this work are fourfold.
First of all, we propose a novel algorithm for solving bi-level optimization problems of the form (
1.1
) with a potentially nonconvex non-smooth lower-level objective function
L
and a potentially nonconvex non-smooth upper-level objective function
G
.
Such problems are of widespread relevance in science and engineering, machine learning and artificial intelligence as well as economics and operations research.
We tackle this problem by proposing
consensus-based bi-level optimization, abbreviated as CB
2
O,
which takes inspiration from the family of particle-based optimization algorithms, in particular from consensus-based optimization
[
82
]
, which in contrast has been designed for classical unconstrained optimization problems. Our method leverages a particle selection principle implemented through the suitable choice of a quantile of the lower-level objective function
L
to ensure that the constraint in (
1.1
) is satisfied, while the remaining particles are weighted w.r.t. a Gibbs-type measure in order to ensure optimization in terms of the upper-level objective function
G
. In stark contrast to a variety of other algorithms, particle-based or not, CB
2
O is intrinsically designed to solve the problem (
1.1
), rather than a relaxation thereof. In particular, the CB
2
O dynamics are unchanged if the objective
L
is composed with an increasing function.

Second, and marking the first main theoretical contribution of the paper,
we rigorously prove in Theorem
2.4
existence of solutions to the mean-field dynamics (
1.12
) and the associated Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
) of CB
2
O under reasonable regularity assumptions on the initial distribution
\rho_{0}
.
The design of the consensus point in (
1.11
) creates technical challenges of fundamental nature when establishing such result due to a lack of stability under Wasserstein perturbations of the measure, see Remark
3.1
.
This renders the standard classical coupling method, which is typically used in the CBO literature
[
31
]
, ineffective and in particular insufficient to prove existence and uniqueness of solutions to (
1.12
) and (
1.13
).
To resolve this, we prove with Proposition
3.2
the stability of the consensus point (
1.11
) w.r.t. a combination of Wasserstein and
L^{2}
perturbations.
This requires the development of several new ideas that enhance the standard toolbox
[
31
]
used in papers discussing CBO-like dynamics and may be of independent interest beyond CB
2
O and this paper.
More specifically, we resort to PDE considerations and work in appropriate spaces of functions to extend the classical Picard iteration method used in
[
3
,
47
]
to analyze standard CBO dynamics in bounded domains.
With this, our paper contributes in novel ways to the CBO literature, and, more broadly, to the corpus of global optimization methods.

Third, and constituting the other central theoretical contribution of our paper,
is a global convergence analysis of the CB
2
O method in mean-field law
[
51
]
.
Following the analytical framework put forward by the authors of
[
51
,
50
,
84
]
,
Theorem
2.7
states that under mild assumptions about the initial distribution
\rho_{0}
and minimal assumptions on the objective functions
L
and
G
,
CB
2
O converges in mean-field law to the good global minimizer
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
of the bi-level optimization problem (
1.1
).
More rigorously, we prove that the law
\rho_{t}
solving the nonlinear nonlocal Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
) converges w.r.t. the Wasserstein distance exponentially fast to a Dirac delta located at
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
provided suitable choices of the hyperparameters
\beta
and
\alpha
.
The influence of those choices is extensively discussed and illustrated in Remarks
2.9
and
2.10
, respectively.
Once again,
the design of the consensus point in (
1.11
) requires a novel treatment due to the additional particle selection principle. For this reason,
we first establish in Proposition
4.2
a quantitative “quantiled” Laplace principle (Q
2
LP),
which combines a suitable choice of the hyperparameter
\beta
, and through it a choice of the quantile
q^{L}_{\beta}
, with the quantitative Laplace principle
[
51
, Proposition 4.5]
.
This technique may be of independent interest.

Fourth and lastly, we provide extensive numerical experiments that demonstrate the practicability and efficacy of the CB
2
O method (
1.2
).
In Section
5.2
, we treat the special instance of constrained global optimization, where we firstly compare CB
2
O to particle-based methods designed for this task and then perform a thorough study of the influence of the various hyperparameters of the scheme. In Section
5.3
, we then tackle a sparse representation learning task
[
59
]
thereby demonstrating that the CB
2
O method is also applicable in high-dimensional real-world scenarios.
For an additional machine learning example, in the context of federated learning,
we refer to the paper
[
56
]
.

1.2
Related Works

Related works in bi-level optimization problems of the form (
1.1
).

The convex case of bi-level optimization problems of the form (
1.1
) (also known as simple bi-level optimization problems), i.e., where both the upper- and lower-level objective functions
G
and
L
in (
1.1
) are assumed to be convex,
has been extensively studied in recent years.
In what follows,
we discuss those works that are related to our method,
and refer the reader to
[
58
,
69
,
40
]
for a more comprehensive review of the literature on this problem.

One standard approach for solving convex bi-level optimization problems of the form (
1.1
) is to recast the problem as a single-level unconstrained optimization problem by introducing a suitable Lagrangian; this approach is commonly referred to as regularization approach. Specifically, in
[
94
]
the authors propose to combine the upper- and lower-level objective functions in (
1.1
) to obtain the unconstrained optimization problem
\operatorname*{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\{\chi L(\theta)+G(\theta)\}
, with regularization parameter
\chi>0
.
To address the issue of tuning
\chi
, works such as
[
28
,
93
,
45
]
propose to iteratively update the regularization parameter in a suitable way, producing in this way a sequence
\{\chi_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
. This approach has been further explored under various assumptions and different algorithmic choices in
[
61
,
77
,
5
,
70
,
91
,
40
]
.
However, these approaches are dual methods inherently and thus are effective only when strong duality holds, thereby limiting their applicability to the convex case.
In contrast, our interacting particle system-based approach is a primal method,
which directly and inherently tackles the primal problem (
1.1
),
making it consequently more suitable for the nonconvex setting.

Another strategy is to approximate the set
\Theta
of global minimizers of
L
with a surrogate set of simpler and more explicit form, before applying conventional projection-based or projection-free algorithms using this surrogate set
[
13
,
69
,
29
,
30
]
.
This approach is referred to as the sub-level set approach.
However, these surrogate sets are built based on the convexity of the lower-level objective function
L
, which limits their ability to accurately approximate the set
\Theta
in nonconvex settings, thereby reducing the effectiveness of those methods.
Our CB
2
O method (
1.2
) shares some conceptual similarities with the sub-level set approach.
Specifically, the set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]
, as defined in (
1.7
), can be regarded as an approximation of the neighborhoods of the set
\Theta
of global minimizers of the lower-level objective
L
, in particular, in the mean-field regime with small
\beta
.
Unlike existing methods, however, the construction of
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{N}]
does not depend on the convexity of the objective function
L
, making it, in consequence, applicable to the relevant class of nonconvex problems that are of interest to us.

To the best of our knowledge,
[
59
]
is the only paper that directly addresses nonconvex bi-level optimization problems of the form (
1.1
).
That paper introduces a dynamic barrier constraint on the search direction at each iteration, leading to a simple gradient-based algorithm that balances the objective functions
G
and
L
through an adaptive combination of coefficients, thereby tracing a trajectory towards the optimal solution.
Notably, similarly to our approach,
[
59
]
is also a primal method, which is generally preferred for nonconvex problems as explained earlier. However, their theoretical results only guarantee convergence to stationary points of the nonconvex problem.
In contrast, we rigorously demonstrate that our method converges to the desired target global minimizer
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
with arbitrary precision, at least in the mean-field regime.

Related works in general consensus-based optimization.

Consensus-based optimization methods for classical optimization have been introduced by the authors of
[
82
]
motivated by the urge to develop and design a class of metaheuristic particle-based algorithms that are amenable to a rigorous mathematical convergence analysis.
The first serious and so far most promising global convergence frameworks,
leveraging a mean-field perspective,
were proposed in
[
31
,
34
]
and
[
51
,
50
,
83
,
84
]
, respectively.
While the authors of the former analyze the variance of the mean-field dynamics, thereby establishing consensus formation, and consecutively bounding the distance between the found consensus and the global minimizer of the problem, the authors of the latter group of papers directly investigate the Wasserstein distance between the law of the mean-field dynamics and the global minimizer. To further understand the global convergence property in the finite particle regime,
[
65
,
50
,
51
,
57
,
64
]
study the mean-field approximation of the finite particle system, which, together with the global convergence of the mean-field system, yields convergence guarantees for the finite particle algorithm implemented in practice.
It is worth mentioning that a convergence analysis directly at the finite particle level has so far been addressed only in
[
72
,
27
,
15
]
, where
[
15
]
adopts the Wasserstein-based analysis framework which we also follow in this work.

The applications and analyses of CBO-type algorithms have been extended to a variety of other optimization problems, including, e.g., stochastic optimization
[
18
]
,
multi-objective optimization
[
20
,
21
,
19
]
,
min-max problems
[
67
,
24
]
,
the simultaneous search of multiple global minimizers
[
25
,
53
]
,
sampling
[
33
]
and the optimization over the space of probability distributions
[
23
]
.
Further applications of CBO have also been applied to high-dimensional
machine learning problems
[
34
,
48
]
such as decentralized clustered federated learning
[
32
]
.
CBO has also been shown to be related to the well-known particle swarm optimization method
[
41
,
66
]
and stochastic gradient descent with specific noise
[
85
,
86
]
.

The closely related work
[
62
]
proposes a CBO-type method to solve general bi- and multi-level optimization problems.
In their approach, multiple interacting populations of particles are employed,
each designated to optimize one level of the problem, with the individual populations evolving independently according to a CBO-type dynamics with different time-scaling.
While the bi-level optimization problem that we study here can be seen as a specific instance of the type of problem considered in
[
62
]
, we highlight that their proposed method does not fully capture and apply to our setting.
Specifically, our typical scenario is that the lower-level objective function
L
possesses multiple global minimizers, a scenario that the standard CBO dynamics (as applied to each level of optimization problem in
[
62
]
) cannot effectively handle as it is not designed for such setting. Furthermore, here we provide rigorous justification of the global convergence of our method in the mean-field regime, whereas
[
62
]
puts more emphasis on the algorithmic development.

Related works in constrained consensus-based optimization.

For standard constrained optimization problems, which can be formulated as a specific instance of the bi-level optimization setting (
1.1
), several variants of CBO-type methods have been proposed.
These approaches can be broadly categorized into three main classes.

The first approach recasts the constrained optimization problem as an unconstrained problem by incorporating the constraint into the upper-level objective function via a penalization term.
The standard CBO
[
82
]
is then applied to this modified problem
[
22
,
36
,
63
]
.
However, penalization-based methods are effective primarily when the penalty is exact
[
26
,
22
]
, a condition that does not generally hold for nonconvex constraint sets.

The second approach involves projection onto the constrained set, referred to projection-based methods.
For example,

[
11
]
proposes a predictor-corrector CBO method where particles are projected onto the feasible convex set at each time step.
Similarly,

[
47
,
48
,
49
]
design CBO dynamics intrinsic to manifolds such as spheres and tori, leveraging simple projection mechanisms.
Despite their effectiveness, these methods require the computation of the distance function
\text{dist}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet},\Theta)
to the constraint set
\Theta
, which becomes infeasible for complex constraint sets and may not be possible in nonconvex settings.

The third approach
[
33
,
35
]
introduces a large gradient forcing term, derived from the constraint set, into the standard CBO dynamics
[
82
]
.
This ensures rapid particle concentration on the constraint set, achieving high accuracy and stability when the constraint set is convex or otherwise well-behaved.
However, this method lacks convergence guarantees for general constraint sets and requires the evaluation of the inverse of the Hessian at each step
[
35
]
, which is computationally infeasible for high-dimensional problems.

In addition to these main approaches,
[
14
]
implements and explores a reflective boundary condition for CBO within compact convex domains.

In contrast to these existing methods, which typically rely on favorable geometric properties (e.g., convexity) of the constraint set, our CB
2
O method, by leveraging a lower-level objective function
L
whose set of minimizers coincide with the constraint set of interest, can be applied to general (and in particular nonconvex) constraint sets, achieving the accuracy and stable behavior at the same time.

Related works in evolutionary algorithms for bi-level optimization problems.

Due to the potential nonconvexity in both the upper- and lower-level objective functions,
evolutionary and metaheuristic algorithms have been proposed to address bi-level optimization problems.
Some of the pioneering methods date back to the late 1980s; see
[
6
,
78
]
.
Since then, various evolutionary algorithms tailored to bi-level optimization problems have been explored, see, e.g.,
[
98
,
81
,
74
,
100
,
7
,
68
]
, amongst many others.
For a comprehensive review, we refer the reader to
[
92
]
.
Despite their popularity and success, those metaheuristic approaches typically lack a rigorous mathematical foundation.
In contrast, for our CB
2
O method, which falls within the class of metaheuristic algorithms, we can provide a rigorous mathematical justification for its ability to solve problem (
1.1
) by
building upon the works
[
31
,
36
,
51
,
50
]
and by introducing new analysis and mathematical constructions. We thus believe that this paper strengthens and advances the field of metaheuristic methods for bi-level optimization.

1.3
Organization

In Section
2
we present the main theoretical contributions of this paper.
To this end, Section
2.1
first reintroduces the CB
2
O dynamics which is theoretically investigated in the remainder of the theoretical parts of this paper.
It should be regarded as a merely (for analytical purposes) regularized version of the CB
2
O algorithm that has been described in the introduction. While in practice this regularized version does work well, the modifications are not necessary to obtain successful experimental results and for this reason we implement the simpler variant discussed in the introduction.
Section
2.2
then provides an existence result as well as regularity estimates for solutions to the mean-field CB
2
O dynamics.
The accompanying proofs of this section are deferred to Section
3
.
In Section
2.3
we state a global mean-field law convergence result for the CB
2
O dynamics to the good global minimizer
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
of the bi-level optimization problem (
1.1
).
We furthermore provide an elaborate discussion of the choices of the hyperparameters
\beta
and
\alpha
, obtaining in this way deeper insights into the design of our CB
2
O algorithm, while additionally hinting at the mechanisms at play in the convergence proof.
The proof details of this section can be found in Section
4
. Thereafter we turn towards the experimental validation of the proposed CB
2
O method.
For this purpose, Section
5
showcases a series of numerical experiments which demonstrate the practicability as well as efficiency of the CB
2
O algorithm, whose implementation is formalized in Section
5.1
.
In Section
5.2
, we consider the special instance of classical constrained global optimization in lower-dimensional settings, where we conduct an extensive experimental study of the influence of the various hyperparameters of our method on its performance.
Section
5.3
is concerned with a sparse representation learning task. We use this experiment to demonstrate the applicability of the CB
2
O method in high-dimensional modern applications with real data. For a further machine learning example in the setting of (clustered) federated learning,
we refer to the recent paper
[
56
]
,
where CB
2
O is used to implement a defense mechanism against backdoor adversarial attacks. We wrap up the paper in Section
6
.

For the sake of reproducible research, we provide the code implementing the CB
2
O algorithm introduced in this work and used to run the numerical experiments in Section
5
in the GitHub repository
https://github.com/SixuLi/CB2O
.

1.4
Notation

We use
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
to denote the set of
d
-dimension vectors with each element being a non-negative integer.
For a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, and
a=(a_{1},\dots,a_{d})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
|a|:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}a_{i}
, we denote
f^{(a)}:=\frac{\partial^{|\nu|}}{\partial_{\theta_{1}}^{a_{1}}\cdots\partial_{\theta_{d}}^{a_{d}}}f
.
For notation simplicity, we will use
f^{(l)}
or
D^{l}f
to denote
f^{(a)}
for some
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
|a|=l
.
Unless otherwise specified, we use
C
or
\widetilde{C}
to denote some generic constants.
Euclidean balls are denoted as
B_{r}(\theta):=\{\tilde{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\!:\!\|{\tilde{\theta}-\theta}\|_{2}\leq r\}
.
We define the distance between a point
\theta
and set
S
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as
\operatorname{dist}(\theta,S):=\inf_{\tilde{\theta}\in S}\,\|{\tilde{\theta}-\theta}\|_{2}
.
Furthermore, we denote the neighborhood of set
S
with radius
r
as
\mathcal{N}_{r}(S):=\{\tilde{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\operatorname{dist}(\tilde{\theta},S)\leq r\}
.
For the space of continuous functions
f:X\rightarrow Y
we write
\mathcal{C}(X,Y)
, with
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a suitable topological space
Y
.
For an open set
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and for
Y=\mathbb{R}^{m}
the spaces
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{c}(X,Y)
and
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{b}(X,Y)
consist of functions
f\in\mathcal{C}(X,Y)
that are
k
-times continuously differentiable and have compact support or are bounded, respectively.
We avoid making
Y
explicit in the real-valued case.
Furthermore, following the conventional notation in the literature, we abbreviate
\mathcal{C}^{1}([0,T];\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
by
\mathcal{C}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T])
.
The operators
\nabla
and
\Delta
denote the gradient and Laplace operators of a function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The main objects of study in this paper are laws of stochastic processes,
\rho\in\mathcal{C}([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, where the set
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
contains all Borel probability measures over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
With
\rho_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we refer to the snapshot of such law at time
t
.
In case we refer to some fixed distribution, we write
\varrho
.
Measures
\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with finite
p
-th moment
\int\|{\theta}\|_{2}^{p}\,d\varrho(\theta)
are collected in
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
For any
1\leq p<\infty
,
W_{p}
denotes the
Wasserstein-
p
distance between two Borel probability measures
\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(see, e.g.,
[
4
]
), which is defined by

\displaystyle W_{p}(\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2})=\left(\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2})}\int\|{\theta-\tilde{\theta}}\|_{2}^{p}\,d\pi(\theta,\tilde{\theta})\right)^{1/p},

(1.17)

where
\Pi(\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2})
denotes the set of all couplings of
\varrho_{1}
and
\varrho_{2}
, i.e., the collection of all Borel probability measures over
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
with marginals
\varrho_{1}
and
\varrho_{2}
.

\mathbb{E}(\varrho)
denotes the expectation of a random variable distributed according to a probability measure
\varrho
.

2
Consensus-Based Bi-Level Optimization (CB
2
O) and Main Theoretical Results

This section is dedicated to the presentation of the main theoretical contributions of this paper.
To this end, we first introduce in Section
2.1
the CB
2
O dynamics that we theoretically investigate in the subsequent two sections and which shall be regarded as a merely (for analytical purposes) regularized version of the variant that has been described intuitively and instructively in the introduction; we note that, for simplicity, it is the version of the dynamics presented in the Introduction the one that is implemented towards the end of the paper in Section
5
. In Section
2.2
, we then provide an existence result for solutions to the mean-field CB
2
O dynamics and establish regularity estimates thereof. The proofs of this section are deferred to Section
3
.
After studying existence and regularity of solutions to the mean-field PDE, we discuss in Section
2.3
the behavior of the CB
2
O dynamics by stating a global convergence result of the CB
2
O dynamics in mean-field law to the global minimizer of the bi-level optimization problem (
1.1
).
Its proof details can be found in Section
4
.

2.1
Consensus-Based Bi-Level Optimization (CB
2
O)

To solve a bi-level optimization problem of the form (
1.1
),
we propose an interacting particle system inspired by the class of consensus-based optimization methods
[
82
,
31
,
36
,
51
,
50
,
84
]
,
namely

C
onsensus-
B
ased
B
i-level
O
ptimization (CB
2
O).
It is described, as we restate in this concise overview here, by the system of stochastic differential equations (
1.2
) given by

\displaystyle d\theta_{t}^{i}

\displaystyle=-\lambda\left(\theta_{t}^{i}-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})\right)dt+\sigma D\left(\theta_{t}^{i}-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}^{N})\right)dB_{t}^{i},

\displaystyle\theta_{0}^{i}

\displaystyle\sim\rho_{0}\quad\text{for all }i=1,\dots,N,

where
((B_{t}^{i})_{t\geq 0})_{i=1,\dots,N}
are independent standard Brownian motions in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Moreover, the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}
is defined for an arbitrary measure
\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
according to

m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho):=m^{G}_{\alpha}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]):=\int\theta\frac{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta)}{\left\|{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}}\right\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho])}}dI^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho](\theta),\quad\text{with}\quad\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta):=\exp\left(-\alpha G(\theta)\right).

(2.1)

Here, the unnormalized measure
I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
is given as

\displaystyle I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]

\displaystyle:=\mathbbm{1}_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}\varrho,

(2.2)

where, for some fixed parameters
\delta_{q}>0
and sufficiently large
R>0
, the sub-level set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
is defined as

\displaystyle Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]

\displaystyle:=\left\{\theta\in B_{R}(0):L(\theta)\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]\,da+\delta_{q}\right\},

(2.3)

with the
a
-quantile function
q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]
of
\varrho
under
L
given by

\displaystyle q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]

\displaystyle:=\inf\big\{q\text{ s.t. }a\leq\varrho(L(\theta)\leq q)\big\}.

(2.4)

This latter definition generalizes the one of the quantile function (
1.5
) from the setting of a finite number of particles
to a general distribution
\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
As before,
the quantile function
q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]
from (
2.4
) returns the particular value such that the probability of a random variable with distribution
\varrho
having an objective value w.r.t.
L
smaller or equal to
q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]
is at least
a
.
The set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
, as defined in (
2.3
),
denotes, analogously to the definition in (
1.7
), the set of all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
whose objective function value w.r.t. the lower-level objective function
L
is, roughly speaking (see
(2)
and
(3)
below), smaller than the value of the quantile function
q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
for a parameter
\beta
. For our later analytical studies, however, and compared to the analogous definition (
1.7
) in the finite particle setting, the above definitions come with three modifications that can be interpreted as different ways of regularization.

(1)

The set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
is limited to a ball
B_{R}(0)
.
Since our goal is to search
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
, we naturally assume that the radius
R>0
is large enough so that
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\in B_{R}(0)
, i.e., in particular such that
R\geq\|{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\|_{2}+r_{R}
for some
r_{R}>0
.

(2)

Instead of choosing
q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
as the threshold parameter on the level of the lower-level objective function
L
in the definition of
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
in (
2.3
),
we use the smoothened threshold
\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]\,da
.
The theoretical analysis later on can be straightforwardly generalized to any regularized threshold of the form
\frac{1}{\vartheta\beta}\int_{(1-\vartheta)\beta}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]\,da
with
\vartheta\in(0,1)
, which can be made arbitrarily close to
q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
.

(3)

We introduce a small slack parameter
\delta_{q}
in (
2.3
).

Despite these modifications to the original definitions in the introduction, the sub-level set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
can still be interpreted as an approximation to the neighborhood of the set
\Theta
of global minimizers of the lower-level objective function
L
. This is expected when the support of
\varrho
covers
\Theta
and
\beta
and
\delta_{q}
are sufficiently small. We emphasize again that while the above modifications are required for our theoretical analysis, in our practical implementations
we stick to the definitions in (
1.7
) given that these definitions induce a scheme that is simpler to implement and has a comparable numerical performance as the modified scheme. At the theoretical level, the regularizations
(1)
and
(2)
are introduced to establish the well-posedness of the mean-field CB
2
O dynamics that we present in Section
2.2
. They are, on the other hand, not required for the convergence analysis presented in Section
2.3
. The regularization
(3)
is needed for the global convergence in mean-field law analysis.

Remark 2.1
.

With the definition of
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
as in (
2.3
),
the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}
as defined in (
2.1
) is, due to
(1)
, uniformly bounded,
i.e., satisfies
\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)}\|_{2}\leq R
for any
\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

As discussed in the introduction,
our mathematical analysis takes a mean-field perspective.
Motivated by several mean-field approximation results in the setting of classical optimization, see Remark
1.1
,
it is natural to postulate that in the mean-field limit,
i.e., as the number of particles
N
tends to infinity,
the CB
2
O dynamics (
1.2
) can be described by the self-consistent stochastic process
\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta=(\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
which solves the self-consistent nonlinear nonlocal SDE (
1.12
) given by

\displaystyle d\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta_{t}

\displaystyle=-\lambda\left(\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta_{t}-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})\right)dt+\sigma D\left(\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta_{t}-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})\right)dB_{t},

where
\rho_{t}:=\mathrm{Law}(\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta_{t})
.
The associated law
\rho
with
\rho_{t}=\rho(t)
must satisfy the Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
) given by

\partial_{t}\rho_{t}=\lambda\textrm{div}\left(\left(\theta-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})\right)\rho_{t}\right)+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\partial_{kk}\left(D\left(\theta-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})\right)_{kk}^{2}\rho_{t}\right)

in the weak sense, defined as follows.

Definition 2.2

(Weak solution to Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
))
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
T>0
.
We say
\rho\in\mathcal{C}([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
satisfies the Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
) with initial condition
\rho_{0}
in the weak sense in the time interval
[0,T]
,
if for all
\phi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and all
t\in(0,T)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int\phi(\theta)\,d\rho_{t}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\lambda\int\sum_{k=1}^{d}(\theta-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t}))_{k}\partial_{k}\phi(\theta)\,d\rho_{t}(\theta)

(2.5)

\displaystyle+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\int\sum_{k=1}^{d}D\!\left(\theta-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})\right)_{kk}^{2}\partial^{2}_{kk}\phi(\theta)\,d\rho_{t}(\theta),

and
\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\rho_{t}=\rho_{0}
in the weak sense of probability distributions.

Our main theoretical contributions, which we will present in the remainder of this section,
are split into two key theorems.
First, we discuss the existence of regular solutions to the mean-field system (
1.12
) in Theorem
2.4
.
Second, we discuss in Theorem
2.7
the long-time behavior properties of the mean-field PDE (
1.13
) and show that, under some mild assumptions on initialization and the correct tuning of hyperparameters, the measure
\rho_{t}
concentrates around the target global minimizer
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
.

For the remainder of the theoretical parts of this paper we limit our attention to the case of CB
2
O with isotropic diffusion, i.e.,
D\!\left(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}\right)=\left\|{\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}}\right\|_{2}\mathrm{Id}
in (
1.3
).

2.2
Well-Posedness of the Mean-Field CB
2
O Dynamics

Let us start the presentation of our main theoretical contributions with an existence result for solutions to the mean-field CB
2
O dynamics (
1.12
) and the associated Fokker-Plank Equation (
1.13
).
We moreover establish their regularity for objective functions
L
and
G
that satisfy the following assumptions.

Assumption 2.3
.

Throughout we are interested in objective functions
L\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
G\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

for which

W1

\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L:=\inf_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}L(\theta)>-\infty
,

W2

there exists
C_{1}>0
such that

\left|{L(\theta)-L(\theta^{\prime})}\right|\leq C_{1}\left\|{\theta-\theta^{\prime}}\right\|_{2}\qquad\text{for all}\;\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(2.6)

W3

L
’s distribution function
q\in[0,\infty)\mapsto\mathrm{Vol}(\{\theta\in B_{R}(0):L(\theta)\leq q\})
is
C_{L}
-Lipschitz continuous, where
R
is the radius of the ball in (
2.3
), see also
(1)
,

W4

\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{G}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{G}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}G:=\inf_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(\theta)>-\infty
,

W5

either
\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{G}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{G}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}G:=\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(\theta)<\infty
, or there exist constants
C_{2},C_{3}>0
such that

G(\theta)-\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{G}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{G}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}G\geq C_{2}\left\|{\theta}\right\|_{2}^{2}\quad\text{for all }\left\|{\theta}\right\|_{2}\geq C_{3},

(2.7)

W6

there exists
C_{4}>0
such that

G(\theta)-\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{G}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{G}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}G\leq C_{4}(1+\left\|{\theta}\right\|_{2}^{2})\quad\text{for all }\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(2.8)

Lower-level objective function
L
:

Assumption
W1
imposes that the lower-level objective function
L
is bounded from below by
\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L
.
Assumption
W2
requires that
L
is Lipschitz continuous with constant
C_{1}
. Assumption
W3
is an assumption on regularity of level sets of
L
that we impose to rule out lower-level objective functions
L
with level sets
\{\theta:L(\theta)=q\}
that are full-dimensional.

Upper-level objective function
G
:

Assumption
W4
requires that also the upper-level objective function
G
is lower-bounded.
Assumption
W5
requires that
G
is either bounded from above or has at least quadratic growth in the farfield. Assumption
W6
, on the other hand, requires that
G
also has bounded growth-rate globally.

In our first main theoretical result,
we establish the existence of solutions to the mean-field CB
2
O dynamics (
1.12
) and the associated nonlinear nonlocal Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
).
The proof of uniqueness of such solution is challenging, and is left for future investigation (see discussions in Section
6
).

Theorem 2.4

(Existence of regular solutions to the mean-field CB
2
O dynamics (
1.12
) and (
1.13
))
.

Let
L\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
G\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfy
W1
–
W6
.
Moreover, for
l\geq 0
, let
\rho_{0}\in H^{l+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathcal{P}_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be such that
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\in\operatorname{supp}(\rho_{0})
.
Then, for
T>0
, there exists a nonlinear process
\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta\in\mathcal{C}\left([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
satisfying the mean-field dynamics (
1.12
) in the strong sense whose associated law
\rho=\Law(\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta)
is a weak solution (Definition
2.2
) to the Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
) and has regularity

\rho\in W^{1,\infty}\left([0,T],H^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)\cap L^{\infty}([0,T],L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap\mathcal{C}\left([0,T],\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)\,.

(2.9)

Moreover,
\rho_{t}\in\mathcal{P}_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and the mapping
t\mapsto m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})
is
\frac{1}{2}
-Hölder continuous for
t\in[0,T]
.

A few comments about Theorem
2.4
are in order.
First and foremost, it is important to highlight that, compared to the classical well-posedness proof for CBO
[
82
]
, which can be found for instance in
[
31
,
51
]
,
the proof of Theorem
2.4
is substantially more involved and intricate.
This added complexity arises from the lack of Wasserstein stability for the consensus point (
2.1
).
For standard CBO, one has by
[
31
, Lemma 3.2]
that
\big\|{m^{G}_{\alpha}(\varrho)-m^{G}_{\alpha}(\widetilde{\varrho})}\big\|_{2}\leq CW_{2}(\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho})
.
Unfortunately, as we demonstrate in Remark
3.1
by giving an instructive counterexample, this is
not
the case for our consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)
. In contrast, we establish in Proposition
3.2
the following stability estimate

\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\widetilde{\varrho})}\big\|_{2}\leq C\left(\|\varrho-\widetilde{\varrho}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0))}+W_{2}\left(\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}\right)\right),

which holds for probability measures that are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure. As a result, we must pursue a different approach to the standard fixed-point argument used in
[
31
]
. Specifically, we extend the Picard’s iteration argument from
[
3
,
47
]
, originally applied to the dynamics restricted to a bounded domain, to one applicable over the entire space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
This requires integrating analyses at both the PDE and SDE levels and working within suitable spaces of functions; we believe that our analysis is thus of independent interest.

Second, the assumption
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\in\operatorname{supp}(\rho_{0})
about the initial configuration
\rho_{0}
is not really a restriction,
as it would anyhow hold immediately for
\rho_{t}
for any
t>0
in view of the diffusive character of
the dynamics (
1.12
), see also
[
51
, Remark 4.8]
.
It is an assumption of technical nature that ensures that the consensus point is always well-defined for
\rho
satisfying the Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
).
More details are to be found in Section
3.3
when proving Theorem
2.4
.

Third, it is important to highlight that the Picard iteration approach that we follow to prove existence of solutions to the mean-field PDE does not allow us to deduce uniqueness. It is reasonable to expect that under additional regularity assumptions one may be able to prove uniqueness within a class of regular solutions of the mean field PDE. We will explore this question in future work.

Remark 2.5
.

Analogously to
[
51
]
for standard CBO,
the proof of Theorem
2.4
implies that,
for a weak solution
\rho\in\mathcal{C}\left([0,T],\mathcal{P}_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)
to the Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
),
we can extend the standard test function space
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to the space
\mathcal{C}^{2}_{*}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where

\mathcal{C}_{*}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\left\{\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):\|\nabla\phi(\theta)\|\leq C\left(1+\|\theta\|_{2}\right)\text{ and }\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\Delta\phi(\theta)|<\infty\right\}.

(2.10)

2.3
Global Convergence of the CB
2
O Dynamics in Mean-Field Law

We now present the main result about the global convergence of the CB
2
O dynamics (
1.12
) in mean-field law for a lower-level objective function
L
and an upper-level objective function
G
that satisfy the following assumptions. We suggest to the reader to take another look at Figure
2
to recall a typical setting for a bi-level optimization problem of the form (
1.1
) in order to convince themselves that the assumptions on the objective functions
L
and
G
that we make below are reasonable.

Assumption 2.6
.

Throughout we are interested in objective functions
L\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
G\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
for which

A1

there exists a unique
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\in\Theta:=\operatorname*{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}L(\theta)
with
\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L:=L(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})=\inf_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}L(\theta)
such that

\displaystyle G(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})=\inf_{\theta^{*}\in\Theta}G(\theta^{*}),

(2.11)

A2

there exist
h_{L},R^{H}_{L}>0
, and
H_{L}<\infty
such that

\displaystyle L(\theta)-\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L\leq H_{L}\left\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}^{h_{L}}\quad\text{ for all }\theta\in B_{R^{H}_{L}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}),

(2.12)

A3

there exist
L_{\infty},R_{L},\eta_{L}>0
, and
\nu_{L}\in(0,\infty)
such that

\displaystyle\operatorname{dist}(\theta,\Theta)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\eta_{L}}\left(L(\theta)-\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L\right)^{\nu_{L}}\quad\text{ for all }\theta\in\mathcal{N}_{R_{L}}(\Theta),

(2.13a)

\displaystyle L(\theta)-\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L

\displaystyle>L_{\infty}\quad\text{ for all }\theta\in\big(\mathcal{N}_{R_{L}}(\Theta)\big)^{c},

(2.13b)

A4

there exist
h_{G},R^{H}_{G}>0
, and
H_{G}<\infty
such that

\displaystyle G(\theta)-G(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\leq H_{G}\left\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}^{h_{G}}\quad\text{ for all }\theta\in B_{R^{H}_{G}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}),

(2.14)

A5

there exist
G_{\infty},R_{G},\eta_{G}>0
, and
\nu_{G}\in(0,\infty)
such that for all
r_{G}\leq R_{G}
there exists
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}\in B_{r_{G}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
such that

\displaystyle\big\|{\theta-\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}}\big\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\eta_{G}}\left(G(\theta)-G(\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}})\right)^{\nu_{G}}\quad\text{ for all }\theta\in B_{r_{G}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}),

(2.15a)

\displaystyle G(\theta)-G(\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}})

\displaystyle>G_{\infty}\quad\text{ for all }\theta\in\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)\backslash B_{r_{G}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}).

(2.15b)

In Assumption
A1
, we clearly state that we assume there is a unique minimizer to the bilevel optimization problem (
1.1
). Typically,
L
has more than one global minimizer, and it may even take the minimal value at an entire set of any shape in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
subject to the additional assumptions
A2
–
A3
.
All those global minimizers have objective value
\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L
.
Amongst them,
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
is
the
global minimizer performing best w.r.t. the robustness-promoting upper-level objective function
G
, i.e., the good global minimizer.
Let us recall at this point that we additionally assumed in the introduction that
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\in B_{R}(0)
, where
R>0
is supposed to be sufficiently large such that
R\geq\big\|{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}+r_{R}
for
r_{R}>0
as defined earlier.

Lower-level objective function
L
:
Assumption
A2
imposes that the objective function
L
is
h_{L}
-Hölder continuous with constant
H_{L}
locally around
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
.
The lower-level objective function
L
moreover satisfies a tractability condition on its landscape in the form of Assumption
A3
.
In the neighborhood of the set of global minimizers
\Theta
, i.e., on the set
\mathcal{N}_{R_{L}}(\Theta)
, the first part of
A3
, Equation (
2.13a
), requires the local coercivity of
L
w.r.t.
\Theta
, thereby ensuring that the objective function values of
L
on the level sets give instructive information about the distance of the associated points in the level sets to the set of global minimizers.
Such condition is known as an inverse continuity property
[
51
,
50
,
48
,
83
]
.
Compared to the assumptions made in those references, however,
A3
is more local in nature by being an assumption on the distance to the whole set
\Theta
, instead of to a single point.
This in particular warrants the existence of multiple global minimizers of the lower-level objective function
L
.
For similar assumptions in the literature, we refer to quadratic growth or error bound conditions, as, e.g., discussed in
[
8
,
80
,
17
]
.
Away from the neighborhood
\mathcal{N}_{R_{L}}(\Theta)
of the set of global minimizers
\Theta
, the second part of
A3
, Equation (
2.13b
), merely ensures that there is a sufficient gap in the objective function values to the minimal value
\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L
,
thereby preventing in particular that
L(\theta)\approx\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L
for some
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
considerably far from
\Theta
.
For an illustration of this assumption, both Figures
2
and
2
may be consulted.

Figure 2
:
Illustration of Assumptions
A3
and
A5
on the lower-level objective function
L
and the upper level objective function
G
.

Upper-level objective function
G
:

Assumption
A4
imposes that the objective function
G
is
h_{G}
-Hölder continuous with constant
H_{G}
locally around
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
.
In addition, Assumption
A5
requires the upper-level objective function
G
to admit an inverse continuity condition
[
51
,
50
,
48
,
83
]
, which has to hold exclusively on a neighborhood of the set of global minimizers
\Theta
of
L
.
More precisely,
A5
imposes an inverse continuity condition for all neighborhoods
\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
with
r_{G}\leq R_{G}
for some (potentially sufficiently small)
R_{G}>0
.
Outside the set
\mathcal{N}_{R_{G}}(\Theta)
, the behavior of the function
G
is of no relevance.
On any such neighborhood
\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
,
the minimizer of the upper-level objective function
G
, which we denote by
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
(the reader may want to convince themselves that
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
is typically not
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
; see Figure
2
for an illustration), is required to lie close to
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
; to be precise, within the ball
B_{r_{G}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\subset\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
of radius
r_{G}
around
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
.
On this ball, the objective function
G
is locally coercive around
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
as required by the first condition of
A5
, Equation (
2.15a
).
Outside of
B_{r_{G}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
, but for
\theta\in\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
, the second condition of
A5
, Equation (
2.15b
), ensures that
G(\theta)\approx G(\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}})=\min_{\tilde{\theta}\in\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)}G(\tilde{\theta})
does not occur.
That is, far away from
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
but within the neighborhood
\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
, the value
G(\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}})
is not attained.
This resembles the standard inverse continuity condition
[
51
,
50
,
48
,
83
]
, however, restricted to the neighborhood
\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
and w.r.t. the point
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
.
More intuitively speaking, recall that
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
, by definition of the bi-level optimization problem (
1.1
), is the global minimizer of the upper-level objective function
G
on the set
\Theta
.
Hence,
A5
can be regarded as a stability condition of the global minimizer of
G
for neighborhoods of
\Theta
.
It extends the aforementioned optimality property to those neighborhoods
\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
of
\Theta
with
r_{G}\leq R_{G}
, while allowing at the same time an error of order
r_{G}
when wishing to approximate
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
.
This error is necessary and natural as
G
typically admits (in realistic scenarios) on the set
\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
a global minimizer
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}\not=\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
.

A5
ensures that
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
is
r_{G}
-close to
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
, hence excluding cases where
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
might have jumped to, e.g., a separate component of the set
\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
.
Here, we again refer for an illustration to both Figures
2
and
2
.

We are now ready to state the main result about the global convergence of the mean-field CB
2
O dynamics (
1.13
).
The proof is deferred to Section
4
.

Theorem 2.7

(Convergence of the mean-field CB
2
O dynamics (
1.12
) and (
1.13
))
.

Let
L\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
G\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfy
A1
–
A5
.
Moreover, let
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be such that
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\in\operatorname{supp}(\rho_{0})
.
Fix any
\varepsilon\in(0,W_{2}^{2}(\rho_{0},\delta_{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}})/2)
and
\vartheta\in(0,1)
,
choose parameters
\lambda,\sigma>0
with
2\lambda>d\sigma^{2}
, and
define the time horizon

\displaystyle T^{*}:=\frac{1}{(1-\vartheta)\big(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\big)}\log\left(\frac{W_{2}^{2}(\rho_{0},\delta_{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}})/2}{\varepsilon}\right).

(2.16)

Then,
for
\delta_{q}>0
in (
2.3
) sufficiently small (see Remark
2.8
),
there exist
\beta_{0}>0
and
\alpha_{0}>0
(see Remarks
2.9
and
2.10
, as well as Figures
3
and
4
),
depending (among problem-dependent quantities) on
R
,
\delta_{q}
,
\varepsilon
and
\vartheta
,
such that for all
\beta<\beta_{0}
and
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
,
if
\rho\in\mathcal{C}([0,T^{*}],\mathcal{P}_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is a weak solution to the Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
) on the time interval
[0,T^{*}]
with initial condition
\rho_{0}
and if the mapping
t\mapsto m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})
is continuous for
t\in[0,T^{*}]
,
it holds

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\rho_{T},\delta_{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}})/2=\varepsilon\quad\text{ with }\quad T\in\left[\frac{1-\vartheta}{(1+\vartheta/2)}\;\!T^{*},T^{*}\right].

(2.17)

Furthermore, on the time interval
[0,T]
,
W_{2}^{2}(\rho_{t},\delta_{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}})
decays at least exponentially fast.
More precisely,

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\rho_{t},\delta_{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}})\leq W_{2}^{2}(\rho_{0},\delta_{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}})\exp\left(-(1-\vartheta)\left(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\right)t\right)

(2.18)

for all
t\in[0,T]
.

A few comments about Theorem
2.7
are in order.
For a discussion regarding the choices of the central hyperparameters
\beta
and
\alpha
we refer to the in-depth discussions below in Remarks
2.9
and
2.10
, respectively.
First we note, as already noted for Theorem
2.4
, that the assumption
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\in\operatorname{supp}(\rho_{0})
about the initial configuration
\rho_{0}
is not really a restriction,
as it would anyhow hold immediately for
\rho_{t}
for any
t>0
in view of the diffusive character of
the dynamics (
1.12
); see also
[
51
, Remark 4.8]
.
In fact, this has been rigorously proven recently in
[
54
]
.
Second, the dimensionality dependence of the condition
2\lambda>d\sigma^{2}
on the drift and noise parameter may be removed by using anisotropic noise
[
34
,
50
]
, which would require that
2\lambda>\sigma^{2}
.
Such conditions may be further relaxed by truncating the noise; see
[
52
]
.
Third, the rate of convergence in (
2.18
) is at least
(1-\vartheta)(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2})
and, as can be seen from the proof, at most
(1+\vartheta/2)(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2})
.
Thus, at the cost of taking
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
to allow for
\vartheta\rightarrow 0
, it can be made arbitrarily close to the rate
