(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2})
.
This at the same time renders more precise the time horizon
T
in (
2.17
) at which time the desired
\varepsilon
accuracy is reached for the Wasserstein distance
W_{2}^{2}(\rho_{T},\delta_{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}})/2
.
Fourth, we require in the statement of the theorem that
\rho\in\mathcal{C}([0,T^{*}],\mathcal{P}_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
has to be a weak solution to the Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
) with initial condition
\rho_{0}

and
the mapping
t\mapsto m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})
must be continuous.
Theorem
2.4
in the preceding section proves that solutions to the mean-field Fokker Planck equation with the desired regularity and for which the map
t\mapsto m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})
is continuous indeed exist under the assumptions stated in this result. Those assumptions are therefore sufficient, but they may not be necessary and
in particular, the statement of the above theorem may be valid under a different set of assumptions as long as the aforementioned requirements about the regularity of
\rho
as well as the continuity of the consensus point mapping are satisfied.

Let us now highlight how the hyperparameters
\beta
and
\alpha
influence the behavior of the CB
2
O dynamics (
1.12
) and thereby the CB
2
O algorithm (
1.2
).
The illustrations and insights that we provide here hint at how we later rigorously prove in Section
4
our main convergence statement, Theorem
2.7
.

We start with the parameter
\delta_{q}
, which is of mere proof-technical purpose and is convenient due to its regularizing effect.

Remark 2.8

(Choice of
\delta_{q}
)
.

The requirement
\delta_{q}>0
sufficiently small in Theorem
2.7
means that

\displaystyle\delta_{q}\leq\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{L_{\infty},(\eta_{L}r_{G,\varepsilon})^{1/\nu_{L}}\right\}

(2.19)

where
r_{G,\varepsilon}
is as defined explicitly in (
4.37
).

Let us now address the choice and influence of the hyperparameter
\beta
.

Remark 2.9

(Choice of hyperparameter
\beta
)
.

The hyperparameter
\beta
is related to the lower-level objective function
L
and steers (in the finite particle terminology) the particle selection.
In the mean-field interpretation, it analogously restricts the particle density
\varrho
to its most favorable regions w.r.t.
L
.
Thereby,
\beta
warrants that CB
2
O is successful in identifying the set
\Theta
of global minimizers of
L
, which is a prerequisite to eventually succeeding in determining a point in the proximity of
\Theta
that is best w.r.t. the upper-level objective function
G
.
Note that at this point and with the parameter
\beta
only we can not drive the dynamics towards
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
.
Rather, our aim at this stage is to more and more accurately identify
\Theta
as
\beta
becomes smaller and smaller.

This selection principle is implemented through the
\beta
-quantile
q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
and the associated level set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
, which identifies those regions of the domain of
L
that have the best (lowest) lower-level objective function value.
Only the parts of the density supported on this set are then taken into account when computing the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)=m^{G}_{\alpha}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho])
in (
2.1
),
where
I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]=\mathds{1}_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}\varrho
denotes the associated restricted measure which exerts precisely this selection principle.
Phrased differently, the consensus point is exclusively computed on the quantile set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
3
3

3

A word of caution: Note that this does not yet ensure that the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)
itself lies, even if computed on it, within the set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
, let alone that it approximates
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\in\Theta
.
That we are indeed able to achieve the latter two properties is the topic of Remark
2.10
and thanks to the inverse continuity property
A5
on
G
and the hyperparameter
\alpha
as we discuss there.
.

With the set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
, and thus through the hyperparameter
\beta
, we can identify a set which is expected to approximate
\Theta
up to an accuracy level that is controllable through
\beta
.
The quality of the approximation of the set
\Theta
with the set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
improves as
\beta\rightarrow 0
(given some regularity of
L
).
As we let
\beta
become smaller and smaller,
by construction,
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
is induced to contain only points with objective function value closer and closer to
\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L
, narrowing down the set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
towards
\Theta
in the process.
As soon as
\beta=0
,
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]=\Theta
.
This behavior is illustrated in Figure
3
, where we plot the set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
, i.e., the support of the measure
I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]=\mathds{1}_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}\varrho
(provided that
\varrho
were supported everywhere),
for decreasing (from left to right) values of
\beta
.

Figure 3
:
The influence of the hyperparameter
\beta
. By decreasing the value of
\beta
from left to right, we shrink the quantile set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
(illustrated as yellow shadows on the
xy
plane and projected onto the upper-level objective), thereby ensuring an increasingly finer approximation of the set
\Theta
of global minimizers of
L
.

In order to technically approximate the set
\Theta
through
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
sufficiently well,
we need to choose in Theorem
2.7
the hyperparameter
\beta
sufficiently small in the sense that

\beta<\beta_{0}
with

\beta_{0}:=\frac{1}{2}\rho_{0}\big(B_{r_{H,\varepsilon}/2}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)\exp(-p_{H,\varepsilon}T^{*}),

(2.20)

where
r_{H,\varepsilon}
is as in (
4.39
) and where
p_{H,\varepsilon}
is defined as discussed after (
4.40
).

Being now able to approximate the set
\Theta
with
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
through the choice of
\beta
, our next task is to locate
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
within this set.
For this we need to discuss the choice and influence of the hyperparameter
\alpha
.

Remark 2.10

(Choice of hyperparameter
\alpha
)
.

The hyperparameter
\alpha
is related to the upper-level objective function
G
and is concerned with the actual approximation of the good global minimizer
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
within the set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
.
To this end,
we actually don’t aim at approximating
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
directly but instead intend to find the global minimizer
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
of
G
restricted to the set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
.
However, granted the stability of the global optimizer of
G
for neighborhoods of
\Theta
, which is ensured by the inverse continuity property
A5
,
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
is close to
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
.
This closeness has been controlled and quantified through the choice of
\beta
in Remark
2.9
.
Thus, it is sufficient to approximate
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
when computing the consensus point (
2.1
).

In order to do so, we leverage a quantitative nonasymptotic variant of the classical Laplace principle
[
42
,
79
]
, namely
[
51
, Proposition 4.5]
, which ensures that
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)=m^{G}_{\alpha}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho])
can approximate
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}=\operatorname*{\arg\min}_{\theta\in Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}G(\theta)
arbitrarily well provided a large enough choice of the hyperparameter
\alpha
.
In the mean-field limit, the quality of this approximation improves as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
.
As we let
\alpha
become larger and larger,
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)
better and better approximates
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
.
As
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
,
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)\rightarrow\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
.
We illustrate this behavior in Figure
4
, where we depict the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)
for increasing (from left to right) values of
\alpha
.

Figure 4
:
The influence of the hyperparameter
\alpha
. By increasing the value of
\alpha
from left to right, we improve on the approximation of
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
, i.e., how well
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)
, depicted as the orange dot in all three plots, eventually approximates
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
.

In order to technically approximate
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
(and thus
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
by Remark
2.9
and
A5
) through
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)
sufficiently well,
we need to choose in Theorem
2.7
the hyperparameter
\alpha
sufficiently large in the sense that

\alpha>\alpha_{0}
with

\begin{split}\alpha_{0}&:=\frac{1}{u_{\varepsilon}}\Bigg(\log\left(\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{c\left(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma\right)}\right)-\log\left(\rho_{0}\big(B_{r_{\varepsilon}/2}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)\right)+\max\left\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{p_{\varepsilon}}{(1-\vartheta)\big(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\big)}\right\}\log\left(\frac{\mathcal{V}(\rho_{0})}{\varepsilon}\right)\!\Bigg),\end{split}

(2.21)

where
c(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma)
is as in (
4.36
), where
u_{\varepsilon}
and
r_{\varepsilon}
are as in (
4.43
) and (
4.44
), respectively, and where
p_{\varepsilon}
is defined as discussed after (
4.46
).

To wrap up the discussions about the choices of the hyperparameters
\beta
and
\alpha
, let us point out their interplay in the convergence proof, which is presented in Section
4
. When deriving time-evolution inequalities for
W_{2}^{2}(\rho_{t},\delta_{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}})
, as we do in Lemma
4.1
, the central quantity to be controlled by suitable choices of the hyperparameters is
\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}
.
To obtain this control, we employ the quantitative quantiled Laplace principle in Proposition
4.2
, where we identify two error contributions. The first of which comes from the approximation accuracy of the set
\Theta
of global minimizers of the lower-level objective function
L
by the set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}]
.
The second contribution arises from approximating, within this set, the global minimizer
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
of the upper-level objective function
G
by
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})
.
In summary, we have a decomposition of the error
\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}
of the form

\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}\leq\underbrace{\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}}\big\|_{2}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{small by choice of $\alpha$ since}\\
\text{$\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}=\operatorname*{\arg\min}_{\theta\in Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}]}G(\theta)$}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\big\|{\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{small by choice of $\beta$ since}\\
\text{$\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}\in Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}]$}\end{subarray}}.

(2.22)

Let us point out, though, that the decomposition in the proof of Proposition
4.2
in Section
4.3
is slightly more implicit.
However, (
2.22
) does paint the correct picture for an understanding of the interplay between the hyperparameters
\beta
and
\alpha
.
For more technical details we refer to Remark
4.3
.

Before closing this section about the main results of the paper and providing the proof details as well as numerical experiments thereafter,
let us continue the discussion from Remark
1.3
on integrating a gradient drift w.r.t. the lower-level objective function
L
into the CB
2
O algorithm (
1.2
) as well as the associated CB
2
O mean-field dynamics (
1.12
).
For standard CBO
[
82
,
31
,
51
,
50
]
, this has been done and analyzed in
[
83
]
.

Remark 2.11

(Mean-field law convergence of the CB
2
O dynamics (
1.16
) with additional gradient drift w.r.t.
L
)
.

As discussed in Remark
1.3
, it is reasonable and in some applications advisable to add an gradient drift w.r.t.
L
in the CB
2
O algorithm (
1.16
), i.e.,
\lambda_{\nabla}\not=0
.
In such case, the lower-level objective function
L
naturally needs to be continuously differentiable and we additionally require that
L\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is such that

A6

there exists
C_{\nabla L}>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla L(\theta)-\nabla L(\theta^{\prime})}\right\|_{2}\leq C_{\nabla L}\left\|{\theta-\theta^{\prime}}\right\|_{2}\quad\text{for all }\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(2.23)

Assumption
A6
is a standard smoothness assumption on
L
which requires
\nabla L
to be Lipschitz continuous.

Under this additional assumption, the convergence statement of Theorem
2.7
can be extended to the mean-field dynamics of (
1.16
) by following
[
83
]
.

3
Proof Details for Section
2.2

In this section, we provide the details of the proof of Theorem
2.4
, the existence of regular solutions to the mean-field dynamics (
1.12
). In Section
3.1
, we present a proof sketch to outline the main steps.
In Section
3.2
, we provide a concrete example showing that, different from the consensus points defined in the standard CBO setting
[
31
]
, our consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}
is not stable under Wasserstein perturbations.
This lack of stability forces us to develop a stability estimate for
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}
with respect to more restrictive perturbations as presented in Proposition
3.2
.
In Section
3.3
, we complete the proof of Theorem
2.4
.

3.1
Proof Sketch

Suppose that
\rho=\mathrm{Law}(\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta)
is a solution to the Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
) satisfying
\rho\in\mathcal{C}\left([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
. Then we may apply standard theory of SDEs (see, e.g.,
[
9
, Chapter 6.2]
) to conclude that there exists a unique strong solution to the mean-field dynamics (
1.12
).
Therefore, in what follows we mainly prove that there exists a solution to the PDE (
1.13
) with the desired regularity.

The proof is based on the Picard’s iteration method. In what follows, we denote by
\rho^{n}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the measure at time
t
of the
n
-th iterate in the Picard-type recursion. Precisely, we set
\rho^{0}_{t}:=\rho_{0}
for all
t>0
, i.e.,
\rho^{n=0}_{t}(\theta)=\rho_{0}(\theta)
for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and for all
t>0
.
Then, having defined
\rho^{n}
for a given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\rho^{n+1}
be the solution to the
linear
Fokker-Planck equation

\partial_{t}\rho^{n+1}_{t}=\lambda\nabla\cdot\left(\left(\theta-m(\rho^{n}_{t})\right)\rho^{n+1}_{t}\right)+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\Delta\left(\left\|{\theta-m(\rho^{n}_{t})}\right\|_{2}^{2}\rho^{n+1}_{t}\right)

(3.1)

with initial data
\rho^{n+1}_{0}=\rho_{0}\in H^{l+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathcal{P}_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
We will show by induction that

\displaystyle\{\rho^{n+1}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq W^{1,\infty}([0,T],H^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathcal{C}([0,T],\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\,.

(3.2)

Moreover, we prove that the sequence
\{\rho^{n+1}\}_{n}
has uniform bounded norms in each of those spaces.

Secondly, by the Aubin-Lions lemma and a diagonal argument, we are able to conclude that there exists a subsequence
\{\rho^{n_{k}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\{\rho^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and a limiting function
\rho
such that
\rho^{n_{k}}
strongly converges to
\rho
in
\mathcal{C}([0,T],L_{\text{loc}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.
Since
\{\rho^{n_{k}}\}_{k}
has uniformly bounded norms, we can further conclude that the limiting function
\rho
satisfies the similar regularity of
\{\rho^{n_{k}}\}_{k}
, specified in (
2.9
).

Lastly, we verify that
\rho
is a solution to the Equation (
1.13
) in the weak sense (Definition
2.2
).
In Equation (
3.1
), by
m(\varrho)
we mean
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)
as defined in Section
2.1
. We have simplified the notation for the reader’s convenience and also to highlight that our approach in the proof of Theorem
2.4
, which is presented in Section
3.3
, continues to work for general functions
m(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})
satisfying a suitable stability estimate.

3.2
Stability Estimate for the Consensus Point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}

A stability estimate for the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)
w.r.t. perturbations of the measure
\varrho
is crucial for proving the well-posedness of the mean-field dynamics (
1.12
) and (
1.13
),
as well as for establishing results about a mean-field approximation
[
51
, Section 3.3 and 5]
.
For the standard CBO system
[
82
,
31
]
used for simple unconstrained optimization,
the consensus point is stable w.r.t. Wasserstein perturbations
[
31
, Lemma 3.2]
, allowing for an estimate of the form (
3.3
).
This stability enables an effective application of the coupling method as worked out in
[
31
]
to prove existence (and uniqueness) of solutions of the corresponding mean-field PDE.

However, due to the particle selection principle implemented through the choice of quantile of the lower-level objective function
L
, our consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)
, as defined in (
2.1
),
is no longer stable w.r.t. Wasserstein perturbations of the measure
\varrho
. We demonstrate this through a counterexample in Remark
3.1
. Indeed, we show that
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}
cannot be Lipschitz continuous w.r.t. the Wasserstein distance (alone). In order to resolve this issue, we provide in Proposition
3.2
a new stability estimate for our consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}
by showing that
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}
is stable w.r.t. a combination of Wasserstein and
L^{2}
perturbations.

Remark 3.1

(Lack of stability of the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}
w.r.t. Wasserstein perturbations)
.

We show that there
cannot
exist a constant
c>0
such that

\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\widetilde{\varrho})}\right\|_{2}\leq cW_{2}\left(\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}\right),

(3.3)

for all
\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. For this two dimensional counterexample, consider the setting where the objective functions are
L(\theta)=\left\|{\theta}\right\|_{2}
and
G(\theta)=\left\|{\theta}\right\|_{2}
. Moreover, let us set the quantile hyperparameter
\beta
to be
\beta=0.3
. Consider measures
\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

as depicted in Figure
5
.
The mass of the measure
\varrho
is distributed equally over two concentric circles (depicted in gray in Figure
5(a)
) centered around the origin. The perturbed measure
\widetilde{\varrho}
is obtained by shifting a quarter of the total mass of the measure
\varrho
from the right side of the outer circle to the right by a distance of
s
(depicted in blue in Figure
5(b)
).
The
2
-Wasserstein distance between
\varrho
and
\widetilde{\varrho}
is clearly
W_{2}\left(\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}\right)=\frac{1}{2}s
. On the other hand, according to definition (
2.1
), the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\widetilde{\varrho})
(depicted as a purple dot in Figure
5(b)
) moves from
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)
(depicted as an orange dot in Figure
5(a)
and
5(b)
) to the left.
The distance between these two points is
\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\widetilde{\varrho})}\big\|_{2}=c_{\alpha}
, where
c_{\alpha}
is a constant that depends on the hyperparameter
\alpha
but not on
s
. More details on this example are provided in the caption of Figure
5
.

Therefore, in this case, there cannot exist a constant
c>0
such that (
3.3
) holds true.

(a)

Measure
\varrho
and consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\varrho})

(b)

Measure
\widetilde{\varrho}
and consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}({\color[rgb]{0,0.4453,0.7422}\widetilde{\varrho}})

Figure 5
:
An illustration that the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)
as defined in (
2.1
) cannot be stable w.r.t. Wasserstein perturbations of the measure
\varrho
.
For this counterexample, consider the setting where the objective functions are
L(\theta)=\left\|{\theta}\right\|_{2}
and
G(\theta)=\left\|{\theta}\right\|_{2}
.
Moreover, let us set the quantile hyperparameter
\beta=0.3
.
On the left, in Figure
5(a)
we depict the measure
\varrho
together with its corresponding consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)
.
The two concentric circles are level sets of the function
L
and
we assume that the mass of measure
\varrho
is equally split between the two circles and uniformly distributed over the circles.
By construction, these two level sets correspond to the function values
\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q_{a}^{L}da
and
\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q_{a}^{L}da+\delta_{q}
, respectively, where
q_{a}^{L}
is the same quantile value for
\varrho
or
\tilde{\varrho}
, since
a\leq\beta=0.3
. Consequently, if
R
is set to be large enough, the measure
I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
coincides with
\varrho
and thus, based on (
2.1
), the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)
lies at the center of the circles. On the right, in Figure
5(b)
, we construct the measure
\widetilde{\varrho}
and plot it together with its corresponding consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\widetilde{\varrho})
.
The perturbed measure
\widetilde{\varrho}
is obtained by shifting a quarter of the total mass of the measure
\varrho
from the right side of the outer circle to the right by a distance of
s
(depicted in blue in Figure
5(b)
).
By definition, the measure
I^{L}_{\beta}[\widetilde{\varrho}]
now includes the mass on the inner circle as well as the mass on the left side of the outer circle, but not the mass that has been shifted to the right.
Consequently, the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\widetilde{\varrho})
shifts to the left, as shown in Figure
5(b)
.
The size
c_{\alpha}
of this shift, however, is independent of
s
.

Proposition 3.2

(Stability estimate for the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}
)
.

Let
L\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
G\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfy
W1
–
W6
.
Moreover, let
\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(B_{R}(0))
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|{\theta}\right\|_{2}^{2}\varrho d\theta,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|{\theta}\right\|_{2}^{2}\widetilde{\varrho}d\theta\leq K
, and such that
\varrho(Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]),\widetilde{\varrho}(Q^{L}_{\beta}[\widetilde{\varrho}])\geq c
for constants
K<\infty
and
c>0
.
Then we have the stability estimate

\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\widetilde{\varrho})}\right\|_{2}\leq C\left(\|\varrho-\widetilde{\varrho}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0))}+W_{2}\left(\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}\right)\right),

(3.4)

where the constant
C
only depends on
R
,
K
,
c_{K}
as defined in Lemma
A.2
(which depends on
c
), and
\widetilde{C}
as derived in Lemma
3.4
.

Remark 3.3
.

By assuming that
\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}
satisfy
\varrho(Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]),\widetilde{\varrho}(Q^{L}_{\beta}[\widetilde{\varrho}])\geq c
, we in particular ensure that the consensus points
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)
,
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\widetilde{\varrho})
are well-defined.

Before providing the proof of Proposition
3.2
, we first state and prove an auxiliary lemma that we use in what follows.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
L\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfy
W1
–
W3
.
Then we have, for any bounded function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that
\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|{f(\theta)}\right\|_{2}\leq M
with constant
M>0
, and
\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(B_{R}(0))
, the inequality

\left\|{\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}f\varrho d\theta-\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\widetilde{\varrho}]}f\widetilde{\varrho}d\theta}\right\|_{2}\leq\widetilde{C}\left(\|\varrho-\widetilde{\varrho}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0))}+W_{2}\left(\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}\right)\right),

(3.5)

where the constant
\widetilde{C}
only depends on
R
,
M
,
\|\widetilde{\varrho}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R}(0))}
and
C_{L}
(i.e., the level set constant that we assumed for
L
).

Proof.

We can compute

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}f\varrho d\theta-\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\widetilde{\varrho}]}f\widetilde{\varrho}d\theta\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}f\left(\varrho-\widetilde{\varrho}\right)d\theta\right\|_{2}+\left\|\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}f\widetilde{\varrho}d\theta-\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\widetilde{\varrho}]}f\widetilde{\varrho}d\theta\right\|_{2}

(3.6)

\displaystyle=:T_{1}+T_{2},

where
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are defined implicitly.
For the term
T_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle T_{1}:=\left\|\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}f\left(\varrho-\widetilde{\varrho}\right)d\theta\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}\left\|f(\theta)\right\|_{2}^{2}d\theta\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}|\varrho(\theta)-\widetilde{\varrho}(\theta)|^{2}d\theta\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(3.7)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{B_{R}(0)}\left\|f(\theta)\right\|_{2}^{2}d\theta\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{B_{R}(0)}|\varrho(\theta)-\widetilde{\varrho}(\theta)|^{2}d\theta\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq M\mathrm{Vol}(B_{R}(0))^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\varrho-\widetilde{\varrho}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0))},

where the first inequality follows from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, the second inequality uses the fact that
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]\subseteq B_{R}(0)
by definition (
2.3
), and the last inequality comes from
\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|f(\theta)\|_{2}\leq M
.
For the second term
T_{2}
, without loss of generality, let us assume
\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\widetilde{\varrho}]da\leq\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]da
, and further denote

\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}[\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}]:=\left\{\theta\in B_{R}(0):\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\widetilde{\varrho}]da+\delta_{q}\leq L(\theta)\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]da+\delta_{q}\right\}.

(3.8)

Then, again by the definition of
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}]
in (
2.3
), we can compute

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle:=\left\|\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}f\widetilde{\varrho}d\theta-\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\widetilde{\varrho}]}f\widetilde{\varrho}d\theta\right\|_{2}

(3.9)

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{\{\theta\in B_{R}(0):\;L(\theta)\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]+\delta_{q}\}}f\widetilde{\varrho}d\theta-\int_{\{\theta\in B_{R}(0):\;L(\theta)\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\widetilde{\varrho}]+\delta_{q}\}}f\widetilde{\varrho}d\theta\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq M\left\|\widetilde{\varrho}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}[\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}]}1d\theta

\displaystyle\leq M\left\|\widetilde{\varrho}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}C_{L}\frac{2}{\beta}\left|\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]-\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\widetilde{\varrho}]\right|

\displaystyle\leq M\left\|\widetilde{\varrho}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}C_{\beta,L}C_{L}W_{2}\left(\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}\right).

Here the second-to-last inequality uses Assumption
W3
, and the last inequality employs Lemma
A.1
. We conclude the proof by combining the bounds for both terms
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
.
∎

With this auxiliary result, we can now prove Proposition
3.2
.

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Let
g(\theta;\varrho):={\theta\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta)}/{\left\|\omega_{\alpha}^{G}\right\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho])}}
, where
\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})
and
I^{L}_{\beta}[\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}]
are defined as in (
2.1
) and (
2.2
), respectively. From the definition of the consensus point in (
2.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\widetilde{\varrho})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}g(\theta;\varrho)\varrho d\theta-\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\widetilde{\varrho}]}g(\theta;\widetilde{\varrho})\widetilde{\varrho}d\theta\right\|_{2}

(3.10)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}\left(g(\theta;\varrho)-g(\theta;\widetilde{\varrho})\right)\varrho d\theta\right\|_{2}+\left\|\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}g(\theta;\widetilde{\varrho})\varrho d\theta-\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\widetilde{\varrho}]}g(\theta;\widetilde{\varrho})\widetilde{\varrho}d\theta\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=:T_{1}+T_{2}.

For the term
T_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}\left(g(\theta;\varrho)-g(\theta;\widetilde{\varrho})\right)\varrho d\theta\right\|_{2}

(3.11)

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}\theta\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta)\left(\frac{1}{\|\omega_{\alpha}^{G}\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho])}}-\frac{1}{\|\omega_{\alpha}^{G}\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\widetilde{\varrho}])}}\right)\varrho d\theta\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}\|\theta\|_{2}\frac{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta)}{\|\omega_{\alpha}^{G}\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho])}\|\omega_{\alpha}^{G}\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\widetilde{\varrho}])}}\varrho d\theta\left|\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta^{\prime})\varrho d\theta^{\prime}-\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\widetilde{\varrho}]}\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta^{\prime})\widetilde{\varrho}d\theta^{\prime}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}\|\theta\|_{2}\frac{\exp\left(-2\alpha\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{G}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{G}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}G\right)}{\|\omega_{\alpha}^{G}\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho])}\|\omega_{\alpha}^{G}\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\widetilde{\varrho}])}}\varrho d\theta\widetilde{C}\left(\|\varrho-\widetilde{\varrho}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0))}+W_{2}\left(\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq c_{K}^{2}\widetilde{C}\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}\|\theta\|_{2}\varrho d\theta\left(\|\varrho-\widetilde{\varrho}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0))}+W_{2}\left(\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{K}c_{K}^{2}\widetilde{C}\left(\|\varrho-\widetilde{\varrho}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0))}+W_{2}\left(\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}\right)\right).

Here, the second inequality comes from
\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta)\right|\leq\exp(-\alpha\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{G}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{G}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}G)
and Lemma
3.4
.
The third inequality uses Lemma
A.2
in combination with the assumption
\varrho(Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]),\widetilde{\varrho}(Q^{L}_{\beta}[\widetilde{\varrho}])\geq c>0
and the last inequality holds thanks to Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the assumption
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}\varrho d\theta\leq K
.

Regarding term
T_{2}
, by Lemma
A.2
we have

\sup_{\theta\in B_{R}(0)}\left\|g(\theta;\widetilde{\varrho})\right\|_{2}=\sup_{\theta\in B_{R}(0)}\left\|\frac{\theta\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta)}{\left\|\omega_{\alpha}^{G}\right\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\widetilde{\varrho}])}}\right\|_{2}\leq Rc_{K}.

(3.12)

Applying Lemma
3.4
again yields

\begin{split}T_{2}&:=\left\|\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}g(\theta;\widetilde{\varrho})\varrho d\theta-\int_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\widetilde{\varrho}]}g(\theta;\widetilde{\varrho})\widetilde{\varrho}d\theta\right\|_{2}\\
&\leq Rc_{K}\widetilde{C}\left(\|\varrho-\widetilde{\varrho}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0))}+W_{2}\left(\varrho,\widetilde{\varrho}\right)\right).\end{split}

(3.13)

We conclude the proof by combining the estimates for both terms
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
.
∎

3.3
Proof of Theorem
2.4

Before we present the main proof, let us first recall some preliminary results from
[
54
]
, which studies the regularity of weak solutions to the following type of linear PDEs with diffusion coefficients that may not satisfy a uniform ellipticity condition:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot\left(F_{t}\nabla\mu_{t}\right)-\nabla\cdot\left(J_{t}\mu_{t}\right)+A\mu_{t},

(3.14)

\displaystyle\mu_{0}

\displaystyle=\varrho\in H^{l+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad\forall l\geq 0\,.

Here,
F:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a scalar function,
J:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a vector valued function, and
A
is a constant.
Next, we define a class of test functions determined by
F
,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{F}:=\bigg\{\phi:

\displaystyle\forall T>0,\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{2,1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}),\phi\in W^{1,\infty}([0,T];H^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),

(3.15)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{t}^{2}\|\phi^{(l)}\|^{2}d\theta<\infty,\forall t\in[0,T],l\geq 0,\,\text{and}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{t}^{3}\|\phi^{(l)}\|^{2}d\theta dt<\infty,\forall l\geq 0\bigg\},

which, in turn, we use to introduce a notion of weak solution to (
3.14
).

Definition 3.5

(Definition 2.4 in
[
54
]
)
.

We say
\mu
is a weak solution to equation (
3.14
) if for every
t\in[0,\infty)

\mu_{t}
is a Radon measure and for any test function
\phi\in\Gamma_{F}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{\tau}(\theta)+\nabla\cdot\left(F_{\tau}(\theta)\nabla\phi_{\tau}(\theta)\right)-J_{\tau}(\theta)\cdot\nabla\phi_{\tau}(\theta)+A\phi_{\tau}(\theta)\right)\mu_{\tau}(\theta)d\theta d\tau

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu_{t}(\theta)\phi_{t}(\theta)d\theta-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho(\theta)\phi_{0}(\theta)d\theta,

for any
t\in[0,\infty)
.

Lastly, we state one of the main theorems in
[
54
]
, which is an important preliminary result used in our proof of Theorem
2.4
.

Proposition 3.6

(Theorem 2.5 in
[
54
]
)
.

If Assumptions 2.6, 2.7, and 2.17 in
[
54
]
are satisfied, then equation (
3.14
) has a unique weak solution in the sense of Definition
3.5
, and this weak solution belongs to the function class
\Gamma_{F}
defined in (
3.15
).

We now have all the necessary tools at hand to present a detailed proof of the existence of solutions to the mean-field CB
2
O dynamics (
1.13
).
For notational convenience, we abbreviate the consensus point (
2.1
) by
m(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})=m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})
.

Proof of Theorem
2.4
.

First note that if
\rho=\mathrm{Law}(\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{\theta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{\theta}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}\theta)
is a solution to the Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
) satisfying
\rho\in\mathcal{C}\left([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
, then we may apply standard theory of SDEs (see, e.g., Chapter 6.2 of
[
9
]
) to conclude that there exists a unique strong solution to the mean-field dynamics (
1.12
).
Therefore, in what follows we focus on proving that there exists a solution to the PDE (
1.13
) with the desired regularity.

Step 1:

Let
\rho^{0}_{t}=\rho_{0}
for all
t>0
, i.e.,
\rho^{n=0}_{t}(\theta)=\rho_{0}(\theta)
for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and for all
t>0
.
For
n\geq 0
,
let
\rho^{n+1}
be the solution to the following linear Fokker-Planck equation

\partial_{t}\rho^{n+1}_{t}=\lambda\nabla\cdot\left(\left(\theta-m(\rho^{n}_{t})\right)\rho^{n+1}_{t}\right)+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\Delta\left(\left\|{\theta-m(\rho^{n}_{t})}\right\|_{2}^{2}\rho^{n+1}_{t}\right)

(3.16)

with initial data
\rho^{n+1}_{0}=\rho_{0}\in H^{l+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathcal{P}_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Before we make the induction assumptions on
\rho^{n}
, we first rewrite (
3.16
) in the form of (
3.14
), i.e.,

\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{n+1}=\nabla\cdot\left(F_{t}^{n}\nabla\rho_{t}^{n+1}\right)-\nabla\cdot\left(J_{t}^{n}\rho_{t}^{n+1}\right)+A\rho_{t}^{n+1},

(3.17)

where, for
t\in[0,T]
,

F_{t}^{n}(\theta):=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\|\theta-m(\rho_{t}^{n})\|_{2}^{2},\quad\quad J_{t}^{n}(\theta):=(\lambda+\sigma^{2})(\theta-m(\rho_{t}^{n})),\quad A:=d(\lambda+\sigma^{2})\,.

(3.18)

Now we make the following induction assumptions on
\rho^{n}
: (for simplicity, we use
C,c>0
to denote constants that are
independent
of
n
)

(i)

\rho_{t}^{n}(Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{n}])\geq c
with a constant
c>0
that is independent of
n
or
t\in[0,T]
.
This ensures that
\{m(\rho_{t}^{n})\}_{t\in[0,T]}
is well-defined;

(ii)

\rho^{n}\in\Gamma_{F^{n-1}}\cap L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\Gamma_{F^{n-1}}
defined in (
3.15
) based on function
F^{n-1}
introduced in (
3.18
).
Moreover,
\rho^{n}
satisfies

\|\rho^{n}\|_{W^{1,\infty}([0,T];H^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leq C\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{l+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\quad\text{and}\quad\|\rho^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})};

(iii)

\rho^{n}\in\mathcal{C}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\theta\|^{4}d\rho_{t}^{n}(\theta)\leq C
;

(iv)

\rho^{n}
satisfies
\|m(\rho_{t}^{n})-m(\rho_{s}^{n})\|_{2}\leq C|t-s|^{1/2}
for any
0\leq s,t\leq T
.

We verify that
\rho^{n+1}
satisfies the induction assumptions
(i)
-
(iv)
.

For the induction assumption
(i)
, we first notice that, by the definition of
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}]
in (
2.3
) and Assumption
A2
,

\displaystyle\rho_{t}^{n+1}\left(Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{n+1}]\right)

\displaystyle=\rho_{t}^{n+1}\left(\left\{\theta\in B_{R}(0):L(\theta)\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\rho_{t}^{n+1}]\,da+\delta_{q}\right\}\right)

(3.19)

\displaystyle\geq\rho_{t}^{n+1}\left(\left\{\theta\in B_{R}(0):L(\theta)\leq\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L+\delta_{q}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\rho_{t}^{n+1}\left(B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right),\qquad\qquad\text{with}\;\;r_{q}=\left(\frac{\delta_{q}}{H_{L}}\right)^{1/h_{L}}.

Here the first inequality holds because
\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]\,da\geq\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L
for all
\beta\in[0,1]
, and the second inequality uses the fact that
L(\theta)\leq\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L+\delta_{q}
for all
\theta\in B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
.
Moreover, following the argument described in the proof of Proposition
4.4
, we can infer that the amount of mass in the ball
B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
w.r.t.
\rho^{n+1}
will decrease at most exponentially fast, i.e.,

\rho_{t}^{n+1}\left(B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right)\geq\left(\int\phi_{r_{q}}(\theta)d\rho_{0}^{n+1}(\theta)\right)\exp\left(-pt\right)=\left(\int\phi_{r_{q}}(\theta)d\rho_{0}(\theta)\right)\exp\left(-pt\right),\qquad\text{for}\;\;t\in[0,T],

(3.20)

where
\phi_{r_{q}}
and
p
are as in (
4.33
) and (
4.34
), respectively; we note that this
p
can be chosen to depend only on hyperparameters of our model and not on
t\in[0,T]
or
n
. Because
\rho_{0}
satisfies that
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\in\operatorname{supp}(\rho_{0})
, in other words,
\int\phi_{r_{q}}(\theta)d\rho_{0}(\theta)>0
, we can thus deduce that

\rho_{t}^{n+1}\big(Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{n+1}]\big)\geq c

(3.21)

for a constant
c>0
that is
independent
of
n
or
t\in[0,T]
by combining the inequalities (
3.19
) and (
3.20
).
Furthermore, recall that
m(\rho_{t}^{n+1})
was introduced as

m(\rho_{t}^{n+1})=\int\theta\frac{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta)}{\left\|\omega_{\alpha}^{G}\right\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{n+1}])}}dI^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{n+1}](\theta),

(3.22)

with
I^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{n+1}]:=\mathbbm{1}_{Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{n+1}]}\rho_{t}^{n+1}
as defined in (
2.2
). Therefore,
\rho_{t}^{n+1}(Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{n+1}])>0
ensures that the above formula is well-defined.
This shows that
\rho^{n+1}
satisfies the induction assumption
(i)
.

Next, we check that the induction assumption
(ii)
holds for
\rho_{t}^{n+1}
.
To apply Proposition
3.6
, we need to check that
F_{t}^{n}
and
J_{t}^{n}
, as defined in (
3.18
), satisfy the required assumptions.
Among them, we verify that
F_{t}^{n}(\theta)
and
J_{t}^{n}(\theta)
are locally Hölder continuous in time
t
with Hölder coefficient
\gamma\in[0,1]
for any fixed
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The remaining required assumptions hold for
F_{t}^{n}(\theta)
and
J_{t}^{n}(\theta)
following similar arguments as in
[
54
, Remark 2.18]
. The Hölder continuity follows from the direct computation:

\displaystyle|F_{t}^{n}(\theta)-F_{s}^{n}(\theta)|

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\left|\|\theta-m(\rho_{t}^{n})\|_{2}-\|\theta-m(\rho_{s}^{n})\|_{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\left\|2\theta+m(\rho_{t}^{n})+m(\rho_{s}^{n})\right\|_{2}\|m(\rho_{t}^{n})-m(\rho_{s}^{n})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\sigma^{2}(\sqrt{2}\|\theta\|_{2}+R)C|t-s|^{1/2}\,;

in the last inequality, we applied Young’s inequality, the fact that
m(\rho_{t}^{n}),m(\rho_{s}^{n})\in B_{R}(0)
, and the induction assumption
(iv)
for
\rho^{n}
.
With similar computations, we can also show that
J_{t}^{n}(\theta)
is locally
\frac{1}{2}
-Hölder continuous for any fixed
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Thus, by Proposition
3.6
, we know that
\rho_{t}^{n+1}
is well defined as the unique solution to the PDE (
3.17
) and satisfies the regularity conditions in the definition of
\Gamma_{F^{n}}
.
With similar computations as in the proof of
[
54
, Proposition
3.6
]
, we can further show that

\|\rho^{n+1}\|_{W^{1,\infty}([0,T];H^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leq C(T,l,\lambda,\sigma)\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{l+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,.

(3.23)

Next, we show that
\rho_{t}^{n+1}\in L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Since we know that
\rho_{t}^{n+1}\in\Gamma_{F^{n}}
, it follows that for every
2\leq p<\infty
we can use
(p_{t}^{n+1})^{p-1}
as a test function for the linear Fokker-Planck (
3.17
) and get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\frac{d}{dt}\|\rho_{t}^{n+1}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{n+1}(\rho_{t}^{n+1})^{p-1}d\theta

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\nabla\cdot\left(F_{t}^{n}(\theta)\nabla(\rho_{t}^{n+1})^{p-1}\right)-\nabla\cdot(J_{t}^{n}(\theta)(\rho_{t}^{n+1})^{p-1})+A(\rho_{t}^{n+1})^{p-1}\right)\rho_{t}^{n+1}d\theta

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{t}^{n}(\theta)\left(\nabla(\rho_{t}^{n+1})^{p-1}\right)^{T}\nabla\rho_{t}^{n+1}d\theta

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}J_{t}^{n}(\theta)^{T}\nabla\rho_{t}^{n+1}(\rho_{t}^{n+1})^{p-1}d\theta+A\|\rho_{t}^{n+1}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}

\displaystyle=-(p-1)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{t}^{n}(\theta)\|\nabla\rho_{t}^{n+1}\|^{2}_{2}(\rho_{t}^{n+1})^{p-2}d\theta

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}J_{t}^{n}(\theta)^{T}\nabla(\rho_{t}^{n+1})^{p}d\theta+A\|\rho_{t}^{n+1}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1}{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\cdot J_{t}^{n}(\theta)(\rho_{t}^{n+1})^{p}d\theta+A\|\rho_{t}^{n+1}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{p-1}{p}d(\lambda+\sigma^{2})\|\rho_{t}^{n+1}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\,.

Here, the third equality comes from integration by parts, the first inequality uses the fact that
F_{t}^{n}(\theta)=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\|\theta-m(\rho_{t}^{n})\|^{2}_{2}\geq 0
and integration by parts, and in the last equality we simply plug in the definitions of
J_{t}^{n}
and
A
as introduced in (
3.17
). Using Grönwall’s inequality, we obtain

\left\|\rho_{t}^{n+1}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\exp\left(\frac{p-1}{p}d(\lambda+\sigma^{2})t\right)\left\|\rho_{0}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Thus, for any
t\in[0,T]
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\rho_{t}^{n+1}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\lim_{p\rightarrow\infty}\left\|\rho_{t}^{n+1}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{p\rightarrow\infty}\exp\left(\frac{p-1}{p}d(\lambda+\sigma^{2})t\right)\left\|\rho_{0}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(d(\lambda+\sigma^{2})t\right)\left\|\rho_{0}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(d(\lambda+\sigma^{2})T\right)\left\|\rho_{0}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Form the above we conclude
\rho^{n+1}\in L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, showing that
\rho^{n+1}
satisfies the induction assumption
(ii)
.

To verify the induction assumption
(iii)
for
\rho_{t}^{n+1}
, we denote the SDE corresponding to the linear Fokker-Planck Equation (
3.16
) as

d\theta_{t}^{n+1}=-\lambda\left(\theta_{t}-m(\rho_{t}^{n})\right)dt+\sigma\left\|{\theta_{t}^{n+1}-m(\rho_{t}^{n})}\right\|_{2}dB_{t}.

(3.24)

Note that by the definition of
m(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})
in (
2.1
),
\left\|{m(\rho_{t}^{n})}\right\|_{2}\leq R
for all
t\in[0,T]
.
Therefore, by standard theory of SDEs (see, e.g., Chapter 7 in
[
9
]
), a fourth-order moment estimate for solutions to (
3.24
) takes the form

\mathbb{E}\left\|{\theta_{t}^{n+1}}\right\|_{2}^{4}\leq\left(1+\mathbb{E}\left\|{\theta_{0}^{n+1}}\right\|_{2}^{4}\right)e^{at},

(3.25)

for some constant
a>0
(independent of
n
or
t
). In particular,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|{\theta_{t}^{n+1}}\right\|_{2}^{4}d\rho_{t}^{n+1}\leq K

(3.26)

for a constant
K<\infty
that is
independent
of
n
. This shows
\rho^{n+1}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
t\in[0,T]
.
Furthermore, from the SDE (
3.24
), the Itô isometry yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\|\theta_{t}^{n+1}-\theta_{s}^{n+1}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda^{2}|t-s|\int_{s}^{t}\left\|\theta_{\tau}^{n+1}-m(\rho_{\tau}^{n})\right\|_{2}^{2}d\tau+2\sigma^{2}\int_{s}^{t}\left\|\theta_{\tau}^{n+1}-m(\rho_{\tau}^{n})\right\|_{2}^{2}d\tau

(3.27)

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\lambda^{2}T+\sigma^{2}\right)\left(K+R^{2}\right)|t-s|

\displaystyle=:b|t-s|,

and, therefore,
W_{2}\left(\rho_{t}^{n+1},\rho_{s}^{n+1}\right)\leq b|t-s|^{\frac{1}{2}}
, for some constant
b>0
that is
independent
of
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
This implies that
\rho_{t}^{n+1}\in\mathcal{C}\left([0,T],\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)
, and shows that
\rho_{t}^{n+1}
fulfills the induction assumption
(iii)
.

Lastly, we verify that
\rho^{n+1}
also meets the induction assumption
(iv)
.
For any
0\leq s<t\leq T
, from the above analysis, we know that
\rho_{s}^{n+1},\rho_{t}^{n+1}
satisfy the assumption requirements for the stability estimate for the consensus point
m(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})
stated in Proposition
3.2
.
In particular,

\displaystyle\left\|m(\rho_{t}^{n+1})-m(\rho_{s}^{n+1})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{C}\left(W_{2}\left(\rho_{t}^{n+1},\rho_{s}^{n+1}\right)+\|\rho_{t}^{n+1}-\rho_{s}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0))}\right),

(3.28)

where the constant
\widetilde{C}>0
is independent of
n
.

Moreover, we can compute

\displaystyle\|\rho_{t}^{n+1}-\rho_{s}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0))}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{B_{R}(0)}|\rho_{t}^{n+1}(\theta)-\rho_{s}^{n+1}(\theta)|^{2}d\theta\right)^{1/2}

(3.29)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{B_{R}(0)}\left|\int_{s}^{t}\partial_{\tau}\rho_{\tau}^{n+1}(\theta)d\tau\right|^{2}d\theta\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\partial_{\tau}\rho_{\tau}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}([s,t]\times B_{R}(0))}|t-s|^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\rho^{n+1}\|_{W^{1,\infty}([0,T];H^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}|t-s|^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C(T,l,\lambda,\sigma)\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{l+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}|t-s|^{1/2}\,.

Here, in the first inequality we apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and the last inequality makes use of inequality (
3.23
).
Integrating the above estimate and
W_{2}\left(\rho_{t}^{n+1},\rho_{s}^{n+1}\right)\leq b|t-s|^{\frac{1}{2}}
, derived in (
3.27
), into (
3.28
), we obtain that

\left\|m(\rho_{t}^{n+1})-m(\rho_{s}^{n+1})\right\|_{2}\leq C|t-s|^{1/2}

for a constant
C>0
that is independent of
n
.
With this we conclude that
\rho^{n+1}
indeed satisfies the induction assumption
(iv)
.

Step 2:

From Step 1, we have shown that the sequence
\{\rho^{n}\}_{n}\subset W^{1,\infty}([0,T];H^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
has a uniformly bounded (i.e., independent of
n
) norm in this space.
Consequently, for any fixed
r>0
, we know that
\{\rho^{n}\}_{n}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{1}(B_{r}(0)))
and
\{\partial_{t}\rho^{n}\}_{n}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{-1}(B_{r}(0)))
(since
H^{1}({B_{r}(0)})\subset H^{-1}(B_{r}(0))
).
With the further facts
H^{1}(B_{r}(0))\subset\subset L^{2}(B_{r}(0))
(compactly embedded) and
L^{2}(B_{r}(0))\hookrightarrow H^{-1}(B_{r}(0))
(continuously embedded), we can use the Aubin-Lions lemma
[
10
,
75
]
to conclude that there exists a subsequence
\rho^{n_{k}(1)}
and a function
g_{r}\in\mathcal{C}\left([0,T],L^{2}(B_{r}(0))\right)
such that

\rho^{n_{k}(1)}\rightarrow g_{r}\;\text{in}\;\mathcal{C}\left([0,T],L^{2}(B_{r}(0))\right)\quad\text{as}\;\;k\rightarrow\infty.

(3.30)

Repeating the above argument, from the subsequence
\{\rho^{n_{k}(1)}\}
we can extract a further subsequence
\{\rho^{n_{k}(2)}\}
converging to an element
g_{2r}
in
\mathcal{C}\left([0,T],L^{2}(B_{2r}(0))\right)
. We note that it must be the case that
g_{2r}(t,\theta)=g_{r}(t,\theta)
for all
(t,\theta)\in[0,T]\times B_{r}(0)
.
Proceeding in an inductive fashion, we can create a subsequence
\rho^{n_{k}(j)}
of the sequence
\rho^{n_{k}(j-1)}
such that
\rho^{n_{k}(j)}\rightarrow g_{jr}
in
\mathcal{C}\left([0,T],L^{2}(B_{jr}(0))\right)
; this construction ensures that
\rho^{n_{k}(j)}
converges to
g_{hr}
in
\mathcal{C}\left([0,T],L^{2}(B_{hr}(0))\right)
for all
h\leq j
.
Moreover,
g_{jr}(t,\theta)=g_{hr}(t,\theta)
for
(t,\theta)\in[0,T]\times B_{hr}(0)
for every
h\leq j
.
Taking
n_{k}:=n_{k}(k)
and defining
\rho\in\mathcal{C}\left([0,T];L^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)
as
\rho(t,\theta)=g_{jr}(t,\theta)
for all
(t,\theta)\in[0,T]\times B_{jr}(0)
and
j\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, we then have

\rho^{n_{k}}\rightarrow\rho|_{B_{jr}(0)}\;\text{in}\;\mathcal{C}\left([0,T],L^{2}(B_{jr}(0))\right)\quad\text{as}\;\;k\rightarrow\infty,

(3.31)

for all
j\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
.
This implies that

\rho^{n_{k}}\rightarrow\rho\,\text{ strongly in }\,\mathcal{C}([0,T],L_{\text{loc}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\,.

(3.32)

Next, we show that the limiting function
\rho
satisfies the following properties (similar to the induction assumptions
(i)
-
(iv)
):

(I)

\rho(Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}])\geq c
for all
t\in[0,T]
with constant
c>0
defined in (
3.21
).
This ensures that
\{m(\rho_{t})\}_{t\in[0,T]}
is well-defined;

(II)

\rho
satisfies:

\rho\in\mathcal{C}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap W^{1,\infty}([0,T];H^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}));

(III)

\rho\in\mathcal{C}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\theta\|^{4}d\rho(\theta_{t})\leq K
with a constant
K>0
defined in (
3.26
);

(IV)

\rho
satisfies
\|m(\rho_{t})-m(\rho_{s})\|_{2}\leq C|t-s|^{1/2}
for any
0\leq s,t\leq T
.

For property
(I)
, with similar calculations as in (
3.19
), we know that
\rho_{t}(Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}])\geq\rho_{t}(B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}))
, where
r_{q}
is defined in (
3.19
).
Furthermore, we can compute that

\displaystyle\rho_{t}\left(B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right)

\displaystyle\geq\rho_{t}^{n_{k}}\left(B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right)-\left|\rho_{t}^{n_{k}}\left(B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right)-\rho_{t}\left(B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right)\right|

(3.33)

\displaystyle\geq\rho_{t}^{n_{k}}\left(B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right)-\left(\mathrm{Vol}\left(B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\rho_{t}^{n_{k}}-\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}(B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}))}

\displaystyle\geq c-\left(\mathrm{Vol}\left(B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\rho_{t}^{n_{k}}-\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}(B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}))}\,.

Here the first inequality uses the inverse triangle inequality, the second inequality is an application of Cauchy-Schwarz, and the last inequality utilizes the fact that
\rho_{t}^{n_{k}}(B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}))\geq c
for all
k\geq 0
and
t\in[0,T]
as shown in (
3.21
).
By letting
k\rightarrow\infty
in (
3.33
) and using
L^{2}_{\mathrm{Loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
convergence property (
3.32
) with
\Omega=B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
, we verify that
\rho_{t}(B_{r_{q}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}))\geq c
with a constant
c>0
for all
t\in[0,T]
.
This implies that
\rho_{t}(Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}])\geq c
, and further indicates that
m(\rho_{t})
is well-defined for all
t\in[0,T]
.

To verify that
\rho
satisfies property
(II)
, we make use of the fact that
\rho^{n_{k}}\rightarrow\rho
strongly in
\mathcal{C}([0,T];L_{\text{loc}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, and
\{\rho^{n_{k}}\}_{n_{k}}\subset W^{1,\infty}([0,T];H^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
has uniform bounded norms in these two spaces, respectively.
The proof follows from standard functional analysis arguments, and we defer the details to Lemma
A.6
and Lemma
A.7
in the Appendix.

For the property
(III)
of
\rho
, since for all
t\in[0,T]
we have that
\rho_{t}^{n_{k}}
strongly converges to
\rho_{t}
in
L_{\text{loc}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
n\rightarrow\infty
and
\sup_{n}\int\|\theta\|_{2}^{4}d\rho^{n}_{t}(\theta)\leq K
with a constant
K
defined in (
3.26
), we can show that
\rho^{n_{k}}_{t}
converges to
\rho_{t}
in
W_{2}
-distance and moreover
\int\|\theta\|_{2}^{4}d\rho_{t}(\theta)\leq\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int\|\theta\|^{4}_{2}d\rho_{t}^{n_{k}}\leq K
.
We defer the details of these facts to Lemma
A.9
and Lemma
A.10
in the Appendix. Then, by
\rho^{n_{k}}\in\mathcal{C}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and triangle inequality, we have, for any
0\leq s,t\leq T
,

\lim_{t\rightarrow s}W_{2}(\rho_{t},\rho_{s})\leq\lim_{t\rightarrow s}W_{2}(\rho_{t},\rho^{n_{k}}_{t})+W_{2}(\rho^{n_{k}}_{t},\rho^{n_{k}}_{s})+W_{2}(\rho^{n_{k}}_{s},\rho_{s})\rightarrow 0\qquad\text{as}\;k\rightarrow+\infty\,.

Thus,
\rho\in\mathcal{C}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, and we finish verifying property
(III)
of
\rho
.

To justify that
\rho
has property
(IV)
, note that for any
0\leq s,t\leq T
,
\rho_{s}
we can compute that

\displaystyle\|m(\rho_{t})-m(\rho_{s})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{3}\left(\|m(\rho_{t})-m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})\|_{2}+\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})-m(\rho_{s}^{n_{k}})\|_{2}+\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{s}})-m(\rho_{s})\|_{2}\right)

(3.34)

\displaystyle\leq C|t-s|^{1/2}+\sqrt{3}\left(\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})-m(\rho_{t})\|_{2}+\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{s}})-m(\rho_{s})\|_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C|t-s|^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\widetilde{C}\left(\|\rho_{t}^{n_{k}}-\rho_{t}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0))}+W_{2}(\rho_{t},\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})+\|\rho_{s}^{n_{k}}-\rho_{s}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0))}+W_{2}(\rho_{s},\rho_{s}^{n_{k}})\right)\,.

Here the first inequality applies Young’s inequality, the second inequality applies the induction property
(iv)
of
\rho^{n_{k}}
, and the last inequality makes use of the stability estimate of the consensus point in Proposition
3.2
.
We further note that the constants
C,\widetilde{C}>0
are
independent
from
n_{k}
as shown in the induction properties
(i)
-
(iv)
.
Then by letting
k\rightarrow\infty
and noticing that
\rho^{n_{k}}
strongly converges to
\rho
in
\mathcal{C}([0,T];L_{\text{loc}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, and
\rho_{\tau}^{n_{k}}
converges to
\rho_{\tau}
in
W_{2}
for all
\tau\in[0,T]
, we conclude that
\rho
satisfies the property
(IV)
.

Step 3:

Next we show that the limiting function
\rho
solves the Equation (
2.5
) in the sense of Definition
2.2
.

First of all, we verify that for any fixed
r>0
,

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\left\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})\rho_{t}^{n_{k+1}}-m(\rho_{t})\rho_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}d\theta dt\rightarrow 0\quad\text{as}\;\;k\rightarrow\infty,

(3.35a)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\left|\left\|\theta-m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})\right\|_{2}^{2}\rho_{t}^{n_{k+1}}-\left\|\theta-m(\rho_{t})\right\|_{2}^{2}\rho_{t}\right|^{2}d\theta dt\rightarrow 0\quad\text{as}\;\;k\rightarrow\infty\;.

(3.35b)

For term (
3.35a
), one can compute

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\left\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})\rho_{t}^{n_{k+1}}-m(\rho_{t})\rho_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}d\theta dt

(3.36)

\displaystyle\leq 2\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\left\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})-m(\rho_{t})\right\|_{2}^{2}\rho_{t}^{2}d\theta dt+2\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\left\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})\right\|_{2}^{2}\left|\rho_{t}^{n_{k+1}}-\rho_{t}\right|^{2}d\theta dt

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{0}^{T}\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})-m(\rho_{t})\right\|_{2}^{2}\left(\int_{B_{r}(0)}\rho_{t}^{2}d\theta\right)dt+2R^{2}\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\|\rho^{n_{k+1}}-\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T]\times B_{r}(0))}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(\left\|\rho_{t}^{n_{k}}-\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0))}+W_{2}\left(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}},\rho_{t}\right)\right)\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{r}(0))}dt

\displaystyle+2R^{2}\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\|\rho^{n_{k+1}}-\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T]\times B_{r}(0))}

\displaystyle=0.

The second inequality comes from the fact that
\left\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})-m(\rho_{t})\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq 4R^{2}
and the dominated convergence theorem. The third inequality uses the stability estimate of consensus point in Proposition
3.2
with the fact that
\varrho_{t}^{n_{k}},\rho_{t}\in L_{\text{loc}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\rho_{t}^{n_{k}}(Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}^{n_{k}}]),\rho_{t}(Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}])\geq c>0
for all
t\in[0,T]
and all
k
.
The last equality uses (
3.32
) and Lemma
A.9
, and (
3.31
), i.e.

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(\left\|\rho_{t}^{n_{k}}-\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0))}+W_{2}\left(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}},\rho_{t}\right)\right)=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\|\rho^{n_{k+1}}-\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T]\times B_{r}(0))}=0.

(3.37)

For term (
3.35b
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\left|\left\|\theta-m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})\right\|_{2}^{2}\rho_{t}^{n_{k+1}}-\left\|\theta-m(\rho_{t})\right\|_{2}^{2}\rho_{t}\right|^{2}d\theta dt

(3.38)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\left|\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}\left(\rho_{t}^{n_{k+1}}\!-\!\rho_{t}\right)\!-\!2\theta^{T}\left(m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})\rho_{t}^{n_{k+1}}\!-\!m(\rho_{t})\rho_{t}\right)\!+\!\left(\left\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})\right\|_{2}^{2}\rho_{t}^{n_{k+1}}\!-\!\left\|m(\rho_{t})\right\|_{2}^{2}\rho_{t}\right)\right|^{2}d\theta dt

\displaystyle\lesssim r^{4}\left\|\rho^{n_{k+1}}-\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T]\times B_{r}(0))}+r^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\left\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})\rho_{t}^{n_{k+1}}-m(\rho_{t})\rho_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}d\theta dt

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\left|\left\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})\right\|_{2}^{2}\rho_{t}^{n_{k+1}}-\left\|m(\rho_{t})\right\|_{2}^{2}\rho_{t}\right|^{2}d\theta dt.

In the above inequality, the first two terms converge to
0
as
k\rightarrow\infty
by (
3.31
) and (
3.35a
).
For the last term, we can compute that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\left|\left\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})\right\|_{2}^{2}\rho_{t}^{n_{k+1}}-\left\|m(\rho_{t})\right\|_{2}^{2}\rho_{t}\right|^{2}d\theta dt

(3.39)

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\left|\left\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})\right\|_{2}^{2}-\left\|m(\rho_{t})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right\|_{2}^{2}\rho_{t}^{2}d\theta dt+2\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\left\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})\right\|_{2}^{4}\left\|\rho_{t}^{n_{k+1}}-\rho_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}d\theta dt

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\left|\left(m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})+m(\rho_{t})\right)^{T}\left(m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})-m(\rho_{t})\right)\right|^{2}\rho_{t}^{2}d\theta dt+2R^{4}\left\|\rho^{n_{k+1}}-\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T]\times B_{r}(0))}

\displaystyle\leq 8R^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\left\|m(\rho_{t}^{n_{k}})-m(\rho_{t})\right\|_{2}^{2}\rho_{t}^{2}d\theta dt+2R^{4}\left\|\rho^{n_{k+1}}-\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T]\times B_{r}(0))}

\displaystyle\rightarrow 0\text{ as }k\rightarrow\infty.\quad(\text{by \eqref{eq:convInL2} and \eqref{eq:convTerm1Varify}})

Therefore, we have verified that (
3.35b
) holds.
Furthermore, we can easily show that for any test function
\phi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\rho^{n_{k+1}}(\theta,t)\phi(\theta)d\theta=\int_{B_{r}(0)}\rho(\theta,0)\phi(\theta)d\theta,

(3.40)

\lim_{t\rightarrow T}\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\rho^{n_{k+1}}(\theta,t)\phi(\theta)d\theta=\int_{B_{r}(0)}\rho(\theta,T)\phi(\theta)d\theta

(3.41)

and

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{r}(0)}\left\|\theta\rho_{t}^{n_{k+1}}-\theta\rho_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}d\theta dt\rightarrow 0\;\;\text{as}\;\;k\rightarrow\infty,

(3.42)

due to
\rho^{n_{k}}\in\mathcal{C}\left([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)
and (
3.31
).
Hence we have shown that the limiting function
\rho
is a solution to the Equation (
1.13
) in the weak sense (Definition
2.2
).
∎

4
Proof Details for Section
2.3

In this section, we provide the proof details for the global convergence result of CB
2
O in mean-field law, Theorem
2.7
.
Therefore, we present in Section
4.1
a proof sketch outlining the main ideas and giving an overview of the crucial steps.
Sections
4.2
–
4.4
then provide with Lemma
4.1
, Proposition
4.2
, and Proposition
4.4
the individual statements that are required and central in the overall proof of Theorem
2.7
, which we then conclude in Section
4.5
.

For the sake of convenience, we introduce the notation
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})=\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\rho_{t},\delta_{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}})
, i.e.,

\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})=\frac{1}{2}\int\left\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}^{2}d\rho_{t}(\theta).

(4.1)

The proof framework follows the one introduced in
[
51
,
50
,
83
,
84
]
.

4.1
Proof Sketch

In order to show the convergence of the mean-field CB
2
O dynamics (
1.13
) to the desired solution
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
of the bi-level optimization problem (
1.1
),
our eventual goal is to establish that a solution
\rho
of (
1.13
) approximates arbitrarily well a Dirac measure located at
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
w.r.t. the Wasserstein-
2
distance
W_{2}
.
More rigorously, we wish to show that for any
\varepsilon>0
, when choosing suitably the hyperparameters
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\delta_{q}
of the CB
2
O dynamics (
1.13
),
it holds

W_{2}^{2}(\rho_{T},\delta_{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}})=2\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T})\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{for}\quad T=T(\varepsilon)

(4.2)

sufficiently large.
Let us recall here that
R
has to be sufficiently large from the beginning fulfilling
R\geq\big\|{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}+r_{R}
for some
r_{R}>0
.

The main idea to achieve the former is to show that the functional
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
as defined in (
4.1
) satisfies for any
\vartheta\in(0,1)
differential inequalities of the form

\displaystyle-(1+\vartheta/2)\left(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\right)\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})\leq\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})\leq-(1-\vartheta)\left(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\right)\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})

(4.3)

until the time
T=T^{*}
or until
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T})\leq\varepsilon
.
In the case
T=T^{*}
, it is easy to check that the definition of
T^{*}
in (
2.16
) in Theorem
2.7
implies
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T^{*}})\leq\varepsilon
, thus giving
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T})\leq\varepsilon
in either case.

As a first step towards (
4.3
) we derive a differential inequality for the evolution of
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
by using the dynamics (
1.13
) of
\rho
.
More concisely, by using the weak form (
2.5
) of (
1.13
) with test function
\phi(\theta)=1/2\big\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}^{2}
,
we derive in Lemma
4.1
the upper bound

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})\leq

\displaystyle-\left(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\right)\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})+\sqrt{2}\left(\lambda+d\sigma^{2}\right)\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}\phantom{.}

(4.4)

\displaystyle+\frac{d\sigma^{2}}{2}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}^{2}

and analogously a lower bound on
\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
.

To find suitable bounds on the second and third term in (
4.4
), we need to control the quantity
\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}
through appropriate choices of the hyperparameters
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\delta_{q}
.
Before describing how to do this, let us recall that as of (
2.1
) we have

m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})=m^{G}_{\alpha}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}]).

(4.5)

Control on the discrepancy
\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}
is now obtained by the quantitative quantiled Laplace principle (Q
2
LP) developed in Proposition
4.2
.
It combines a quantile choice with the quantitative Laplace principle
[
51
, Proposition 4.5]
.
The former, i.e., the quantitative quantile choice, is associated with a suitably small choice of
\beta>0
, to be precise, such that

q_{\beta}[\varrho]+\delta_{q}\leq\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L+\min\{L_{\infty},(\eta_{L}r_{G})^{1/\nu_{L}}\}

(4.6)

holds for some small
r_{G}\in(0,R_{G}]
.
This warrants that, under the inverse continuity property
A3
on
L
w.r.t. the set
\Theta
of all global minimizers of
L
, the support of the measure
I^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}]
approximates well (by being contained in) a small local neighborhood around the set
\Theta
, denoted by
\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
.
With the inverse continuity property
A5
on
G
being valid on
\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
and thus on the support of
I^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}]
, we can imagine a decomposition (in the proof below, this decomposition will be more implicit, see Remark
4.3
) of the error as in (
2.22
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}&=\Big\|{m^{G}_{\alpha}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}])-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\Big\|_{2}\\
&\leq\left\|{m^{G}_{\alpha}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}])-\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}}\right\|_{2}+\left\|{\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}\\
&\leq\left\|{m^{G}_{\alpha}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}])-\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}}\right\|_{2}+r_{G},\end{aligned}

(4.7)

where
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}\in B_{r_{G}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
exists as of
A5
, thus giving the bound on the second term in (
4.7
).
For an illustration, we ask the reader to consult once more Figure
2
above and imagine the set
\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
to coincide with the support of
I^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}]
.
Since by assumption
A5
,
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
globally minimizes the objective function
G
restricted to
\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
and thus the support of
I^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}]
,
the quantitative Laplace principle can be employed to get control over the first term in (
4.7
).
This is since the Laplace principle, for a sufficiently large choice of
\alpha>0
, ensures that
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})=m^{G}_{\alpha}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}])
does approximate
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
.
More precisely, under the inverse continuity property
A5
on
G
, we can show

\displaystyle\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell(r_{G},r)+\frac{\exp\left(-\alpha r\right)}{\rho_{t}\big(B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)}

(4.8)

for sufficiently small
r>0
.
Here,
\ell
is a strictly positive but monotonically decreasing functions with
\ell(r_{G},r)\rightarrow 0
as
r_{G},r\rightarrow 0
.
As long as we can guarantee that
\rho_{t}(B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}))>0
,
we achieve
\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}\lesssim\ell(r_{G},r)
, which can be made arbitrarily small by suitable choices of
r\ll 1
as well as
r_{G}\ll 1
,
which in turn require sufficiently small choices of
\beta
and
\delta_{q}
, as well as a sufficiently large choice of
\alpha
.

It thus remains to ensure that
\rho_{t}(B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}))>0
for all
r>0
.
To do so, we employ
[
51
, Proposition 4.6]
, see also Proposition
4.4
below for a more detailed explanation,
showing that the initial mass
\rho_{0}(B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}))>0
can, thanks to an active Brownian motion term, i.e.,
\sigma>0
, decay at most at an exponential rate for any
r>0
, therefore remaining strictly positive during finite time
[0,T]
.

4.2
Time-Evolution of
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})

In this section,
we derive evolution inequalities from above and below for the functional
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
defined in (
4.1
).

Lemma 4.1

(Time-evolution of
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
, cf.
[
51
, Lemmas 4.1 and 4.3]
)
.

Let
L:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
G:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.
Fix
\alpha,\beta,R,\delta_{q},\lambda,\sigma>0
.
Moreover, let
T>0
and let
\rho\in\mathcal{C}([0,T],\mathcal{P}_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
be a weak solution to the Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
) in the sense of Definition
2.2
.
Then the functional
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
satisfies

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})\leq&-\left(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\right)\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})+\sqrt{2}\left(\lambda+d\sigma^{2}\right)\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}\phantom{.}\\
&+\frac{d\sigma^{2}}{2}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\end{split}

(4.9)

as well as

\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})\geq-\left(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\right)\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})-\sqrt{2}\left(\lambda+d\sigma^{2}\right)\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}.

(4.10)

Proof.

The proof follows the lines of
[
51
, Lemmas 4.1 and 4.2]
.

To start with, we recall that the function
\theta\mapsto\phi(\theta):=1/2\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\|_{2}^{2}
is in the function space
\mathcal{C}_{*}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and that
\rho
satisfies the weak solution identity (
2.5
) in Definition
2.2
for all test functions in
\mathcal{C}_{*}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, see Remark
2.5
.
Hence,
by applying (
2.5
) with
\phi
as defined above,
we obtain for the time evolution of
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})

\displaystyle=\underbrace{-\lambda\int\langle\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*},\theta-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})\rangle\,d\rho_{t}(\theta)}_{=:T_{1}}+\underbrace{\frac{d\sigma^{2}}{2}\int\left\|{\theta-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})}\right\|_{2}^{2}d\rho_{t}(\theta)}_{=:T_{2}},

(4.11)

where we used
\partial_{k}\phi(\theta)=(\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})_{k}
and
\partial^{2}_{kk}\phi(\theta)=1
for all
k\in\{1,\dots,d\}
.
Expanding the right-hand side of the scalar product in the integrand of
T_{1}
by subtracting and adding
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
yields

\begin{split}T_{1}&=-\lambda\int\langle\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*},\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\rangle\,d\rho_{t}(\theta)+\lambda\left\langle\int(\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\,d\rho_{t}(\theta),m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\right\rangle\\
&\leq-2\lambda\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})+\lambda\left\|{\mathbb{E}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2},\end{split}

(4.12)

with the last step involving Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
Analogously,
again by subtracting and adding
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
in the integrand of the term
T_{2}
,
we have with Cauchy-Schwarz inequality the upper bound

\begin{split}T_{2}&=\frac{d\sigma^{2}}{2}\left(\int\left\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}^{2}d\rho_{t}(\theta)-2\left\langle\int(\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\,d\rho_{t}(\theta),m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\right\rangle+\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\\
&\leq d\sigma^{2}\left(\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})+\int\left\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}d\rho_{t}(\theta)\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right).\end{split}

(4.13)

The first part of the statement, namely the upper bound in (
4.9
), now follows after noting that by Jensen’s inequality it holds
\|{\mathbb{E}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\|_{2}\leq\int\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\|_{2}\,d\rho_{t}(\theta)\leq\sqrt{2\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})}
.

What concerns the second part, i.e., the lower bound in (
4.10
), we have by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality also

\pm\left\langle\int(\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\,d\rho_{t}(\theta),m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\right\rangle\geq-\left\|{\mathbb{E}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}.

(4.14)

Thus,

\begin{split}T_{1}&=-\lambda\int\langle\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*},\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\rangle\,d\rho_{t}(\theta)+\lambda\left\langle\int(\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\,d\rho_{t}(\theta),m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\right\rangle\\
&\geq-2\lambda\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})-\lambda\left\|{\mathbb{E}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}\end{split}

(4.15)

as well as

\begin{split}T_{2}&=\frac{d\sigma^{2}}{2}\left(\int\left\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}^{2}d\rho_{t}(\theta)-2\left\langle\int(\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\,d\rho_{t}(\theta),m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\right\rangle+\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\\
&\geq d\sigma^{2}\left(\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})-\left\|{\mathbb{E}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}\right),\end{split}

(4.16)

where we used additionally that
\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\|_{2}^{2}\geq 0
.
The lower bound (
4.10
) now follows after recalling that
\|{\mathbb{E}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{2\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})}
by Jensen’s inequality.
∎

4.3
Quantitative Quantiled Laplace Principle (Q
2
LP)

In order to leverage the differential inequalities from Lemma
4.1
to establish the exponential convergence of the functional
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
,
we need to control the term
\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}
through the choices of the hyperparameters
\delta_{q}
,
\beta
and
\alpha
, which is the content of the following statement, which we refer to as the quantitative quantiled Laplace principle (Q
2
LP),
as it combines a quantitative quantile choice with the quantitative Laplace principle
[
51
, Proposition 4.5]
.

Proposition 4.2

(Quantitative quantiled Laplace principle)
.

Let
\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
fix
\alpha>0
,
let
r_{G}\in(0,\min\{R_{G},R^{H}_{G},(G_{\infty}/(2H_{G}))^{1/h_{G}}\}]
and
\delta_{q}\in(0,\min\{L_{\infty},(\eta_{L}r_{G})^{1/\nu_{L}}\}/2]
.
For any
r>0
define
G_{r}:=\sup_{\theta\in B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})}G(\theta)-G(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
.
Then, under
the inverse continuity property
A3
on
L
,
the Hölder continuity assumption
A2
on
L
,
the inverse continuity property
A5
on
G
and the Hölder continuity assumption
A4
on
G
,
and provided that there exists

\beta\in(0,1)\quad\text{satisfying}\quad q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]+\delta_{q}\leq\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L+\min\{L_{\infty},(\eta_{L}r_{G})^{1/\nu_{L}}\},

(4.17)

for any
r\in(0,\min\{r_{R},r_{G},R^{H}_{L},(\delta_{q}/H_{L})^{1/h_{L}}\}]
and
u>0
such that
u+G_{r}+H_{G}r_{G}^{h_{G}}\leq G_{\infty}
,
we have

\begin{split}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}&\leq\frac{(u+G_{r}+H_{G}r_{G}^{h_{G}})^{\nu_{G}}}{\eta_{G}}+\frac{\exp\left(-\alpha u\right)}{\varrho\big(B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)}\!\left(\int\left\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}dI^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho](\theta)\right).\end{split}

(4.18)

We furthermore have
B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\subset Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]\subset\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
.

Before providing the proof of Proposition
4.2
,
let us discuss the bound (
4.18
) in view of the error decomposition
\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}\leq\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)-\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}}\big\|_{2}+\big\|{\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}
.

Remark 4.3
.

The bound

\begin{split}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}&\leq\frac{(u+G_{r}+H_{G}r_{G}^{h_{G}})^{\nu_{G}}}{\eta_{G}}+\frac{\exp\left(-\alpha u\right)}{\varrho\big(B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)}\!\left(\int\left\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}dI^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho](\theta)\right)\end{split}

from (
4.18
) in Proposition
4.2
comprises two levels of approximation that correspond to the two error terms in the decomposition
\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}\leq\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)-\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}}\big\|_{2}+\big\|{\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}
from (
4.18
), and are thus related to the two hyperparameters
\beta
and
\alpha
, for which we refer to the discussion in Section
2.3
after Theorem
2.7
.

Let us discuss now which part of the bound (
4.18
) corresponds to which term and can be controlled through which hyperparameter.
For this purpose, imagine taking, for
\beta
fixed,

\alpha\rightarrow\infty
.
Recall that by the Laplace principle
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)=m^{G}_{\alpha}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho])
is expected to converge to
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}=\operatorname*{\arg\min}_{\theta\in Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}G(\theta)
as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
.
Indeed, taking
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
in the bound (
4.18
), allows for
u\rightarrow 0
and
r\rightarrow 0
, thus yielding the error bound
\big\|{\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}\leq(H_{G}r_{G}^{h_{G}})^{\nu_{G}}/\eta_{G}
.
This term can be controlled through the choice of the hyperparameter
\beta
since, according to (
4.17
), we need to find
\beta\in(0,1)
sufficiently small such that
q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]+\delta_{q}\leq\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L+\min\{L_{\infty},(\eta_{L}r_{G})^{1/\nu_{L}}\}
holds.
Feasible choices of
\beta
are described during the proof of Theorem
2.7
in Section
4
.

The remaining error contributions in (
4.18
) can be, as already alluded to, controlled through the choice of the hyperparameter
\alpha
.
Imagining setting
\beta=0
, (
4.18
) reduces to the classical quantitative Laplace principle
[
51
, Proposition 4.5]
.

Proof of Proposition
4.2
.

Preliminaries.

To begin with, notice that it holds

q^{L}_{\beta/2}[\varrho]\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]\,da\leq q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho],

(4.19)

since the
a
-quantile
q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]
as defined in (
2.4
) is monotonously increasing in
a
.

Set inclusion
B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\subset Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
.

We now start by establishing that with the choice of
r
it holds
B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\subset Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
, which will be useful throughout the proof.
First, since
r\leq r_{R}
, we have
B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\subset B_{R}(0)
, since for all
\theta\in B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
it holds
