\left\|{\theta}\right\|_{2}\leq\big\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}+\big\|{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}\leq r+\big\|{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}\leq r_{R}+\big\|{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}\leq R
by assumption on
R
from Section
2.1
.
Moreover, since
r\leq R^{H}_{L}
, employing the Hölder continuity
A2
of
L
yields for all
\theta\in B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
that

L(\theta)-\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L\leq H_{L}\left\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}^{h_{L}}\leq H_{L}r^{h_{L}}\leq\delta_{q}\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]\,da-\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L+\delta_{q},

(4.20)

where we further used that
r\leq(\delta_{q}/H_{L})^{1/h_{L}}
in the next-to-last step and that obviously
q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]\geq\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L
for any
a\in(0,1]
in the last.
Then
L(\theta)\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]\,da+\delta_{q}
together with the first observation made implies
\theta\in Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
, ergo
B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\subset Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
.

Set inclusion
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]\subset\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
.

We notice
that with (
2.13a
) of the inverse continuity property
A3
on the function
L
w.r.t. the set
\Theta

it holds

\operatorname{dist}(\theta,\Theta)\leq\frac{1}{\eta_{L}}\left(L(\theta)-\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L\right)^{\nu_{L}}\leq\frac{1}{\eta_{L}}\left(\left(\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]\,da+\delta_{q}\right)-\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L\right)^{\nu_{L}}\quad\text{for all }\theta\in\mathcal{N}_{R_{L}}(\Theta)\cap Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho],

(4.21)

where for the latter inequality we recall that by definition of
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
it holds
L(\theta)\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]\,da+\delta_{q}
for all
\theta\in Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
.
Together with the observation (
4.19
) we may further bound

\operatorname{dist}(\theta,\Theta)\leq\frac{1}{\eta_{L}}\left(\left(q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]+\delta_{q}\right)-\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L\right)^{\nu_{L}}\quad\text{for all }\theta\in\mathcal{N}_{R_{L}}(\Theta)\cap Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho].

(4.22)

For
\theta\in Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
it furthermore holds, again by (
4.19
), and by assumption on
\beta
that

L(\theta)\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]\,da+\delta_{q}\leq q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]+\delta_{q}\leq\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L+L_{\infty}.

(4.23)

Since, on the other hand, for all
\theta\in\big(\mathcal{N}_{R_{L}}(\Theta)\big)^{c}
it holds
L(\theta)>\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L+L_{\infty}
by (
2.13b
) of the inverse continuity property
A3
,
we have
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]\subset\mathcal{N}_{R_{L}}(\Theta)
.
Thus (
4.22
) holds on
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
.
Moreover, with the assumption on
\beta
we conclude from (
4.22
) and the latter observation that

\operatorname{dist}(\theta,\Theta)\leq r_{G}\quad\text{for all }\theta\in Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho],

(4.24)

i.e.,
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]\subset\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
.

With
r_{G}\leq R_{G}
the inverse continuity property
A5
on
G
holds.
Since
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]\subset\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
as established in (
4.24
), it holds in particular on the set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
, on which
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}
is computed.

Main proof.

In order to control the term
\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}
,
we can follow the proof of the quantitative Laplace principle
[
51
, Propostion 4.5]
and
[
50
, Proposition 1]
with the necessary modification which are due to the inverse continuity property
A5
on
G
being slightly different as a consequence of the necessity of distinguishing between
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
and
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}
.

Let
\widetilde{r}\geq r>0
and recall that
r\in(0,\min\{r_{R},r_{G},R^{H}_{L},(\delta_{q}/H_{L})^{1/h_{L}}\}]
by assumption.
Using the definition of the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)=\int\theta\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta)/\|{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}}\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho])}\,dI^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho](\theta)
we can decompose

\begin{split}\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}&\leq\int_{B_{\widetilde{r}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})}\big\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}\frac{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta)}{\left\|{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}}\right\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho])}}dI^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho](\theta)\\
&\qquad+\int_{\left(B_{\widetilde{r}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right)^{c}}\big\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}\frac{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta)}{\left\|{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}}\right\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho])}}dI^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho](\theta).\end{split}

(4.25)

First term in (
4.25
)
:
Since for all
\theta\in B_{\widetilde{r}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
it holds
\big\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}\leq\widetilde{r}
, the first term in (
4.25
) is bounded by
\widetilde{r}
.
Recalling the definition of
G_{r}
and introducing the notation
\widetilde{G}_{r}:=\sup_{\theta\in B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})}G(\theta)-G(\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}})
,
we choose
\widetilde{r}=(u+\widetilde{G}_{r})^{\nu_{G}}/{\eta_{G}}
, which is a valid choice since

\begin{split}\widetilde{r}=\frac{(u+\widetilde{G}_{r})^{\nu_{G}}}{\eta_{G}}\geq\frac{\widetilde{G}_{r}^{\nu_{G}}}{\eta_{G}}&=\frac{\left(\sup_{\theta\in B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})}G(\theta)-G(\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}})\right)^{\nu_{G}}}{\eta_{G}}\\
&\geq\sup_{\theta\in B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})}\big\|{\theta-\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}}\big\|_{2}\\
&\geq r.\end{split}

(4.26)

Here,
the inequality in the second line holds since the first part (
2.15a
) of the inverse continuity property
A5
on
G
holds on the set
B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\subset B_{r_{G}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
since
r\leq r_{G}
.
To see the inequality in the last line recall that
r\leq r_{G}
and
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}\in B_{r_{G}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
(note that the expression is minimal if
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}=\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
, in which case the supremum is equal to
r
).

Second term in (
4.25
)
:
Let us first note that because of Markov’s inequality it holds for any
a>0
that

\|{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}}\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho])}\geq a\varrho\left(\left\{\theta\in B_{R}(0):\exp(-\alpha G(\theta))\geq a\text{ and }L(\theta)\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]\,da+\delta_{q}\right\}\right)

(4.27)

Choosing
a=\exp\big(-\alpha(\widetilde{G}_{r}+G(\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}))\big)
and noticing that

\begin{split}&\varrho\left(\left\{\theta\in B_{R}(0):\exp(-\alpha G(\theta))\geq\exp\big(-\alpha(\widetilde{G}_{r}+G(\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}))\big)\text{ and }L(\theta)\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]\,da+\delta_{q}\right\}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad=\varrho\left(\left\{\theta\in B_{R}(0):G(\theta)\leq\widetilde{G}_{r}+G(\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}})\text{ and }L(\theta)\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]\,da+\delta_{q}\right\}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad=\varrho\left(\left\{\theta\in B_{R}(0):G(\theta)\leq\sup_{\tilde{\theta}\in B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})}G(\tilde{\theta})\text{ and }L(\theta)\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\int_{\beta/2}^{\beta}q^{L}_{a}[\varrho]\,da+\delta_{q}\right\}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\geq\varrho\left(B_{R}(0)\cap B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\cap Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad=\varrho\left(B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right),\end{split}

(4.28)

where we reused the in the preliminaries shown set inclusions
B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\subset B_{R}(0)
and
B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\subset Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]
in the last step,
allows to conclude (
4.27
) to obtain
\|{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}}\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho])}\geq\exp\big(-\alpha(\widetilde{G}_{r}+G(\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}))\big)\varrho\big(B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)
.
With this we have

\begin{split}&\int_{\left(B_{\widetilde{r}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right)^{c}}\big\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}\frac{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta)}{\left\|{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}}\right\|_{L^{1}(I^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho])}}dI^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho](\theta)\\
&\qquad\qquad\leq\int_{\left(B_{\widetilde{r}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right)^{c}\cap Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}\big\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}\frac{\exp\big(-\alpha(G(\theta)-(\widetilde{G}_{r}+G(\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}})))\big)}{\varrho\big(B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)}dI^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho](\theta)\\
&\qquad\qquad\leq\!\frac{\exp\left(-\alpha\left(\inf_{\theta\in\left(B_{\widetilde{r}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right)^{c}\cap Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}G(\theta)-G(\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}})-\widetilde{G}_{r}\right)\right)}{\varrho\big(B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)}\!\int\big\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}dI^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho](\theta)\end{split}

(4.29)

for the second term in (
4.25
).
Thus, for any
\widetilde{r}\geq r>0
we obtain for (
4.25
) that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\varrho)-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}&\leq\widetilde{r}+\frac{\exp\left(-\alpha\left(\inf_{\theta\in\left(B_{\widetilde{r}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right)^{c}\cap Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}G(\theta)-G(\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}})-\widetilde{G}_{r}\right)\right)}{\varrho\big(B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)}\!\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\int\left\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}dI^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho](\theta).\end{aligned}

(4.30)

With the choice
\widetilde{r}=(u+\widetilde{G}_{r})^{\nu_{G}}/{\eta_{G}}

it holds under the assumption
u+G_{r}+H_{G}{r_{G}}^{h_{G}}\leq G_{\infty}
that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{r}=\frac{(u+\widetilde{G}_{r})^{\nu_{G}}}{\eta_{G}}&=\frac{(u+G_{r}+(G(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})-G(\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}})))^{\nu_{G}}}{\eta_{G}}\\
&\leq\frac{(u+G_{r}+H_{G}{r_{G}}^{h_{G}})^{\nu_{G}}}{\eta_{G}}\leq\frac{G_{\infty}^{\nu_{G}}}{\eta_{G}}.\end{split}

(4.31)

In order to obtain the inequality in the next-to-last step be reminded that
\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}\in B_{r_{G}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
and that
r_{G}\leq R^{H}_{G}
, allowing us to employ the Hölder continuity
A4
of
G
to derive the upper bound
\big|{G(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})-G(\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}})}\big|\leq H_{G}\big\|{\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}-\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}}}\big\|_{2}^{h_{G}}\leq H_{G}r_{G}^{h_{G}}
.
Reordering (
4.31
) shows
(\eta_{G}\widetilde{r})^{1/\nu_{G}}\leq G_{\infty}
.
Thus, recalling that
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]\subset\mathcal{N}_{r_{G}}(\Theta)
as established in (
4.24
) and using again both (
2.15a
) and (
2.15b
) of
A5
, we have the first inequality in

\inf_{\theta\in\left(B_{\widetilde{r}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right)^{c}\cap Q^{L}_{\beta}[\varrho]}G(\theta)-G(\tilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{good}})-\widetilde{G}_{r}\geq\min\big\{G_{\infty},(\eta_{G}\widetilde{r})^{1/{\nu_{G}}}\big\}-\widetilde{G}_{r}=(\eta_{G}\widetilde{r})^{1/{\nu_{G}}}-\widetilde{G}_{r}=u.

(4.32)

The other two steps are a result of the definition of
\widetilde{r}
.
Inserting this and the upper bound
\widetilde{r}\leq(u+G_{r}+H_{G}{r_{G}}^{h_{G}})^{\nu_{G}}/\eta_{G}
from (
4.31
) into (
4.30
),
we obtain the result.
∎

4.4
A Lower Bound for the Probability Mass around
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}

In this section, we establish a lower bound on the probability mass
\min_{t\in[0,T]}\rho_{t}(B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}))
for any arbitrarily small radius
r>0
and a finite time horizon
T<\infty
,
which is crucial to ensure the feasibility of the choice (
4.17
) and employ (
4.18
) from Lemma
4.2
.

To do so, we define a smooth mollifier
\phi_{r}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow[0,1]
satisfying
\operatorname{supp}(\phi_{r})=B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
.
One such possibility is

\phi_{r}(\theta):=\begin{cases}\exp\left(1-\frac{r^{2}}{r^{2}-\left\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right),&\textrm{ if }\big\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}<r,\\
0,&\textrm{ else.}\end{cases}

(4.33)

It then holds
\rho_{t}(B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}))\geq\int\phi_{r}(\theta)\,d\rho_{t}(\theta)
,
where the right-hand side can be studied by using the weak solution property (
2.5
) of
\rho
as in Definition
2.2
.

Proposition 4.4

(Lower bound for
\rho_{t}\big(B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)
, cf.
[
51
, Proposition 4.6]
)
.

Let
T>0,\ r>0
, and fix parameters
\alpha,\beta,R,\delta_{q},\lambda,\sigma>0
.
Assume
\rho\in\mathcal{C}([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
weakly solves the Fokker-Planck Equation (
1.13
) in the sense of Definition
2.2
with initial condition
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and for
t\in[0,T]
.
Then, for all
t\in[0,T]
we have

\displaystyle\rho_{t}\left(B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\right)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\int\phi_{r}(\theta)\,d\rho_{0}(\theta)\right)\exp\left(-pt\right),\text{ where}

(4.34)

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle:=2\max\left\{\frac{\lambda(cr+B\sqrt{c})}{(1-c)^{2}r}+\frac{\sigma^{2}(cr^{2}+B^{2})(2c+d)}{(1-c)^{4}r^{2}},\frac{2\lambda^{2}}{(2c-1)\sigma^{2}}\right\}

(4.35)

for any
B<\infty
with
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}\leq B

and for any
c\in(1/2,1)
satisfying
d(1-c)^{2}\leq(2c-1)c
.

Proof.

The result follows immediately from
[
51
, Proposition 4.6]
after noticing that its proof does not rely on the specific form and relation of
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})
and
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
as long as
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}\leq B<\infty
, which is per assumption.
This has been pointed out in Remark 4.7(i) in the extended arXiv version of
[
51
]
(corresponding to
[
51
, Remark 4.7]
4
4

4

This remark was removed in the published version
[
51
]
due to the journal’s page limit policy.
in the journal version).
∎

4.5
Proof of Theorem
2.7

We are now ready to combine the formerly established technical tools and present the proof of Theorem
2.7
.

Proof of Theorem
2.7
.

Let us start by recalling that
G_{r}:=\sup_{\theta\in B_{r}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})}G(\theta)-G(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
for
r>0

and by introducing the abbreviation

\displaystyle c\left(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma\right):=\min\left\{\frac{\vartheta}{2}\frac{\left(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\right)}{\sqrt{2}\left(\lambda+d\sigma^{2}\right)},\sqrt{\vartheta\frac{\left(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\right)}{d\sigma^{2}}}\right\}.

(4.36)

Moreover, we define

\displaystyle r_{G,\varepsilon}:=\min\left\{\left(\frac{1}{2H_{G}}\left(\eta_{G}\frac{c\left(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma\right)\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)^{1/{\nu_{G}}}\right)^{1/{h_{G}}},R_{G},R^{H}_{G},\left(\frac{G_{\infty}}{2H_{G}}\right)^{1/h_{G}}\right\},

(4.37)

which satisfies
r_{G,\varepsilon}\in(0,\min\{R_{G},R^{H}_{G},(G_{\infty}/(2H_{G}))^{1/h_{G}}\}]
by design.
We further emphasize, that, by assumption,
\delta_{q}>0
is sufficiently small (see Remark
2.8
) in the sense that it satisfies

\displaystyle\delta_{q}\leq\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{L_{\infty},(\eta_{L}r_{G,\varepsilon})^{1/\nu_{L}}\right\}.

(4.38)

Choice of
\beta
.

Let us further abbreviate by
\xi_{L,\varepsilon}:=\min\left\{L_{\infty},(\eta_{L}r_{G,\varepsilon})^{1/\nu_{L}}\right\}-\delta_{q}
and point out that
\xi_{L,\varepsilon}>0
granted by the afore-discussed properties of
\delta_{q}
.
Further, define

\displaystyle r_{H,\varepsilon}:=\min\left\{R^{H}_{L},\left(\frac{\xi_{L,\varepsilon}}{H_{L}}\right)^{1/h_{L}}\right\},

(4.39)

which satisfies
r_{H,\varepsilon}\in(0,R^{H}_{L}]
by design.

We now choose
\beta\in(0,1)
such that

\beta<\beta_{0}:=\frac{1}{2}\rho_{0}\big(B_{r_{H,\varepsilon}/2}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)\exp(-p_{H,\varepsilon}T^{*}),

(4.40)

where
p_{H,\varepsilon}
is as defined in (
4.35
) in Proposition
4.4
with
B=c(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma)\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(\rho_{0})}
and with
r=r_{H,\varepsilon}
.
Such choice of
\beta
is possible since
\beta_{0}\in(0,1)
, which in turn is due to
p_{H,\varepsilon},T^{*}<\infty
,
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\in\operatorname{supp}(\rho_{0})
and
r_{H,\varepsilon}>0
on the one hand, and
p_{H,\varepsilon}T^{*}>0
and
\rho_{0}
being a probability measure on the other.
For such
\beta
we have
q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}]\leq\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L+\xi_{L,\varepsilon}
for all
t\in[0,T^{*}]
for the following reason:
Due to
A2
, for all
\theta\in B_{r_{H,\varepsilon}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
it holds

L(\theta)-\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L\leq H_{L}\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\|_{2}^{h_{L}}\leq H_{L}r_{H,\varepsilon}^{h_{L}}\leq\xi_{L,\varepsilon}
.
Thus,
B_{r_{H,\varepsilon}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\subset\{\theta:L(\theta)-\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L\leq\xi_{L,\varepsilon}\}
.
We further note that by Proposition
4.4
with
r_{H,\varepsilon}
,
p_{H,\varepsilon}
and
B
(for
r
,
p
and
B
) as defined before, it holds for all
t\in[0,T^{*}]
that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \rho_{t}\big(B_{r_{H,\varepsilon}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)&\geq\left(\int\phi_{r_{H,\varepsilon}}(\theta)\,d\rho_{0}(\theta)\right)\exp(-p_{H,\varepsilon}t)\\
&\geq\frac{1}{2}\,\rho_{0}\big(B_{r_{H,\varepsilon}/2}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)\exp(-p_{H,\varepsilon}T^{*})>\beta,\end{aligned}

(4.41)

where we used in the second line the fact that
\phi_{r}
(as defined in Equation (
4.33
)) is bounded from below on
B_{r/2}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
by
1/2
.
The last inequality is by choice of
\beta
.
With this and the aforementioned set inclusion, we have for all
t\in[0,T^{*}]
that

\displaystyle\beta<\rho_{t}\big(B_{r_{H,\varepsilon}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)\leq\rho_{t}\big(\{\theta:L(\theta)-\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L\leq\xi_{L,\varepsilon}\}\big)

(4.42)

and thus by definition of
q^{L}_{\beta}[\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}]
as the infimum, that
q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{t}]\leq\makebox[0.0pt]{$\phantom{L}\mkern 1.5mu\underline{\mkern-1.5mu\phantom{L}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$}L+\xi_{L,\varepsilon}
for all
t\in[0,T^{*}]
.

Choice of
\alpha
.

Let us further define

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{1}{4}\min\bigg\{\!\left(\eta_{G}\frac{c\left(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma\right)\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)^{1/{\nu_{G}}}\!,G_{\infty}\bigg\}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}:=\max_{s\in[0,r_{G,\varepsilon}]}\left\{\max_{\theta\in B_{s}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})}G(\theta)-G(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\leq u_{\varepsilon}\right\},

(4.43)

which satisfy
u_{\varepsilon}>0
and
\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}\in(0,r_{G,\varepsilon}]
by design.
Notice that the continuity of
G
ensures that there exists
s_{u_{\varepsilon}}>0
such that
G(\theta)-G(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\leq u_{\varepsilon}
for all
\theta\in B_{s_{u_{\varepsilon}}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
, thus yielding also
\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}>0
.
Moreover, since
G_{r}
is monotonously increasing in
r
, any
r_{\varepsilon}\in(0,\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}]\subset(0,r_{G,\varepsilon}]
,
in particular the choice

\displaystyle r_{\varepsilon}:=\min\left\{r_{R},\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon},R^{H}_{L},\left(\frac{\delta_{q}}{H_{L}}\right)^{1/h_{L}}\right\},

(4.44)

satisfies

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon}+G_{r_{\varepsilon}}+H_{G}r_{G,\varepsilon}^{h_{G}}\leq u_{\varepsilon}+G_{\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}}+H_{G}r_{G,\varepsilon}^{h_{G}}\leq u_{\varepsilon}+G_{\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}}+\frac{G_{\infty}}{2}\leq 2u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{G_{\infty}}{2}\leq G_{\infty},

(4.45)

where we utilized that
r_{G,\varepsilon}\leq(G_{\infty}/(2H_{G}))^{1/h_{G}}
in the second step, the definitions of
\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}
as well as
G_{r}
in the third, and the definition of
u_{\varepsilon}
in the last.

All parameters
r_{G,\varepsilon}
,
\delta_{q}
,
\beta
,
r_{\varepsilon}
, and
u_{\varepsilon}
are now in line with the requirements of Proposition
4.2
and it remains to choose
\alpha

such that

\begin{split}\alpha>\alpha_{0}&:=\frac{1}{u_{\varepsilon}}\Bigg(\log\left(\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{c\left(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma\right)}\right)-\log\left(\rho_{0}\big(B_{r_{\varepsilon}/2}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)\right)+\max\left\{\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{\mathcal{V}(\rho_{0})}{\varepsilon}\right),p_{\varepsilon}T^{*}\right\}\!\Bigg)\end{split}

(4.46)

where
p_{\varepsilon}
is as
p
defined in (
4.35
) in Proposition
4.4
with
B=c(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma)\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(\rho_{0})}
and with
r=r_{\varepsilon}
.

Main proof.

Let us now define the time horizon
T_{\alpha,\beta}\geq 0
, which may depend on
\alpha
and
\beta
,
by

\displaystyle T_{\alpha,\beta}:=\sup\big\{t\geq 0:\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t^{\prime}})>\varepsilon\text{ and }\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}<C(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[0,t]\big\}

(4.47)

with
C(t):=c(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma)\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})}
.
Notice for later use that
C(0)=B
.

Our aim is to show
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})=\varepsilon
with
T_{\alpha,\beta}\in\big[\frac{1-\vartheta}{(1+\vartheta/2)}\;\!T^{*},T^{*}\big]
and that we have at least exponential decay of
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
until time
T_{\alpha,\beta}
, i.e., until accuracy
\varepsilon
is reached.

First, however, we ensure that
T_{\alpha,\beta}>0
.
With the mapping
t\mapsto\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
being continuous as a consequence of the regularity
\rho\in\mathcal{C}([0,T^{*}],\mathcal{P}_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
(as established in Theorem
2.4
) and
t\mapsto\!\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})\!-\!\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}
being continuous as a consequence of the continuity of the mapping
t\mapsto m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})
(as established in Theorem
2.4
),

T_{\alpha,\beta}>0
follows from the definition, since
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{0})>\varepsilon
and
\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{0})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}<C(0)
.
While the former is immediate by assumption, for the latter an application of Proposition
4.2
with
r_{G,\varepsilon}
,
r_{\varepsilon}
,
u_{\varepsilon}
and
\rho_{0}
(for
r_{G}
,
r
,
u
and
\varrho
) as defined at the beginning of the proof yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{0})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}&\leq\frac{(u_{\varepsilon}+G_{r_{\varepsilon}}+H_{G}r_{G,\varepsilon}^{h_{G}})^{\nu_{G}}}{\eta_{G}}+\frac{\exp\left(-\alpha u_{\varepsilon}\right)}{\rho_{0}\big(B_{r_{\varepsilon}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)}\int\left\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}dI^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{0}](\theta)\\
&\leq\frac{(2u_{\varepsilon}+H_{G}r_{G,\varepsilon}^{h_{G}})^{\nu_{G}}}{\eta_{G}}+\frac{\exp\left(-\alpha u_{\varepsilon}\right)}{\rho_{0}\big(B_{r_{\varepsilon}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)}\int\left\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}dI^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{0}](\theta)\\
&\leq\frac{c\left(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma\right)\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{2}+\frac{\exp\left(-\alpha u_{\varepsilon}\right)}{\rho_{0}\big(B_{r_{\varepsilon}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)}\sqrt{2\mathcal{V}(\rho_{0})}\\
&\leq\frac{c\left(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma\right)\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{2}+\frac{\exp\left(-\alpha u_{\varepsilon}\right)}{\rho_{0}\big(B_{r_{\varepsilon}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)}\sqrt{2\mathcal{V}(\rho_{0})}\\
&\leq c\left(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma\right)\sqrt{\varepsilon}<c\left(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma\right)\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(\rho_{0})}=C(0).\end{split}

(4.48)

Proposition
4.2
is applicable, since all assumptions hold as formerly verified.
Thus, the first inequality in (
4.48
) holds.
The second step is due to the definition of
r_{\varepsilon}
,
the third and fourth step follow after plugging in the definitions and properties of
r_{G,\varepsilon}
and
u_{\varepsilon}
, and simplifying.
Finally, the first inequality in the last line of (
4.48
) holds by choice of
\alpha
in (
4.46
).
The remainder is by assumption and definition.

Next, we show that the functional
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
decays essentially exponentially fast in time.
More precisely, we prove that, up to time
T_{\alpha,\beta}
,
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
decays

(i)

at least exponentially fast (with rate
(1-\vartheta)(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2})
), and

(ii)

at most exponentially fast (with rate
(1+\vartheta/2)(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2})
).

To obtain
(i)
, recall that Equation (
4.9
) in Lemma
4.1
provides an upper bound on
\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
given by

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})\leq&-\left(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\right)\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})+\sqrt{2}\left(\lambda+d\sigma^{2}\right)\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}\phantom{.}\\
&+\frac{d\sigma^{2}}{2}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}^{2}.\end{split}

(4.49)

Combining this with the definition of
T_{\alpha,\beta}
in (
4.47
) we have by construction

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})\leq-(1-\vartheta)\left(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\right)\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})\quad\text{ for all }t\in(0,T_{\alpha,\beta}).

(4.50)

Analogously, for
(ii)
, by the second part of Lemma
4.1
, Equation (
4.10
),
we obtain a lower bound on
\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
of the form

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})&\geq-\left(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\right)\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})-\sqrt{2}\left(\lambda+d\sigma^{2}\right)\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}\\
&\geq-(1+\vartheta/2)\left(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\right)\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})\quad\text{ for all }t\in(0,T_{\alpha,\beta}),\end{split}

(4.51)

where the second inequality again exploits the definition of
T_{\alpha,\beta}
.
Grönwall’s inequality now implies for all
t\in[0,T_{\alpha,\beta}]
the upper and lower bound

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{V}(\rho_{0})\exp\left(-(1-\vartheta)\left(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\right)t\right),

(4.52)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{V}(\rho_{0})\exp\left(-(1+\vartheta/2)\left(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\right)t\right),

(4.53)

thereby proving
(i)
and
(ii)
.
We further note that the definition of
T_{\alpha,\beta}
in (
4.47
) together with the definition of
C(t)
and (
4.52
) permits to control

\displaystyle\max_{t\in[0,T_{\alpha,\beta}]}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}\leq\max_{t\in[0,T_{\alpha,\beta}]}C(t)\leq C(0).

(4.54)

To conclude, it remains to prove that
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})=\varepsilon
with
T_{\alpha,\beta}\in\big[\frac{1-\vartheta}{(1+\vartheta/2)}\;\!T^{*},T^{*}\big]
.
For this we distinguish the following three cases.

Case
T_{\alpha,\beta}\geq T^{*}
:

We can use the definition of
T^{*}
in (
2.16
) and the time-evolution bound of
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
in (
4.52
) to conclude that
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T^{*}})\leq\varepsilon
.
Hence, by definition of
T_{\alpha,\beta}
in (
4.47
) together with the continuity of
t\mapsto\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
, we find
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})=\varepsilon
with
T_{\alpha,\beta}=T^{*}
.

Case
T_{\alpha,\beta}<T^{*}
and
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})\leq\varepsilon
:

By continuity of
t\mapsto\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
, it holds for
T_{\alpha,\beta}
as defined in (
4.47
),
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})=\varepsilon
.
Thus,
\varepsilon=\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})\geq\mathcal{V}(\rho_{0})\exp\left(-(1+\vartheta/2)\left(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\right)T_{\alpha,\beta}\right)
by (
4.53
), which can be reordered as

\displaystyle\frac{1-\vartheta}{(1+\vartheta/2)}\,T^{*}=\frac{1}{(1+\vartheta/2)\left(2\lambda-d\sigma^{2}\right)}\log\left(\frac{\mathcal{V}(\rho_{0})}{\varepsilon}\right)\leq T_{\alpha,\beta}<T^{*}.

(4.55)

Case
T_{\alpha,\beta}<T^{*}
and
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})>\varepsilon
:

We shall show that this case can never occur by verifying that
\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}<C(T_{\alpha,\beta})
due to the choices of
\alpha
in (
4.46
) and
\beta
in (
4.40
).
In fact, fulfilling simultaneously both
\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})>\varepsilon
and
\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}<C(T_{\alpha,\beta})
would contradict the definition of
T_{\alpha,\beta}
in (
4.47
) itself.
To this end, we apply again Proposition
4.2
with
r_{G,\varepsilon}
,
r_{\varepsilon}
and
u_{\varepsilon}
as defined at the beginning of the proof.
Proposition
4.2
is again applicable, since all assumptions hold as formerly verified and since
T_{\alpha,\beta}\leq T^{*}
.
Thus, the first inequality in (
4.56
) below holds and the following steps are obtained when plugging in the definitions and properties of
r_{\varepsilon}
,
r_{G,\varepsilon}
and
u_{\varepsilon}
.
For the sharp inequality in the fourth step we recall that in this case we assumed
\varepsilon<\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})
.
The fifth step merely results from simplifying.
We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}&\leq\frac{(u_{\varepsilon}+G_{r_{\varepsilon}}+H_{G}r_{G,\varepsilon}^{h_{G}})^{\nu_{G}}}{\eta_{G}}+\frac{\exp\left(-\alpha u_{\varepsilon}\right)}{\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}}\big(B_{r_{\varepsilon}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)}\int\left\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}dI^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}}](\theta)\\
&\leq\frac{(2u_{\varepsilon}+H_{G}r_{G,\varepsilon}^{h_{G}})^{\nu_{G}}}{\eta_{G}}+\frac{\exp\left(-\alpha u_{\varepsilon}\right)}{\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}}\big(B_{r_{\varepsilon}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)}\int\left\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}dI^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}}](\theta)\\
&\leq\frac{c\left(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma\right)\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{2}+\frac{\exp\left(-\alpha u_{\varepsilon}\right)}{\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}}\big(B_{r_{\varepsilon}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)}\int\left\|{\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}dI^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}}](\theta)\\
&<\frac{c\left(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma\right)\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})}}{2}+\frac{\exp\left(-\alpha u_{\varepsilon}\right)}{\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}}\big(B_{r_{\varepsilon}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)}\sqrt{2\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})}.\end{split}

(4.56)

Since, thanks to (
4.54
), it holds the bound
\max_{t\in[0,T_{\alpha,\beta}]}\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}\leq B
for
B=C(0)
, which is in particular independent of
\alpha
, Proposition
4.4
guarantees that there exists a
p_{\varepsilon}>0
not depending on
\alpha
(but depending on
B
and
r_{\varepsilon}
) with

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}}(B_{r_{\varepsilon}}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}))&\geq\left(\int\phi_{r_{\varepsilon}}(\theta)\,d\rho_{0}(\theta)\right)\exp(-p_{\varepsilon}T_{\alpha,\beta})\\
&\geq\frac{1}{2}\,\rho_{0}\big(B_{r_{\varepsilon}/2}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)\exp(-p_{\varepsilon}T^{*})>0,\end{aligned}

(4.57)

where we used
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}\in\operatorname{supp}(\rho_{0})
for bounding the initial mass
\rho_{0}
, the fact that
\phi_{r}
(as defined in Equation (
4.33
)) is bounded from below on
B_{r/2}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})
by
1/2
and that
T_{\alpha,\beta}\leq T^{*}
.
With this we can continue the chain of inequalities in (
4.56
) to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\right\|_{2}&<\frac{c\left(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma\right)\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})}}{2}+\frac{2\exp\left(-\alpha u_{\varepsilon}\right)}{\rho_{0}\big(B_{r_{\varepsilon}/2}(\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*})\big)\exp(-p_{\varepsilon}T^{*})}\sqrt{2\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})}\\
&\leq c\left(\vartheta,\lambda,\sigma\right)\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(\rho_{T_{\alpha,\beta}})}=C(T_{\alpha,\beta}),\end{split}

(4.58)

where the last inequality in (
4.58
) holds by choice of
\alpha
in (
4.46
).
This establishes the desired contradiction, again as consequence of the continuity of the mappings
t\mapsto\mathcal{V}(\rho_{t})
and
t\mapsto\big\|{m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{t})-\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}}\big\|_{2}
.
∎

5
Numerical Experiments

In this section, we conduct a series of numerical experiments to demonstrate the practicability as well as efficiency of the CB
2
O method proposed in (
1.2
).
For this purpose, we first present in Section
5.1
a practical implementation of CB
2
O.
Thereafter, we showcase in Sections
5.2
and
5.3
, respectively, two examples for solving nonconvex bi-level optimization problems of the form (
1.1
).
In Section
5.2
, we consider the special instance of classical constrained global optimization in lower-dimensional settings.
We compare CB
2
O with the methods
[
22
,
36
,
35
,
48
,
47
]
designed specifically for this task.
In Section
5.3
, we then tackle a sparse representation learning task
[
59
]
, which aims at learning a sparse feature representation on a supervised dataset by using neural networks.
With this we demonstrate that the CB
2
O method is also applicable in high-dimensional real-data applications.
A further machine learning example in the setting of (clustered) federated learning
[
32
]

is tackled in the recent paper
[
56
]
, where CB
2
O is demonstrated to be robust against backdoor attacks by leveraging a bi-level optimization problem of the form (
1.1
).

5.1
Implementation of the CB
2
O Algorithm

To implement the CB
2
O algorithm,
we consider an Euler-Maruyama discretization of the interacting
N
particle system introduced in (
1.2
) with time discretization step size
\Delta t>0
.
At each time step
k=0,1,2,\dots
, the particles
\{\theta^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
are updated according to the iterative update rule

\theta_{k+1}^{i}=\theta_{k}^{i}-\Delta t\lambda\left(\theta_{k}^{i}-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{k}^{N})\right)+\sqrt{\Delta t}\sigma D\left(\theta_{k}^{i}-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{k}^{N})\right)B_{k}^{i},\qquad\text{for }i\in\{1,\dots,N\},

(5.1)

where
B_{k}^{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d\times d})
.
The consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{k}^{N})
is defined analogously to (
1.6
) for the particle positions
\{\theta_{k}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
.
For readers’ convenience, let us recall

\begin{split}m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{k}^{N}):=\sum_{\theta_{k}^{i}:L(\theta_{k}^{i})\leq q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{k}^{N}]}\theta_{k}^{i}\frac{\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta_{k}^{i})}{\sum_{\theta_{k}^{j}:L(\theta_{k}^{j})\leq q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{k}^{N}]}\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta_{k}^{j})},\qquad\text{with}\quad\omega_{\alpha}^{G}(\theta):=\exp\left(-\alpha G(\theta)\right).\end{split}

(5.2)

Here the sub-level set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{k}^{N}]
and the
\beta
-quantile function
q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{k}^{N}]
are defined analogously to (
1.7
) and (
1.5
), respectively, as

Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{k}^{N}]=\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:L(\theta)\leq q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{k}^{N}]\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{k}^{N}]=L\left(\theta_{k}^{\#\lceil\beta N\rceil}\right),

(5.3)

with the empirical measure given by
\rho_{k}^{N}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\theta_{k}^{i}}
.

Algorithm
1
below now summarizes the CB
2
O algorithm.

Algorithm 1

CB
2
O Algorithm

0:

CB
2
O hyperparamerters
\lambda,\sigma,\alpha,\beta
; discretization time step size
\Delta t
; stopping criteria
\varepsilon_{\text{stop}}
; maximal termination round
K
;

1:

Initialize particles
\{\theta_{0}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
;

2:

Initialize the stopping criterion
c_{\text{stop}}>\varepsilon_{\text{stop}}
and set iteration counter
k=0
;

3:

while

c_{\text{stop}}>\varepsilon_{\text{stop}}\text{ and }k\in\{0,\dots,K\}]

do

4:

Evaluate the
\beta
-quantile
q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{k}^{N}]
and estimate the quantile set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{k}^{N}]
using (
5.3
);

5:

Compute the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{k}^{N})
according to (
5.2
) from the particle positions
\{\theta_{k}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}\cap Q^{L}_{\beta}[\rho_{k}^{N}]
;

6:

Update the particle positions
\{\theta^{i}_{k+1}\}_{i=1}^{N}
by evaluating (
5.1
);

7:

Set
k\leftarrow k+1
and evaluate the stopping criterion
c_{\text{stop}}\leftarrow\frac{1}{dN}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\|{\theta_{k+1}^{i}-m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho_{k}^{N})}\right\|_{2}^{2}
;

8:

end

while

8:

Consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}(\rho^{N}_{\text{stop}})
.

Remark 5.1
.

Algorithm
1
describes the standard implementation of the CB
2
O algorithm.
Many useful numerical tricks, including random batch methods
[
34
]
, particle re-initialization
[
34
,
12
]
, hyperparameter cooling strategies
[
50
]
, amongst others, proposed in the past to improve the performance of CBO-type methods, can also be applied immediately to our CB
2
O algorithm.
We will discuss some of them in the later experimental sections, and refer to
[
34
,
50
,
12
]
for more detailed and elaborate descriptions.

5.2
Constrained Optimization

Standard constrained optimization problems can be reformulated as bi-level optimization problems of the form (
1.1
).
In particular,
any optimization problem with equality constraints,

\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(\theta)\quad\text{s.t.{}}\quad g_{\ell}(\theta)=0,\quad\ell=1,\dots,m,

(5.4)

can be equivalently written as

\min_{\theta^{*}\in\Theta}G(\theta^{*})\quad\text{s.t.{}}\quad\Theta=\operatorname*{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}L(\theta):=\sum_{l=1}^{m}g_{l}^{2}(\theta).

(5.5)

Thanks to this reformulation, we can employ our proposed CB
2
O methods, as described in Algorithm
1
, to constrained optimization problems.

5.2.1
Baseline Comparisons

Let us first describe in detail the experimental setup for constrained optimization problem.

Optimization Problem Setup.

We choose for the upper-level objective function
G
the two-dimensional Ackley function, i.e.,

\displaystyle G(\theta)=-A\exp\left(-a\sqrt{\frac{b^{2}}{d}\big\|{\theta-\hat{\theta}}\big\|_{2}^{2}}\right)-\exp\left(\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\cos\left(2\pi b\big(\theta_{i}-\hat{\theta}_{i}\big)\right)\right)+e^{1}+A,

(5.6)

where
A=20
,
a=0.2
,
b=3
,
d=2
, and
\hat{\theta}=(1/2,1/3)
. Furthermore, we consider two different constraints, a circular and a star constraint, i.e.,

Circular constraint:

\displaystyle\left\{(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,\theta_{1}^{2}+\theta_{2}^{2}-1=0\right\},

(5.7a)

Star constraint:

\displaystyle\left\{(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,\theta_{1}^{2}+\theta_{2}^{2}-(1+0.5\sin(5\text{atan2}(\theta_{2},\theta_{1})))^{2}=0\right\}.

(5.7b)

With respect to the constraints (
5.7a
) and (
5.7b
), respectively, the unique constrained global minimizers of
G
are

\displaystyle\text{Circular:}\quad\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}

\displaystyle=(0.781475,0.623937),

(5.8a)

\displaystyle\text{Star:}\quad\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}

\displaystyle=(0.482208,0.468687).

(5.8b)

Please find visualizations of the two-dimensional Ackley function with circular and star constraints in Figures
6(a)
and
6(b)
, respectively.

(a)

Circular constraint

(b)

Star constraint

Figure 6
:
2D Ackley function with two different constraint sets.

Thanks to the reformulation (
5.5
), we obtain the equivalent lower-level objective functions
L
for circular and star constraints given by

\displaystyle\text{Circular:}\quad L(\theta)

\displaystyle:=\left(\theta_{1}^{2}+\theta_{2}^{2}-1\right)^{2},

(5.9a)

\displaystyle\text{Star:}\quad L(\theta)

\displaystyle:=\left(\theta_{1}^{2}+\theta_{2}^{2}-(1+0.5\sin(5\text{atan2}(\theta_{2},\theta_{1})))^{2}\right)^{2},

(5.9b)

respectively.

Baselines & Implementations.

We compare our CB
2
O (Algorithm
1
) with four baseline methods: Penalized CBO
[
36
]
, Adaptive Penalized CBO
[
22
]
, CBO with Gradient Force (GF)
[
35
]
and Projected CBO
[
47
]
.
The shared hyperparameters across all five algorithms are,

\displaystyle\lambda=1,\qquad\sigma=1,\qquad\alpha=30,\qquad\Delta t=0.01,\qquad T=300.

The stopping criteria are set to
\varepsilon_{\text{stop}}=0
for the circular constraint and
\varepsilon_{\text{stop}}=10^{-3}
for the star constraint.
Let us now give the details about the baselines and their specific hyperparameters.

•

Penalized CBO.
The Penalized CBO method
[
36
]
transforms the constrained optimization problem into an unconstrained problem by linearly combining the upper- and lower-level objective functions
G
and
L
with a penalty parameter
\chi
, and then applies the standard CBO method
[
82
]
on the resulting unconstrained problem.
The reformulated objective is
\chi L+G
, where
\chi
is a key hyperparameter.
We set
\chi=100
for both the circular and the star constraint problems.

•

Adaptive Penalized CBO.
The Adaptive Penalized CBO method
[
22
]
also applies the standard CBO to the unconstrained objective
\chi_{k}L+G
, yet dynamically adjusts
\chi_{k}
at each iteration
k
based on the constraint violations of the current particles.
If the violation is below the tolerance threshold
1/\sqrt{\zeta_{k}}
, the tolerance decreases according to
\zeta_{k+1}=\eta_{\zeta}\zeta_{k}
for some
\eta_{\zeta}>1
. Otherwise,
\chi_{k}
increases by a factor
\eta_{\chi}>1
, i.e.,
\chi_{k+1}=\eta_{\chi}\chi_{k}
.
Please find the choices of hyperparameters in Appendix
B
.

•

CBO with GF.
CBO with gradient forcing solves the constrained optimization problem by adding a gradient force term
-\chi\nabla L
to the standard CBO dynamics with
G
as objective function.
A large
\chi
encourages particles to rapidly concentrate around the constraint set.
We set
\chi=100
for both the circular and the star constraint problems.

•

Projected CBO.
The Projected CBO methods modifies the standard CBO method
[
82
]
by projecting the drift and diffusion terms onto the tangent space of the constraint set and adding an extra term to ensure the dynamics remains on the constraint set despite the Brownian motion.
This method does not introduce additional hyperparameters.

•

CB
2
O.
In addition to shared hyperparameters, CB
2
O introduces a key hyperparameter
\beta
.
For this study,
\beta
is set to be constant.
Details of the chosen values are provided in Appendix
B
.

Remark 5.2
.

In practical implementations of the CB
2
O algorithm, the quantile parameter
\beta
cannot be chosen too small,
since the number of particles
N
is finite.
Specifically, if
\lceil\beta N\rceil\leq 1
, only one particle is selected at each time step to when computing the consensus point according to (
5.2
).
In this case, the consensus point is simply at every time step the particle with the smallest value of the lower-level objective function
L
.
Consequently, CB
2
O behaves in such case similarly to the standard CBO method
[
82
]
with a large value of the hyperparameter
\alpha
, when
L
is used as the objective function, neglecting completely the upper-level objective function
G
.
This hinders the algorithm’s ability to converge to the target minimizer
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
.
Therefore, in practical implementations of the CB
2
O algorithm, we impose a lower bound on the hyperparameter
\beta
, denoted as
\beta_{\text{min}}
, which is the value such that
\lceil\beta N\rceil=2
.

Performance Metrics.

We evaluate the algorithm’s performance using the averaged
\ell^{2}
-distance between the optimizer each algorithm finds and the target global minimizer
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
over
100
simulations, referred to as
precision
.
Comparisons are conducted in two scenarios, where
(i) all algorithms use the same number of particles (
N=100
), and where (ii) all algorithms run for approximately the same total time.
For each algorithm, this requires to determine the maximum number of particles it can handle within a fixed runtime.
Runtime is measured in seconds over
100
simulations using MATLAB.

5.2.2
Experimental Results

We now report on the results of our numerical experiments.

•

Circular constraint.

We first compare the algorithms using the same number of particles, i.e., setting (i).
As shown in Table
1
and Figure
7(a)
, both CBO with Gradient Force and Projected CBO achieve the best precision but require relatively long running times.
In contrast, the CB
2
O algorithm demonstrates a good balance, maintaining strong performance while using significantly less computational time.
The reduced computational cost of CB
2
O is due to its use of only
\lceil\beta N\rceil
particles to compute the consensus point at each iteration, which significantly reduces complexity, particularly for large particle counts.
This efficiency enables CB
2
O algorithm to utilize a greater number of particles under the same computational cost.

To investigate further, we compare the algorithms under equivalent computational costs (running times), i.e., setting (ii).
From Table
2
and Figure
7(b)
,
we observe that while CBO with Gradient Force and Projected CBO exhibit faster initial convergence, CB
2
O ultimately achieves the best precision among all methods given the same computational budget.

Notably, although Adaptive Penalized CBO shows a convergence pattern similar to CB
2
O,
the later consistently outperforms it in both precision and computational efficiency.
Furthermore, Adaptive Penalized CBO is sensitive to its specific hyperparameters, such as
\eta_{\zeta}
and
\zeta_{\chi}
, whereas CB
2
O remains stable across a broader range of values of the hyperparameter
\beta
.
For a detailed discussion, please see Section
5.2.3
.

•

Star constraint.
Similar to the circular constraint case, we compare the performance of different algorithms using the same number of particles (setting (i)) and under approximately the same running time (setting (ii)).
Due to the complexity of the star constraint, applying projection is not straightforward, so we exclude Projected CBO from this comparison.
From Table
3
and Figure
8(a)
, we observe that even using the same number of particles, CB
2
O already outperforms most of the baselines, and has comparable performances as Penalized CBO meanwhile having less computational cost.
Furthermore, when CB
2
O is allowed to use more particles but maintaining the same computational cost as others, as shown in Table
4
and Figure
8(b)
, it consistently demonstrates superior performance in both precision and efficiency.
Notably, CBO with Gradient Force requires significantly longer running times to achieve reasonable precision and behaves highly unstable compared to CB
2
O.
This is primarily due to the computationally expensive matrix inversion step in the CBO with Gradient Force algorithm and the greatly nonconvex nature of the star constraint.
These results underscore the advantages of CB
2
O in handling nonconvex constraint sets effectively.

Remark 5.3
.

From the two experiments above, we observe that CB
2
O is computationally efficient, as it computes the consensus point at each iteration using only a subset of particles.
However, this approach also means that the unselected particles is not fully utilized, which generally necessitates using a larger total number of particles to achieve good performance.
This observation motivates further exploration into strategies for accelerating CB
2
O by better leveraging the unselected particles, which we leave for future work.

5.2.3
The Influence of Hyperparameters

In this section, we investigate more deeply the influence of the hyperparameters
N
,
\beta
,
\alpha
, and
\varepsilon_{\text{stop}}
on the performance of the CB
2
O algorithm in terms of both precision and running time through an ablation study, see Figure
9
.
Specifically, in the experiments below, we vary one or two hyperparameters at a time while keeping all others fixed.
Detailed information on the hyperparameter settings are provided in Appendix
B
.

Ablation Study: Influence of the Hyperparameters on Precision.

•

Impact of the particle number
N
.

In Figure
9(a)
, we observe that increasing the number of particles
N
from
10^{2}
to
10^{3}
improves the algorithm’s performance.
However, the precision plateaus as
N
increases further from
10^{3}
to
10^{5}
.
This phenomenon can be understood through theoretical intuition by considering a hypothetical “mean-field” version of the CB
2
O algorithm with the same hyperparameters as in Figure
9(a)
but with infinitely many particles.
This mean-field algorithm achieves a fixed precision determined by the choice of hyperparameters.
For the finite particle CB
2
O algorithm, its precision can be decomposed into two parts. First, the mean-field approximation error (how closely the finite particle algorithm approximates the mean-field algorithm) and second the intrinsic precision of the mean-field algorithm.
When
N
is small, the finite particle algorithm’s performance is limited by the mean-field approximation error.
As
N
increases, the algorithm’s precision approaches the one of the mean-field algorithm.
While our paper does not formally justify the mean-field approximation property, these observations align with theoretical results in the CBO literature
[
65
,
51
,
57
]
.

•

Impact of the quantile parameter
\beta
.

From Figure
9(b)
, we make the following three observations.
First, for a fixed number of particles
N=1000
and other hyperparameters held constant, the optimal precision occurs for
\beta\in[0.01,0.05]
.
Furthermore, selecting
\beta
within the range
[0.002,0.1]
results in relatively good algorithm performance, indicating that the CB
2
O algorithm is relatively insensitive to the choices of
\beta
.
Second, choosing
\beta
too large causes the CB
2
O algorithm to fail in finding the target global minimizer
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
.
This occurs because, with large
\beta
, the quantile set
Q^{L}_{\beta}[\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}]
may include the regions with high loss in terms of
L
but low values of
G
, therefore causing the algorithm to move towards the global minimizer of
G
but not the desired minimizer
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
.
This behavior is consistent with the theoretical results discussed in Remark
2.9
.
Third, and conversely, choosing
\beta
being too small degrades the algorithm performance, as discussed in Remark
5.2
.
However, for a fixed number of particles
N
, selecting
\beta
such that
\lceil\beta N\rceil=2
already results in good performance.
These observations demonstrate that the choice of the quantile parameter

\beta
in CB
2
O is not restrictive, offering flexibility without compromising performance.

We repeat the experiments for varying values of
\beta
for different numbers of particles, namely
N=500
and
N=2000
, respectively.
The three patterns discussed earlier can be observed in analogous form in Figures
10(a)
and
10(b)
.

•

Joint impact of
N
and
\beta
.
Figure
9(c)
shows that as the number of particles
N
increases and the quantile parameter
\beta
decreases while keeping
\beta N=50
constant, the precision of CB
2
O algorithm improves consistently.
Combined with the observation from Figure
9(a)
that the precision plateaus when N exceeds
10^{3}
for a fixed
\beta
, it suggests that smaller
\beta
improves the algorithm performance in the “mean-field” regime.
These findings align with the theoretical perspective discussed in Remark
2.9
.

•

Impact of the temperature parameter
\alpha
.
As shown in Figure
9(d)
, the algorithm’s precision consistently improves with increasing
\alpha
.
This aligns with the theoretical perspective discussed in Remark
2.10
, as well as other CBO literature
[
31
,
50
]
.

•

Impact of the stopping criterion
\varepsilon_{\text{stop}}
.

The choice of stopping criterion
\varepsilon_{\text{stop}}
has a significant impact on the algorithm’s precision, as illustrated in Figure
9(e)
.
A smaller
\varepsilon_{\text{stop}}
requires the algorithm to run for more iterations before stopping, resulting in improved performance.
While selecting a small
\varepsilon_{\text{stop}}
is ideal for achieving high precision, this hyperparameter can be adjusted to balance precision and running time when the algorithm is operating under computational cost limitations.
Further discussions on the influence of
\varepsilon_{\text{stop}}
on the running time are provided below.

(a)

Number of particles
N

(b)

Quantile parameter
\beta
(
N=1000
)

(c)

(N,\beta)
-pairs with fixed
\beta N

(d)

Temperature parameter
\alpha

(e)

Stopping criteria
\varepsilon_{\text{stop}}

-distance between the output of the algorithm and the target global minimizer
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
averaged over

simulations.

(

)

simulations.

Influence of the Hyperparameters on the Running Time.

Figures
11(a)
,
11(b)
and
11(c)
reveal that the running time of the CB
2
O algorithm is predominately influenced by the total number of particles
N
, and the quantile
\beta
affects the constant factor in the running time scaling.
Specifically,
\beta
determines the proportion of particles used in each iteration, impacting the computational overhead but not the overall scaling trend.
Figures
11(d)
and
11(e)
demonstrate that the stopping criterion
\varepsilon_{\text{stop}}
significantly affects the running time, as stricter criteria require more iterations for convergence.
In contrast, the temperature parameter
\alpha
has minimal impact on the overall running time.

Number of particles
N

Quantile parameter
\beta

(N,\beta)
-pairs with fixed
\beta N

Temperature parameter
\alpha

Stopping criteria
\varepsilon_{\text{stop}}

:
The influence of different hyperparameters on the running time of the CB
2
O algorithm.
The vertical axis in each plot corresponds to the algorithm’s total running time (seconds) averaged over
100
simulations.

In summary, we can draw the following three general conclusions.
First, the quantile parameter
\beta
plays a pivotal role in determining the performance of the CB
2
O method for a fixed number of particles
N
.
The results demonstrate that the algorithm is relatively stable and robust across a reasonably large range of
\beta
values for every fixed number of particles
N
.
Second, the number of particles
N
and the stopping criterion
\varepsilon_{\text{stop}}
can be adjusted to achieve a balance between the algorithm’s performance and computational efficiency, offering flexibility to run the algorithm under different computational constraints.
And, third, in this two-dimensional example, the temperature parameter
\alpha
can be set rather large provided that the number of particles
N
is not too small. However, setting
\alpha=\infty
is generally not ideal in practice, as elaborated on in Remark
1.2
.

5.3
Sparse Representation Learning

In this section, we consider the task of sparse representation learning using neural networks
[
59
]
.
Specifically, we want to learn sparse feature representations on a supervised dataset
\mathcal{D}
that contains feature-label pairs
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
, while maintaining high prediction accuracy.
Training a neural network that admits a sparse representation as well as high prediction accuracy for this task
can be formulated as a bi-level optimization problem of form (
1.1
).
Encoding the prediction accuracy in the lower-level objective function
L

and promoting sparsity of the feature representations through the upper-level function
G
,
we can formalize the sparse representation learning task as

\min_{\theta^{*}\in\Theta}G(\theta^{*}):=\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}}\left[\left\|{h_{\theta^{*}}(x)}\right\|_{p}^{p}\right]\quad\text{s.t.{}}\quad\theta^{*}\in\Theta:=\operatorname*{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}L(\theta):=\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}}\left[\ell\left(\phi_{\theta}(h_{\theta}(x)),y\right)\right].

(5.10)

Here,
h_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
denotes the feature representation map, and
\phi_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
the prediction head.
For instance, in a neural network,
h_{\theta}
comprises all layers except the final one, while
\phi_{\theta}
represents the last layer.
Within the lower-level objective function
L
,
the function
\ell:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
represents the loss function, such as the cross-entropy loss used later on during the experiment, which penalizes deviations between the model’s predictions
\phi_{\theta}(h_{\theta}(x))
and the true labels
y
.
The parameter
p\in[0,1]
in the upper-level objective function
G
is chosen to promote a sparse representation.
In our experiment, we set
p=0.5
to enforce nonconvex regularization.

Remark 5.4
.

Disclaimer: It is important to note that our primary goal is not to challenge state-of-the-art methods for this task, such as gradient-based approaches, which may be generically better suited for training neural networks.
Rather, we consider the task of sparse representation learning as a well-understood high-dimensional benchmark bi-level optimization problem to firstly demonstrate the effectiveness of a mathematically rigorously analyzable method such as CB
2
O (even without gradient term) and to secondly numerically analyze the influence of the hyperparameters on the behavior of CB
2
O in practical machine learning applications.
Following Remark
1.3
, it is straightforward to integrate and exploit gradient information in CB
2
O, which will certainly improve the performance as observed for various other tasks
[
83
]
.

5.3.1
Experimental Setup

Let us now describe in detail the experimental setup for the sparse representation learning task.

Dataset & Model Setup.

We use a subset of the MNIST dataset
[
73
]
, consisting of
10,000
training and
10,000
test images.
Following the setup in
[
50
,
83
]
, we use a neural network (NN) with two convolutional layers, two max-pooling layers, and one dense layer as our base model.
A batch normalization step is applied after each ReLU activation.
We denote by
h_{\theta}
all layers up to the final dense layer,
and by
\phi_{\theta}
the final dense layer.

Baselines & Implementations.

Since the simple bi-level optimization problem can be regarded as a constrained optimization problem, where the constraint set is defined as the global minimizers
\Theta
of the lower-level objective function
L
,
some CBO-type algorithms designed for constrained problems
[
36
]
are applicable.
However, several other constrained CBO algorithms
[
2
,
47
,
48
,
49
,
22
,
34
]
, which depend heavily on the structure (e.g., convexity) of the constraint set, are not suitable for our problem.
This is because the constraint set
\Theta
is not explicitly available (see discussions in Remark
5.5
).

We compare our CB
2
O algorithm as described in Algorithm
1
with two baseline algorithms: Standard CBO
[
34
,
50
]
and Penalized CBO
[
36
]
.
The general setup for all three methods follows the setting used in
[
50
]
.
In particular, we use mini-batches when evaluating both the upper- and lower-level objective functions
G
(if necessary) and
L
, with a mini-batch size of
n_{\text{batch}}=60
.
At each time step one mini-batch of training data is processed.
One epoch comprises as many time step as are necessary to loop once through all training samples, i.e., we have
\lceil 10000/60\rceil
time steps per epoch.
Cooling strategies are applied to the parameters
\alpha
and
\sigma
.
Specifically,
\alpha
is multiplied by
2
after each epoch, and the diffusion parameter
\sigma
obeys
\sigma_{\text{epoch}}=\sigma_{0}/\log_{2}(\text{epoch}+2)
.
For the remaining parameters, we choose
\lambda=1
,
\sigma_{0}=\sqrt{0.4}
,
\alpha_{\text{init}}=50
, and a step size of
\Delta t=0.1
.
The default number of particles is set to
N=100
if there is no further specification.
We initialize all particles (recall that each particle contains the parameters
\theta
of one NN) from
\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d\times d})
,
all of which are involved in computing the consensus point and updated at every time step (referred to as the “full update” in
[
34
,
50
]
).
Additionally, we employ the re-initialization ad-hoc method introduced in
[
34
, Remark 2.4]
, where we perturb the positions of all particles by independent Gaussian noise with variance
\sigma^{2}
if the consensus point remains unchanged for
30
consecutive time steps.

Below, we provide details on the baseline methods.

•

Standard CBO.
The standard CBO algorithm
[
34
,
50
]
was implemented using exclusively the lower-level objective function
L
as the objective.
This baseline highlights that minimizing the classification loss
L
alone does not automatically result in a neural network with sparse feature representations,
motivating and necessitating the inclusion of the upper-level objective function
G
.
It further provides a lower bound on the prediction accuracy for CBO-type methods.

•

Penalized CBO.
The same as the one described in Section
5.2.1
, i.e.
applying the Standard CBO
[
82
,
51
]
to the objective
\chi L+G
, where
\chi
is a key hyperparameter to tune.

•

CB
2
O.
In addition to following the standard CBO parameter setup, we consider two types of schedulers for quantile parameter
\beta
. (i) constant
\beta
, and
(ii) decaying
\beta
.
In particular, we apply a cooling strategy, where
\beta
follows the schedule
\beta_{\text{epoch}}=\beta_{0}\kappa^{\text{epoch}}
with a decay factor
\kappa\in(0,1)
until reaching prefixed minimum value
\beta_{\text{min}}
.

Performance Metrics.
We compare the performances of the different algorithms using three metrics:

training loss
(value of the lower-level objective function
L
evaluated on the training set),

norm of feature representation
(value of upper-level objective function
G
evaluated on the training set),
and
test accuracy
(the model’s prediction accuracy on the test set).
These three metrics are assessed based on the location of the consensus point (representing the parameters of an NN) generated by each algorithm.
Experiments are conducted with
5
different random seeds for all algorithms, and we report the averages of the results.

Remark 5.5
.

Certain constrained CBO algorithms like Penalized CBO with adapted Lagrange multiplier
[
22
]
,
Projected CBO
[
47
,
48
,
49
]
and CBO with gradient force
[
34
]
are not implemented here, as they may be unsuitable for the high-dimensional simple bi-level optimization problem at hand.
For instance, in
[
22
]
, the adapted Lagrange multiplier is updated based on constraint violations of the particles, which
is inapplicable since the constrained set
\Theta
is unknown.
Similarly, the projection step during the Projected CBO algorithms
[
2
,
47
,
48
,
49
]
is not feasible without explicit knowledge of the set
\Theta
.
Furthermore, CBO with gradient force
[
34
]
requires computationally expensive matrix inversions, making it prohibitive in high-dimensional problems.

5.3.2
Experimental Results

Let us now report the results of our numerical experiments.

•

CB
2
O with constant hyperparameter
\beta
.

In Figure
12
, we compare the performance of the standard CBO algorithm with CB
2
O using different constant values of the hyperparameter
\beta
.
Both algorithms employ
N=100
particles.
We can make the following observations:

(i) The standard CBO method minimizes exclusively the lower-level objective function
L
, leading to a strong performance in terms of training loss and test accuracy.
However, the norm of the feature representation generated by its learned neural network is large,
illustrating that minimizing
L
alone does not result in a network with sparse feature representations, emphasizing the need for a sparsity-promoting regularization function
G
.

(ii) CB
2
O with
\beta=0.01
behaves almost identical to the standard CBO algorithm in all three performance metrics, which aligns with our discussion in Remark
5.2
.

(iii) As the quantile parameter
\beta
increases, the learned NNs exhibit smaller norms of the feature representations, but the model’s performance w.r.t. training loss and test accuracy gradually declines.
This indicates a trade-off between the model’s prediction performance and the sparsity of feature representation.
CB
2
O with
\beta=0.02
achieves the best balance between these two factors, producing a neural network with a relatively small norm of the feature representation while maintaining nearly the same prediction performance as the model obtained by standard CBO.
This observation aligns with our theoretical justification of the choice of the quantile parameter
\beta
in Remark
2.9
, where we show that
\beta
must be sufficiently small to ensure a good performance of the CB
2
O algorithm.

Let us further compare in Figure
13
the performance of CB
2
O across various combinations of the particle numbers
N
and the quantile parameter
\beta
.
Notably, for a fixed number of particles
N
, choosing
\beta
such that
\lceil\beta N\rceil=2
consistently yields the best trade-off between the model prediction accuracy and the sparsity of the feature representations.
This highlights the simplicity of tuning the quantile parameter
\beta
for this task.
Moreover, when
\lceil\beta N\rceil=2
, we observe, that using
N=100
particles yields roughly the same performance as using
N=200
particles, while
N=50
particles result in a slightly lower prediction accuracy.

Figure 12
:
Comparison between standard CBO and CB
2
O for different constant values of the quantile hyperparameter
\beta
.
All algorithms employ
N=100
particles.
The sub-figures from left to right are comparing the training loss
L
, the norm of the feature representation
G
, and the test accuracy against training epochs, respectively.

Figure 13
:
Comparison of CB
2
O for different number
N
of particles and quantile hyperparameter
\beta
.
In the top row, we compare CB
2
O using
N=200
particles and different constant values of the parameter
\beta
with CB
2
O using
N=100
particles and
\beta=0.02
.
In the bottom row, we compare CB
2
O using
N=50
particles and different constant values of the parameter
\beta
with CB
2
O using
N=100
particles and
\beta=0.02
.
The sub-figures from left to right are comparing the training loss
L
, the norm of the feature representation
G
, and the test accuracy against training epochs, respectively.

•

Comparisons between CB
2
O and Penalized CBO.

We compare our CB
2
O algorithm with Penalized CBO for different Lagrange multipliers
\chi
, and depict the results in Figure
14
.
We observe that Penalized CBO with
\chi=5\times 10^{4}
provides the best trade-off between model performance and the sparsity of the feature representations among all different choices of Lagrange multipliers.
Moreover, it has a similar convergence behavior as CB
2
O with constant value
\beta=0.02
in terms of both upper- and lower-objectives
G
and
L
.
However, it is generally harder to tune the hyperparameter
\chi
since it requires taking into account the scaling between the objective functions
G
and
L
.
In particular,
Penalized CBO with
\chi
being too large will only minimize the lower-level objective
L
, and therefore behaves the same as standard CBO minimizing
L
.
On the other hand, Penalized CBO with
\chi
being too small will mainly minimize the objective function
G
, which will also fail the goal of finding
\theta_{\mathrm{good}}^{*}
.
This phenomenon can be observed in Figure
14
.
Specifically, Penalized CBO with
\chi=10^{6},10^{7}
produces good performance on the prediction accuracy, while not enforcing the sparsity of the feature representations.
On the other extreme, smaller
\chi=10^{3},10^{4}
show good convergence in terms of the objective function
G
, but the model’s prediction performance declines.
In comparison, tuning the hyperparameter
\beta
in CB
2
O is much simpler.
For a fixed number
N
of particles, choosing
\beta
such that
\lceil\beta N\rceil=2
can already delivers good performance.
This observation aligns with similar findings in the constrained optimization task, as discussed in Section
5.2.3
.

Figure 14
:
Comparison between CB
2
O with
\beta=0.02
and Penalized CBO with different Lagrange multipliers
\chi
.
The sub-figures from left to right are comparing the training loss
L
, the norm of the feature representation
G
, and the test accuracy against training epochs, respectively.

•

CB
2
O with decaying hyperparameter
\beta
.

Figure
12
suggests that while choosing
\beta=0.02
gives the best overall performance by balancing the prediction accuracy and sparsity of the feature representations, a small value of the quantile hyperparameter
\beta
also leads to a relatively slow decay of the norm of the feature representations compared to larger choices of
\beta
.
This motivates us to consider a decaying scheduler for
\beta
such that it induces faster convergence on the function
G
, while maintaining good performance in terms of the objective function
L
.
In particular, we consider the scheduler
\beta_{\text{epoch}}=\min\,\{\beta_{0}\kappa^{\text{epoch}},\beta_{\text{min}}\}
, i.e.,
\beta
with initialized value
\beta_{0}
decaying every epoch with decay factor
\kappa
until it reaches a prefixed lower threshold
\beta_{\text{min}}
.
In Figure
15
, we consider different pairs of initialized hyperparameter value
\beta_{0}
and decay factor
\kappa
with minimum value
\beta_{\text{min}}=0.02
.
We observe that larger
\beta_{0}
enjoys faster convergence in terms of the norm of the feature representations (objective function
G
) and large decay factors
\kappa
ensure maintaining the overall model’s performance (objective function
L
).

Figure 15
:
Comparison between standard CBO and CB
2
O for decaying
\beta
with different decay factors.
The sub-figures from left to right are comparing the training loss
L
, the norm of the feature representation
G
, and the test accuracy against training epochs, respectively.

6
Conclusions

In this paper,
we propose consensus-based bi-level optimization,
abbreviated as CB
2
O,
a multi-particle metaheuristic derivative-free optimization method designed to solve nonconvex bi-level optimization problems of the form (
1.1
),
which are also known as simple bi-level optimization problems.
To ensure that the bi-level optimization problem is solved in an intrinsic manner, our method leverages within the computation of the consensus point a carefully designed particle selection principle implemented through a suitable choice of a quantile on the level of the lower-level objective, together with a Laplace principle-type approximation w.r.t. the upper-level objective function.

On the theoretical side,
we give an existence proof of solutions to the mean-field dynamics, which is a nonlinear nonlocal Fokker-Planck equation, and establish its global convergence to the global target minimizer of the bi-level optimization problem in mean-field law.
To achieve the former, we first develop a new stability estimate for our consensus point w.r.t. a combination of Wasserstein and
L^{2}
perturbations,
and consecutively resort to PDE considerations extending the classical Picard iteration to construct a solution.
For the latter, we develop a novel quantitative large deviation result with an integrated selection mechanism in terms of the quantile function.
We believe that both those developments are of independent interest and may become fundamental analytical tools for evolutionary algorithms and metaheuristics in general.

On the experimental side,
we demonstrate the effectiveness of the CB
2
O algorithm on both low-dimensional constrained global optimization problems and high-dimensional machine learning applications.

There are several theoretical considerations of the proposed CB
2
O system that have not been explored in this paper and are left for future research. This includes the well-posedness of the finite particle CB
2
O system (
1.2
), the uniqueness of solutions to the mean-field system (
1.12
) and the mean-field approximation of the finite particle system to the mean-field system.
Since the consensus point
m^{G,L}_{\alpha,\beta}
, as defined in (
2.1
), is not stable under the Wasserstein perturbations, the tools developed in
[
31
,
51
,
65
,
57
,
64
]
to prove the above-mentioned properties for the standard CBO system
[
82
]
do not directly apply to the CB
2
O method.
This necessitates the development of more general and less rigid proof strategies, which we leave for future work.
However, we believe that the tools developed in this paper are of foundational nature to those endeavors.

Learning, where most of this work was done.

KR moreover acknowledges the financial support from the University of Oxford.
